### [Introduction to matrix-product states and tensor networks](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24803v1)

**Authors:** Grégoire Misguich  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24803v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `methods for driven-dissipative` **5/5** · 🔥 `analog quantum simulation` **4/5** · 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5** · 🔥 `non-equilibrium dynamics` **4/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `Tavis-Cummings & cavity-many-emitter` **1/5** · `correlated cavity matter` **1/5** · `exotic spin models in light-matter` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

<img src="2606.24803_figures/2606.24803_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Examples of TNs: a) MPS for a chain of length 8, b) binary tree TN for a system of 8 sites, c) projected entangled pair state for a system with 4 × 4 sites in a square lattice geometry, d) many- body wave function represented as a single N-leg tensor and e) multi-scale entanglement renormalization ansatz (MERA) TN. Each box (or circle) represents a tensor, and each leg represents an index of the tensor. In this representation the open legs (labeled s1, . . . , sN) that are not connected to any other tensor represent the physical indices. <details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2606.24803_figures/2606.24803_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 5: Construction of an MPS from a vector representation by successive QR decompositions. At each step, the Q matrix gives an MPS tensor A[i], while the R matrix is reshaped and factorized again at the next site. Such a construction leads to an MPS in a left-canonical gauge (see Fig. 8).</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2606.24803_figures/2606.24803_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 6: Partial contractions of a left-canonical MPS and a right-canonical MPS. For each value of the open virtual index αn, the left contraction defines a state |αn⟩L in the Hilbert space of the first n sites, while the right contraction defines a state |αn⟩R in the Hilbert space of sites n + 1, . . . , N. The corresponding canonical conditions imply that these states form orthonormal families. See also Fig. 8.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This comprehensive set of notes introduces tensor networks, focusing heavily on Matrix-Product States (MPS), as a computational tool for quantum many-body physics. It details the mathematical machinery required—including entanglement measures and canonical forms—to simulate ground states, time evolution, and open system dynamics efficiently. The work is essential reading for anyone using numerical methods to study strongly correlated or dissipative quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it provides a complete methodological toolkit for simulating dynamics in complex, interacting quantum systems, directly applicable to modeling decoherence or non-equilibrium steady states in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a comprehensive introduction to tensor-network (TN) methods, particularly Matrix-Product States (MPS), for efficiently simulating quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** TNs offer a powerful framework that encodes the entanglement structure of a state, allowing polynomial scaling simulations for states obeying an area law, which is crucial for 1D and low-dimensional quantum physics.

**Method.** The paper develops the mathematical language of TNs (graphical notation, bond dimensions) and details core algorithms like DMRG, time evolution methods (tDMRG/TEBD), and generalizations to PEPS for higher dimensions.

**Model / system.** The notes cover a wide range of systems, including 1D quantum spin chains, open quantum systems governed by Lindblad dynamics, classical statistical mechanics models, and general quantum circuits.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Entropy ($S_{vN}$), Bond Dimension ($\chi$), Magnetization $\langle\sigma^z_{N/2}angle$, and the steady state density matrix $ho_{ss}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$) controls accuracy vs. cost; the area law dictates efficient representability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the physical state or dynamics can be accurately captured by a low-rank tensor decomposition (i.e., obeying an area law).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate MPS structures, PEPS generalizations to 2D lattices, and graphical representations of elementary tensor operations like contractions and SVD.

**Paper structure.** The structure progresses logically from defining the basic TN language and MPS for 1D systems, through core algorithms (DMRG/time evolution), generalizing to higher dimensions (PEPS), and finally applying the formalism to open quantum systems and mixed states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

These notes provide an introduction to tensor-network methods in quantum many-body physics, with an emphasis on matrix-product states (MPS). They develop the basic tensor-network language, including graphical notation, virtual indices, bond dimensions, gauge freedom, canonical forms, QR and singular-value decompositions, and the role of entanglement in controlling the efficiency of the representation. The main MPS algorithms are then introduced, including contractions, correlation functions, matrix-product operators, DMRG, and time-evolution methods. The notes also briefly discuss projected entangled-pair states (PEPS) as a higher-dimensional generalization of MPS, together with the basic ideas behind approximate PEPS contraction. Finally, tensor-network representations of mixed states, quantum channels, and Lindblad dynamics are presented, with applications to thermal states and open quantum systems. The presentation is accompanied by short Julia code examples based on ITensor, ITensorMPS, and TensorMixedStates. These notes were written for the 9th Les Houches Summer School on Computational Physics: Open Quantum Systems, held in June 2026.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically explores the rich non-equilibrium dynamics of Bose condensates and quantum droplets coupled to a dissipative optical cavity. By analyzing collective excitations and stability, the authors characterize a density ordering transition marked by polariton softening. Crucially, they show that the system can exhibit long-lived metastable states, such as double-droplets, even when those states are predicted to be unstable.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it merges concepts from cavity QED (dissipation, polaritons) with quantum gas physics (BEC, droplets), providing a theoretical framework to model complex, driven non-equilibrium phenomena accessible in modern experimental setups.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates non-equilibrium dynamics, collective excitations, and phase transitions in a system combining quantum droplets/condensates with a dissipative cavity mode.

**Main result.** The research identifies a polariton-like gapped excitation whose frequency softens near the density ordering transition, and demonstrates that metastable states (like double-droplets) can persist dynamically even when theoretically unstable.

**Method.** A classical-field approach is used to determine stationary states and linearize equations of motion around these states to analyze collective excitations and stability. Time evolution simulations are also employed for quench dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system models a dilute Bose gas (condensate) and self-bound quantum droplets confined in a 1D optical lattice, coupled to a lossy cavity mode via mean-field interactions.

**Key observables.** Collective excitation frequencies ($\omega_{p\pm}$), relaxation time divergence near critical points, staggered density imbalance ($I$), and the persistence of non-equilibrium states (kinks, double-droplets).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The atom-cavity coupling strength ($\lambda$) relative to a critical value ($\lambda_c$ or $\lambda^*$), lattice parameters ($J$, $V$), and interaction strengths ($\zeta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the classical-field approximation and mean-field theory, which is assumed valid in 1D under specific parameter regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase transition (staggered imbalance vs. coupling), excitation spectra showing polariton branches ($\omega_\pm(k)$), and real-time dynamics demonstrating density wave oscillations and the persistence of odd-parity metastable states after a quench.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the mean-field model, then analyzing steady states and collective excitations (Bogoliubov modes vs. polaritons). It details the phase transition driven by coupling strength ($\lambda$), followed by investigating non-equilibrium dynamics through time evolution simulations of metastable configurations like kinks and double-droplets.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by recent experiments on light-matter interacting systems, we investigate a dilute Bose gas and self-bound quantum droplets in a one-dimensional optical lattice coupled to a lossy cavity mode. Using a classical-field approach, we determine the stationary states and collective excitations of this non-equilibrium system. Apart from the usual Bogoliubov modes, we identify a polariton-like gapped excitation, the frequency of which softens as a precursor of the density ordering transition. Moreover, its relaxation time diverges as the critical point is approached, signaling the non-equilibrium nature of this transition. Dynamically, this polariton-like mode can be probed by inducing cavity field fluctuations, which in turn generates spatio-temporal oscillations of both the condensate and droplet states. In the droplet regime, we also analyze the bound modes which bear the characteristics of such non-equilibrium self-bound state. In addition, we uncover solitonic non-equilibrium states, including condensate with kink-like configuration and double-droplet state, and investigate their robustness following a sudden quench. Remarkably, although these states become unstable beyond a critical coupling, they continue to manifest in the dynamics, akin to the scarring phenomena. Our results demonstrate that dissipative cavity coupling provides a versatile route for exploring rich non-equilibrium dynamics of condensates and quantum droplets within experimentally accessible settings.

</details>

**Authors:** H. Panagopoulos, E. Vicari  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24395v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `non-equilibrium universality` **5/5** · 🔥 `non-equilibrium dynamics` **4/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work analyzes how the particle density evolves in quantum fermionic wires immediately after a sudden change (quench) of the chemical potential near a quantum critical point. The authors analytically derive that this out-of-equilibrium evolution follows a universal scaling law, providing strong theoretical evidence for dynamic scaling behavior previously suggested by numerical simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework for identifying universal dynamic scaling laws in quantum many-body systems after non-equilibrium perturbations, which is crucial for understanding critical dynamics beyond equilibrium measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the out-of-equilibrium scaling behavior of particle density in quantum fermionic wires following an instantaneous quench of the chemical potential near a quantum critical point.

**Main result.** The difference between the post-quench particle density and its critical value exhibits universal out-of-equilibrium dynamic scaling, characterized by a specific singular function $\Gamma(	heta)$.

**Method.** Analytical derivations are used to establish the scaling ansatz, comparing results with numerical finite-size scaling analyses.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using quantum fermionic Kitaev wires (equivalent to XY spin chains) near their quantum critical point. The Hamiltonian involves tunable parameters like chemical potential ($\mu$) and coupling constant ($\gamma$).

**Key observables.** Particle density $\langle \hat{n} angle$ and the difference between the post-quench particle density and its critical equilibrium value.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time ($t$), initial length scale ($\xi$), deviation from criticality ($w$), and dynamic scaling variable $	heta \sim t/\xi^z$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes starting from the ground state near the QCP and relies on approximations (e.g., $\sin k \approx k$) to find asymptotic limits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the derived scaling function $\Gamma(	heta)$ with numerical results obtained by taking the out-of-equilibrium limit; Figure 2 shows a generalized scaling function $\Lambda(	heta, u)$ for different initial conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical context (Kitaev wires at QCP), derives the general scaling ansatz for the particle density difference, and then calculates the asymptotic form of the universal scaling function $\Gamma(	heta)$ by taking appropriate limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the out-of-equilibrium scaling behavior of the particle density in quantum fermionic Kitaev wires, after instantaneous quantum quenches (QQs) of the chemical potential within their quantum critical region. The critical scaling of the ground-state particle density is known to be subleading at its Ising-like quantum transition, hidden by regular and logarithmic terms arising from peculiar mixings with the identity operator. This situation changes along the out-of-equilibrium dynamics arising from QQs of the chemical potential to the critical point, starting from the ground state for Hamiltonian parameters within the critical region. We analytically show that the difference between the post-QQ particle density and its critical value develops an out-of-equilibrium scaling behavior, in terms of the dynamic scaling variable $θ\sim t/ξ^z$ (where $t>0$ is the post-QQ time, $ξ$ is the length scale of the initial state, and $z$ is the dynamic critical exponent) associated with the post-QQ time evolution. The scaling function turns out to have a peculiar singular behavior in the $θ\to 0$ limit, apparently related to the anomalous equilibrium scaling behavior of the particle density at the starting point of the QQ protocol. This provides analytical evidence of earlier conjectures on the general emergence of post-QQ dynamic scaling behaviors of the subtracted particle density (supported by numerical finite-size scaling analyses), unlike their equilibrium counterpart which turns out to be dominated by nonuniversal contributions.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an innovative method to measure path weak-values in a Mach-Zehnder interferometer using only direct intensity measurements, bypassing the need for complicated meter states or weak interactions. By analyzing these intensities, the researchers successfully identified anomalous weak values, providing experimental evidence of nonclassical quantum behavior and contextuality in matter waves.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly practical, simplified measurement protocol for accessing fundamental quantum quantities like weak values in complex interferometers, which is crucial for testing foundational principles of quantum mechanics beyond standard measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize path weak-values in a generalized Mach-Zehnder interferometer without relying on complex techniques like meter states or weak interactions.

**Main result.** The authors successfully extracted path weak-values directly from intensity measurements, identifying anomalous values that confirm the nonclassical behavior and contextuality of the quantum system.

**Method.** Weak values are derived by relating measured output intensities $I_{\pm}(\delta)$—obtained solely through controlled relative phase shifts $\delta$ at the interferometer outputs—to the path weak-values using specific equations.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses a matter-wave interferometry setup, specifically a neutron Mach-Zehnder interferometer, modeling the system as a two-level quantum superposition of spatially separated paths.

**Key observables.** Path weak-values ($	ext{w}_{\pm,j}$), outgoing beam intensities $I_{\pm}(\delta)$, and single-beam path intensities ($I_{+,1}, I_{+,2}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Controlled relative phase shift ($\delta$), initial relative phase ($\phi$), contrast reduction factor ($C$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis incorporates corrections for contrast reduction due to experimental imperfections and notes that the extraction method is sensitive to intensity measurements approaching zero.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MZI setup, show sinusoidal modulation of intensities vs. phase shifts, and present plots comparing extracted weak values ($	ext{w}_R$ and $	ext{w}_I$) for both unbalanced (anomalous) and balanced configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theory of weak values, details the generalized MZI setup, derives the relationship between measurable intensities and weak values, presents methods to handle experimental imperfections (contrast reduction), and finally demonstrates the extraction process using neutron interferometry data.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Weak values provide a powerful framework for characterizing quantum systems. Their experimental extraction conventionally relies on weak conditioned von Neumann measurements, involving weak interactions and meter states that increase experimental complexity and often limit measurement efficiency. Here we introduce a method to fully characterize path weak-values in a generalized Mach-Zehnder interferometer employing neither meter states nor weak interactions. We experimentally demonstrate the technique in matter-wave interferometry. We identify anomalous weak values and, equivalently, negative quasiprobability distributions, which reflect the nonclassical behavior of the quantum system. The approach relies uniquely on intensity measurements at the output ports of the interferometer combined with controlled relative phase shifts between the paths. The absence of meter states enables considerable simplification of the setup and shorter measurement times, while preserving full access to weak values with comparable or increased accuracy. The scheme is directly applicable to a broad class of experiments involving two-level quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops phase-space measures to quantify quantum synchronization in driven-dissipative spin networks. By analyzing the resulting residuals for two and three qubits, the authors demonstrate a key distinction: phase-sensitive measures can reveal collective locking (negative residual) that is not captured by standard entropic correlation measures. This highlights that different mathematical tools probe distinct physical aspects of open system dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated diagnostic tool—the synchronization residual—to distinguish between genuine multipartite quantum correlations (which violate simple additive bounds) and those that can be decomposed into pairwise interactions within open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is quantifying multipartite quantum synchronization, specifically determining if collective phase coherence can be fully described by summing up pairwise contributions.

**Main result.** For three qubits in a driven-dissipative network, the phase-space derived tripartite residual becomes negative, indicating genuine collective locking beyond pairwise description, while entropy-based residuals remain non-negative.

**Method.** The authors introduce and analyze novel synchronization residuals built from first angular moments of phase distributions obtained via Husimi-Q quasiprobability functions in the phase space.

**Model / system.** The study uses a driven-dissipative quantum Rabi network involving two or three qubits coupled to cavity modes. The analysis is performed in the dispersive adiabatic regime, leading to effective qubit Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Phase-space synchronization residuals ($\Delta^{(3)}_R$), bipartite residual ($\Delta^{(2)}_R$), and corresponding entropy-based residuals ($\Delta^{(3)}_S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dispersive adiabatic regime; two-qubit and three-qubit coupling strengths ($J_{ij}$); cavity detunings ($\Delta_c, \Delta_i$); decay rates ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on using Husimi-Q quasiprobability distributions to approximate the true phase distributions in open quantum systems.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates two qubits coupled to a cavity system, while other figures likely compare different residual types or show time evolution of synchronization measures.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical bounds (analogous to subadditivity/strong subadditivity) for multi-partite coherence measures. It calculates these residuals for 2 and 3 qubits in a specific physical model, comparing the results derived from phase-space moments versus entropy correlations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a phase-space measure of quantum synchronization that quantifies relative phase localization for two-qubit and three-qubit systems. This measure is built from the first angular moments of phase distributions obtained from Husimi-Q quasiprobability functions. Using this framework, we formulate a new class of synchronization residuals, motivated by subadditivity-type hierarchies of information-theoretic measures. We investigate these residuals in a driven-dissipative quantum Rabi network in the dispersive adiabatic regime. We show that, for two qubits, collective synchronization remains bounded by single-qubit contributions yielding a non-negative bipartite residual. The three-qubit nonequilibrium steady state exhibits a negative tripartite residual, which indicates collective phase synchronization, which cannot be described by pairwise decomposition. The corresponding entropy-based residuals, however, remain non-negative in both cases. Our results therefore, underscore that phase-sensitive synchronization measures and entropic correlation measures probe distinct aspects of open-system dynamics.

</details>

<sub>Figure 1. Sketch of the cavity-based quantum-battery architecture. **Summary.** The paper investigates how coupling a quantum battery's primary cavity to an auxiliary cavity can suppress energy loss mechanisms causing self-discharge. By analyzing ergotropy retention across single, two-, and multi-qubit systems, the authors show that inter-cavity coupling is highly beneficial. This establishes cavity-assisted protection as a robust strategy for building long-lived quantum energy storage devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses energy storage limitations in quantum devices by engineering structured environments (the auxiliary cavity) to protect stored coherence and extractable work, a key topic in open quantum systems and quantum thermodynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is suppressing the self-discharging of stored quantum work (ergotropy) in a quantum battery due to energy leakage into the environment.

**Main result.** Inter-cavity coupling significantly enhances the protection against self-discharge, and for multi-qubit systems, the retained ergotropy increases monotonically with the number of qubits due to collective effects.

**Method.** The analysis uses a local Lindblad master equation treatment applied to a two-cavity architecture coupled to $N$ qubits.

**Model / system.** The system consists of $N$ qubits in a lossy cavity ($C1$), which is coherently coupled to an auxiliary cavity ($C2$). The dynamics are governed by the full Hamiltonian and dissipation channels, analyzed under resonant conditions.

**Key observables.** Ergotropy, $W(ho_B)$, defined as the maximum extractable work unitarily from the reduced battery state $ho_B$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-cavity coupling strength ($J$), qubit-cavity coupling ($\kappa$), and mean thermal occupation ($ar{n}$) characterizing finite temperature.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis employs a local Lindblad treatment, assuming the system is in the weak-coupling Markovian regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how retained ergotropy ($W(t)/W(0)$) depends on $J/\Gamma_1$ (showing enhancement with coupling), and demonstrate size scaling for GHZ states, showing increasing retention with $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the problem starting from single-qubit systems, progressing to two-qubit entangled states (Bell states), and culminating in multi-qubit GHZ-charged batteries to establish scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyse a two-cavity architecture, in which a lossy cavity hosting $N$ qubits is coherently coupled to an auxiliary cavity, as a resource for the storage phase of an open quantum battery at non-zero temperature. Within a local Lindblad treatment in the resonant configuration, we find that the inter-cavity coupling enhances the suppression of self-discharging across every initial preparation, battery size, and temperature we examine, with the protection degrading smoothly as the mean thermal occupation increases. For a single qubit, the energy-basis coherence of a pure superposition leads to better long-time retention than fully excited state, highlighting the beneficial role of quantum coherence in protecting stored energy against thermal degradation. For two-qubit batteries, Bell-state preparations exhibit enhanced long-time ergotropy retention compared with the fully excited state, while the inclusion of qubit-qubit interactions produces only a weak dependence on the interaction type and strength within the parameter regime considered. Extending the analysis to multi-qubit GHZ-charged batteries with all-to-all Heisenberg interactions, we find that the normalized retained ergotropy increases monotonically with the number of qubits. This behavior is consistent with the collective enhancement of the qubit-cavity coupling in the symmetric Dicke manifold, indicating that larger quantum batteries can benefit from improved protection against self-discharge. These findings establish cavity-assisted protection as a promising strategy for mitigating self-discharging and realizing of long-lived quantum batteries in experimentally accessible platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The research experimentally demonstrates how single Cs atoms can become 'active' particles by harvesting energy from their surrounding Rb bath through inelastic quantum spin-exchange collisions. By coupling the discrete quantum spin dynamics to continuous motional motion, they develop and validate an active Langevin model that successfully predicts the transient broadening of the atomic density distribution. This work opens a novel platform for studying non-equilibrium thermodynamics at the fundamental single-atom scale.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work sits at the intersection of AMO physics, non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, and quantum dynamics. The ability to engineer directed motion using internal quantum degrees of freedom in ultracold atoms is a major step toward quantum simulation of active matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is realizing active matter at the fundamental atomic scale where quantum mechanical effects govern energy transfer, moving beyond classical descriptions.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrate that individual Cs atoms can achieve directed, active motion by extracting internal spin energy from a surrounding Rb bath via inelastic quantum spin-exchange collisions. This establishes a quantum-enabled route to single-atom active matter.

**Method.** The work combines experimental measurements of atomic density distributions with theoretical modeling using parameter-free active Langevin equations and event-driven Monte Carlo simulations derived from kinetic theory.

**Model / system.** The system consists of individual Cs atoms confined in an optical dipole trap immersed in a thermal bath of Rb atoms. Activity is driven by inelastic s-wave spin-exchange collisions, which convert discrete internal spin energy into measurable kinetic motion.

**Key observables.** Cs density distribution width (s_{z,Cs}), time delay of peak broadening, and the magnitude of the active force/energy injection ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength (B), collision energy relative to activation energy ($E_c$ vs $Q$), and temperature of the bath ($	ext{T}_{	ext{Rb}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Rb cloud is treated as a Markovian bath, and the activity mechanism relies on breaking detailed balance via non-reciprocal spin exchange rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum mechanism (Fig. 1) showing energy transfer from spin to motion; later figures show density broadening measurements ($s_{z,	ext{Cs}}$) as a function of time and magnetic field, comparing theory/simulation to experiment.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical system and quantum mechanism (spin-exchange), deriving the necessary theoretical framework (Langevin model from kinetic theory), presenting experimental measurements of activity signatures (density broadening), and finally validating the model against varying external parameters like magnetic field strength.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active matter comprises particles that extract energy from their local environment and convert it into motion. Although active particles have been miniaturized down to the nanoscale, realizing activity at the fundamentally smaller scale of individual atoms remains an open challenge, where quantum effects become increasingly relevant. Here, we experimentally demonstrate that individual Cs-133 atoms confined in an optical dipole trap extract energy from an ultracold bath of Rb-87 atoms via quantum-mechanical spin interactions and convert it into active motion. We quantitatively reproduce the resulting dynamics using a parameter-free active Langevin model derived from kinetic theory and support it with event-driven Monte Carlo collision simulations. The microscopic origin of activity is identified as quantum spin exchange, which transfers discrete internal spin energy into kinetic motion. Our work establishes a quantum-enabled route to active matter at the fundamental size limit of single atoms and opens perspectives for exploring the interplay of activity, quantum physics, and mesoscopic non-equilibrium thermodynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates fast mid-circuit measurement and feedforward control in an array of neutral atoms coupled to an optical cavity. By utilizing site-selective light shifts, they reduced the operational cycle time from milliseconds to microseconds while maintaining high fidelity. This breakthrough establishes a powerful tool for implementing complex quantum algorithms on atomic qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a major bottleneck—the measurement latency—in solid-state and neutral atom quantum computing architectures by integrating high-speed optical readout with real-time classical feedback.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving fast, high-fidelity mid-circuit measurement and real-time feedforward control necessary for advanced quantum information protocols.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a significant speedup, reducing the measurement-and-feedforward cycle time to below 100 $\mu$s, establishing optical cavities as a viable route for fast control in neutral atom arrays.

**Method.** They employ site-selective illumination using near-resonant probe beams and coherence-shielding light shifts on neutral atoms trapped in an array coupled to a high-finesse optical cavity.

**Model / system.** The platform is a neutral atom array (using $^{87}	ext{Rb}$) acting as qubits, interrogated via cavity Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) interactions. Control relies on precisely tuned light shifts and real-time classical feedback processing measurement outcomes.

**Key observables.** Detection infidelity ($\le 1\%$), coherence reduction on unmeasured qubits ($\le 2\%$), and the cycle time ($< 100 \mu$s).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cavity coupling strength, probe/shielding detunings, measurement duration (optimized to $\sim 7-30 \mu$s), and feedback loop latency.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The shielding mechanism is modeled using Hamiltonian diagonalization; the analysis assumes minimal crosstalk between neighboring sites during measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, Ramsey measurements showing fidelity improvement with feedforward correction, and comparisons of state discrimination success probabilities between fixed and adaptive measurement schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the fundamental speedup via cavity coupling to demonstrating its application in complex tasks like Ramsey sequence fidelity enhancement, culminating in benchmarking optimal adaptive control for quantum state discrimination and preparation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measuring part of a quantum system in the midst of its evolution and acting on the result in real time is essential for numerous quantum information protocols. Neutral-atom arrays are a leading platform for quantum information processing, but their mid-circuit measurement-and-feedforward cycle times have remained slow, typically exceeding 1 ms. Here we demonstrate fast mid-circuit measurement and real-time feedforward in an array of atomic qubits coupled to a high-finesse optical cavity. Local light shifts tune individual data qubits out of resonance with the cavity, shielding their coherence, while a near-resonant probe drives a selected qubit whose emission is collected with Purcell enhancement. Mid-circuit measurements of four qubits with sub percent infidelity reduce the coherence of a fifth unmeasured data qubit by less than 2%. We implement real-time feedforward to correct measurement-induced phase shifts and to realize an adaptive circuit for optimal quantum state discrimination and conditional state preparation. Our approach reduces the measurement-and-feedforward cycle time to below 100 $μ$s and establishes optical cavities as a route to fast control of neutral-atom quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces and validates the use of the density matrix formalism to analyze non-adiabatic transitions in two-state quantum systems. It provides a powerful analytical solution that unifies descriptions ranging from the standard Landau-Zener approximation through highly non-adiabatic regimes, while also incorporating decoherence effects. This general framework has broad applications across quantum physics, including neutrino oscillations and cosmology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a unified theoretical tool—the density matrix approach—to study quantum transitions across various physical contexts, including those involving dissipation or environmental coupling, which are central themes in open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the theoretical description of non-adiabatic transitions occurring in two-state quantum systems subjected to time-dependent Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** It develops a general analytical framework using the density matrix formalism that successfully interpolates between known approximations (like LZSM) and regimes including decoherence effects.

**Method.** The core method involves transforming the time evolution equation into a manageable Larmor-like differential equation for the polarization vector, allowing for systematic solution in different physical limits.

**Model / system.** The primary model is a two-state quantum system whose dynamics are governed by the von Neumann equation (or Schrödinger equation). The analysis is benchmarked using neutrino flavor oscillations and can be extended to include decoherence via the Lindblad equation.

**Key observables.** Transition probabilities ($P_{1	o 2}$), survival/appearance probabilities in neutrino physics, and the polarization vector components ($\vec{P}(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The adiabaticity parameter ($\gamma$), resonance crossing probability ($P_c$), decoherence rate ($\Gamma(t)$), and system parameters like mass squared differences ($\Delta m^2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include assuming the two-state nature of the system, using specific parameterizations for the Hamiltonian, and analyzing limits such as the adiabatic or highly non-adiabatic regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of polarization vectors and potential energy landscapes during phenomena like the MSW effect in solar neutrinos, comparing different physical regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the density matrix formalism for two-state systems to deriving a general solution. It then applies this framework sequentially to analyze the adiabatic limit, the resonant crossing probability (reproducing LZSM), and finally extending the description to include decoherence effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that a density matrix formalism provides a useful description of non-adiabatic transitions in two-state quantum systems.   Compared to a traditional Hamiltonian formalism, even in the absence of decoherence when there is full equivalence between the two, the density matrix formalism provides a convenient change of variables that yields a powerful general analytical solution. This solution nicely describes a transition regime between the well known Landau-Zener-Stuckelberg-Majorana (LZSM) approximation and the extremely non-adiabatic limit. Our results have very general applications, within a large variety of problems in quantum physics, neutrino physics, cosmology.

</details>

(b) Flat-vector temporal-variance ratio Var(vpred)/Var(vtrue) across three seeds for n = 3 (partial learning, ≈18%) and n = 4 (static collapse, 0.003–1.16%); bars show seed means and points show individual seeds. The dashed line marks the 10% collapse threshold of the diagnostic.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2606.23894_figures/2606.23894_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 4: Trained NODE surrogate ¯Favg (solid, with seed standard deviation) versus the best trivial predictor at each γ (dashed; the higher of the static ρ(0) and steady-state |0 . . . 0⟩⟨0 . . . 0| baselines, per Table 1), for n = 4 at D = 2000. The curves cross near γ/J ≈0.57 (interpolated in log γ be- tween the γ/J = 0.1 and γ/J = 1 points): the surrogate adds value over trivial predictors at weak-to-moderate dissipation (left), and is matched or overtaken by the steady-state predic- tor under strong dissipation (right), where high ¯Favg reflects trajectory simplification rather than richer learned dynamics.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** The paper explores whether dissipation aids or hinders the ability of neural networks to learn open quantum dynamics in spin chains. It finds that while closed-system learning fails at larger sizes, moderate dissipation restores high fidelity by suppressing prediction errors. Crucially, it distinguishes between genuine dynamical learning and mere trajectory simplification toward a steady state.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the intersection of quantum many-body physics (open systems) and machine learning methodologies, providing crucial guidelines on how to train neural models for complex physical dynamics while distinguishing true learning from numerical artifacts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether incorporating dissipation into open quantum dynamics helps or hinders the ability of Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (NODE) surrogates to accurately learn the system's time evolution.

**Main result.** Dissipation can enhance learnability, particularly in weak-to-moderate regimes, by suppressing prediction errors; however, high fidelity at strong damping often reflects trivial steady-state contraction rather than genuine dynamical learning.

**Method.** The authors train NODE surrogates using various loss functions—comparing standard state Mean Squared Error (MSE) against physics-informed, fidelity-aware objectives incorporating trace distance constraints.

**Model / system.** The system is an open Heisenberg XYZ spin chain simulated via the GKSL master equation. Dissipation is introduced through local dephasing and amplitude damping channels acting on qubits up to $n=4$.

**Key observables.** Trajectory-averaged Uhlmann fidelity ($ar{F}_{	ext{avg}}$), late-time vs. early-time fidelity gap ($\Delta F$), minimum eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\min}$), and comparison against static/steady-state predictors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($n=2, 3, 4$ qubits), dimensionless dissipation strength ($\gamma/J \in \{0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100\}$), and loss function weighting parameters ($\lambda_{TD}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the use of fidelity-aware losses is necessary to overcome the static collapse observed when using only state-MSE training for larger systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $ar{F}_{	ext{avg}}$ vs. $\gamma/J$ showing optimal regimes, and compare late-time versus early-time fidelity ($\Delta F$) to distinguish genuine dynamics from simple error suppression.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares learning performance across different system sizes ($n=2, 3, 4$) and dissipation strengths ($\gamma/J$), contrasting the failure modes of standard MSE training with the recovery achieved using advanced fidelity-aware loss functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dissipation is usually viewed as an obstacle to predicting quantum dynamics, yet it can also contract trajectories toward steady states and thereby suppress accumulated prediction errors, leaving it unclear whether dissipation ultimately helps or hinders the learnability of open quantum dynamics. We investigate this question using Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (NODE) surrogates for open Heisenberg XYZ spin chains. Closed-system learnability deteriorates rapidly with system size, culminating in a static-prediction collapse at four qubits; dissipation reverses this trend, creating a broad high-fidelity regime at intermediate system sizes, while at four qubits a fidelity-aware objective recovers learnable rollout structure that is absent under closed-system training. Comparison against static and steady-state baselines reveals that dissipation improves performance through two fundamentally different mechanisms: at weak-to-moderate dissipation the surrogate captures nontrivial transient dynamics and substantially outperforms trivial predictors, whereas at stronger damping high fidelity increasingly reflects trajectory simplification toward the steady state rather than improved learned dynamics. These results show that dissipation can enhance the learnability of open quantum dynamics, but that fidelity alone is insufficient to distinguish genuine dynamical learning from steady-state trivialization: dissipative contraction and trajectory simplification are distinct effects that peak in different regimes and should be disentangled when evaluating learned quantum-dynamical surrogates.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel linear-optical scheme to certify and lower-bound the dimensionality of entanglement in time-bin encoded photon pairs. By leveraging particle-exchange symmetry and performing measurements via Hong-Ou-Mandel interference, they overcome limitations of traditional characterization methods. This technique provides a scalable tool for verifying high-dimensional quantum resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, experimentally accessible tool for quantifying entanglement in high-dimensional photonic systems without requiring full state tomography, which is crucial for scaling quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a general method to characterize high-dimensional entanglement, particularly in time-bin encoded photonic states, which traditional methods struggle with due to measurement complexity.

**Main result.** The authors developed a linear-optical scheme using particle-exchange symmetry that certifies high-dimensional entanglement and provides a rigorous lower bound on its dimensionality using only two simple dichotomic measurements.

**Method.** The method combines Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference with unitary transformations ($U_{\pm}$) and formulates the problem as an optimization program, solved via Semidefinite Programming (SDP) relaxation to find a Schmidt number witness.

**Model / system.** The system uses photons encoded in discrete time bins, generated via SPDC. The analysis focuses on bipartite states ($ho$) evolving under specific symmetry operations related to particle exchange.

**Key observables.** HOM coincidence probabilities ($C_{\exp, \pm}^{	ext{HOM}}$), visibility ($V$), and the entanglement dimension lower bound derived from SDP solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The certified entanglement dimensionality ($k$) relative to the modeled dimension ($d$); time-bin separation ($\sim 9.1 	ext{ ps}$); and measurement outcomes for symmetry projections.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach relies on exploiting particle-exchange symmetry; approximations involve truncating evolved states back onto the initial subspace, which makes the bound conservative but robust.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate HOM interference dependence on particle exchange symmetry (bunching/anti-bunching) and show how certified entanglement dimension increases with the inclusion of physical symmetries like time-invariance and diagonal symmetry.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework using SDP to bound entanglement dimensionality based on measured symmetry projections from HOM interference. It then details an experimental realization using linear optics on time-bin encoded photons, comparing results under various assumed symmetries (e.g., no assumptions vs. full physical constraints).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photons encoded in discrete time bins can be routinely prepared in temporal superposition states, enabling high-dimensional entanglement and enhanced quantum communication rates. However, characterizing this high-dimensional entanglement presents significant challenges, namely due to the involved measurement complexity or reliance on restrictive assumptions that compromise the generality of traditional approaches. Here, we develop and experimentally demonstrate a simple linear-optical scheme based on particle-exchange symmetry that allows us to probe high-dimensional entanglement in time-bin-encoded states. Combining Hong-Ou-Mandel interference with suitable transformations, our method not only certifies entanglement but also lower-bounds its dimensionality using only two dichotomic symmetry-based measurements. This bound is obtained through a new rigorous theoretical analysis and can be further improved by weak, physically motivated assumptions. The scheme remains effective at any timescale, even far below the temporal detector resolution used. Our work provides a powerful state-characterization tool and demonstrates that we can prove high-dimensional temporal entanglement on timescales inaccessible to the setup.

</details>

**Authors:** Luciano C. Lapas  
**Type:** both · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.23909v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **4/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

(11) (solid lines), against TDTR data of Wilson and Cahill [10] (symbols, with error bars; digitized from their Fig. 3a at w0 = 10 µm). Fourier’s law predicts a constant ΛA; Si and Si:B stay flat while Ge and the SiGe alloys are suppressed at high f, where the shrinking penetration depth dp(f) probes a larger wavevector. **Summary.** This work generalizes Fourier's law by deriving a nonlocal conductivity kernel using advanced statistical mechanics techniques. It shows that heat transport in mesoscopic materials exhibits spatial nonlocality, leading to measurable apparent anisotropy even when the material itself is isotropic. The framework successfully models experimental data from TDTR measurements on semiconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on thermal transport, the use of projection-operator formalism and memory kernels ($\kappa_{	ext{eff}}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$) provides a rigorous theoretical framework for non-Markovian dynamics in open quantum systems, which is highly relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Fourier's law fails when energy carrier mean free paths are comparable to system length/time scales, necessitating a thermodynamically consistent generalization for heat transport in mesoscopic systems.

**Main result.** The generalized conductivity is derived as a nonlocal memory operator $\kappa_{	ext{eff}}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$, which predicts apparent anisotropy ($\Lambda_z 
eq \Lambda_r$) even in isotropic materials due to measurement geometry.

**Method.** The authors combine Mesoscopic Nonequilibrium Thermodynamics with the Mori–Kubo–Zwanzig projection-operator formalism to derive the nonlocal constitutive relation.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to heat conduction in mesoscopic systems (like Si/SiGe) involving energy carriers (phonons, electrons), modeling the transport using a generalized Fourier law incorporating memory and spatial nonlocality.

**Key observables.** Apparent conductivities ($\Lambda_z$, $\Lambda_r$) measured via Time-Domain Thermoreflectance (TDTR); the nonlocal conductivity tensor $\kappa_{	ext{eff}}(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlocality length scale ($\ell$), mean free path ($\ell_c$), and frequency/wavevector dependence ($k, \omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linear response theory, the assumption of small deviations from equilibrium, and approximations like the minimal one-length ansatz for $\kappa_{	ext{eff}}(k)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how a single nonlocal kernel leads to apparent anisotropy ($\Lambda_z 
eq \Lambda_r$) and show theoretical fits comparing the predicted frequency roll-off of apparent conductivity against experimental TDTR data.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the failure of Fourier's law, deriving the generalized nonlocal constitutive relation using projection operators, analyzing its consequences (anisotropy), and finally fitting these predictions to experimental measurements on semiconductor alloys.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fourier's law fails when the mean free path of the energy carriers becomes comparable to the length and time scales over which the temperature field varies. We derive a thermodynamically consistent generalization in which the conductivity is promoted to a nonlocal memory operator $\bmκ_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{k},ω)$, obtained by combining mesoscopic nonequilibrium thermodynamics with the Mori--Kubo--Zwanzig projection-operator formalism. The Onsager kernel decomposes exactly into a tensor sum over vibrational normal modes weighted by their Bose heat capacities and relaxation functions, and satisfies the second law by construction. Two consequences follow. First, because the modal weights carry the directional group velocity, the kernel is anisotropic, so a nominally isotropic crystal exhibits direction-dependent apparent conductivities $Λ_z\neqΛ_r$. Second, in a pump--probe experiment the modulation frequency does not introduce temporal memory but sets the probed wavevector through the thermal penetration depth, so the suppression of the apparent conductivity measured by time-domain thermoreflectance on Si, Ge and Si$_{1-x}$Ge$_x$ is a spatial-nonlocality effect set by a sub-micron carrier mean free path. Fitting the data of Wilson and Cahill yields nonlocality lengths of $0.25$--$0.4~μ$m consistent with the mean-free-path spectra of these crystals. The framework supplies a thermodynamic foundation for the two-channel ballistic/diffusive picture of nondiffusive heat transport.

</details>

**Summary.** The research models charge order melting in electron-phonon systems under strong laser pumping by coupling the system to a thermal bath. Using a two-temperature approximation, it distinguishes between disorder caused by lattice fluctuations (bath temperature) versus electronic excitation energy ($T_{el}$). The findings map out a phase diagram showing that local distortion and long-range coherence melt via distinct mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium many-body physics in correlated materials, using techniques relevant to understanding ultrafast photoinduced transitions common in quantum optics and condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how charge order, driven by electron-phonon coupling, melts and subsequently recovers following strong photoexcitation in a non-equilibrium setting.

**Main result.** The paper distinguishes between thermal melting (loss of spatial coherence due to bath temperature) and pump-induced melting (direct suppression of local distortion amplitude), establishing a phase diagram controlling the transition regimes.

**Method.** A two-temperature, stochastic mean-field approach is employed, treating electronic excitations via an effective time-dependent temperature ($T_{el}(t)$) while phonons interact with a thermal bath at $T_{bath}$.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the spinless Holstein model, incorporating electron hopping, phonon dynamics, and coupling to both a laser pump (driving non-equilibrium electronic states) and an external thermal bath.

**Key observables.** Order parameter ($S_Q$), local lattice displacement distribution ($P(x)$), Electronic Density of States (DOS), and recovery time ($	au_{rec}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bath temperature ($T_{bath}$), effective electronic temperature ($T_{el}(t)$), and the ratio $T_{el}/t_{hop}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations include modeling pump excitations via an effective $T_{el}(t)$ and assuming that variations in $T_{el}$ and phonons occur slowly compared to electron hopping.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between energy decay in closed vs. open systems, show time-dependent fitting of $T_{el}(t)$, and present a phase diagram mapping charge order survival against $T_{el}$ and $T_{bath}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the full Hamiltonian and then develops the two-temperature Langevin dynamics for the open system. It analyzes energy relaxation, characterizes the melting mechanisms via observables like $S_Q$ and $P(x)$, and concludes by mapping out a phase diagram.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Charge order driven by electron-phonon coupling is well understood at equilibrium but pump-probe experiments raise a new question: how does this order melt and recover after strong photoexcitation? A pump pulse promotes carriers across the charge-order gap and creates a nonequilibrium high-energy electronic population. In a closed system the subsequent dynamics is constrained by energy conservation. In an `open system' - where the system is coupled to a thermal bath at some temperature $T_{\rm bath}$ - there are new fluctuation and dissipation processes at play. One can attempt a computational scheme that incorporates coupling of electrons to a laser pump, the coupling of system phonons to a thermal bath, and the Holstein interaction that couples electrons and phonons. We attempt an approximation where the pump induced electronic excitations are modeled by a slowly time varying `electron temperature', $T_{\rm el}(t)$, indicative of a quasi-equilibrium electronic state. We solve the problem for different combinations of $T_{\rm el}$ and $T_{\rm bath}$, probing the order parameter dynamics, the static properties and excitations in the long time `quasi steady state', and establish a `phase diagram' in terms of bath temperature and electron temperature.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes open quantum dynamics in non-Hermitian SSH models subjected to pure dephasing. It reveals that the decay of quantum information (measured by trace distance) is highly dependent on the initial state, leading to phenomena like partial or complete stabilization rather than simple exponential decay. This work highlights a complex interplay between system symmetry and dissipation effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed, non-trivial understanding of how dissipation (dephasing) interacts with engineered non-Hermiticity, revealing that information loss is not monotonic but can be stabilized or even suppressed depending on the initial quantum state.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how pure dephasing affects the dynamical evolution of non-Hermitian quantum systems, particularly focusing on initial-state-dependent effects.

**Main result.** Dephasing can lead to two distinct stabilization mechanisms—partial or complete—and exhibits an unconventional protective effect in certain symmetry phases. The dynamics are strongly dependent on the specific initial state chosen.

**Method.** The analysis uses the quantum Lindblad master equation, solving for the trace distance evolution under pure dephasing and employing strong-dephasing limits to linearize population dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model is a finite-sized non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) lattice with alternating gain and loss in real space. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation, allowing exploration of PT and APT symmetry phases.

**Key observables.** Trace distance ($D(t)$), population elements ($ho_{nn}$), and the Liouvillian gap ($\Delta E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dephasing strength ($\gamma_d$), non-Hermiticity parameters ($\eta_1, \eta_2$), and system size (up to 40 sites).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on the strong dephasing limit ($\gamma_d 	o \infty$) or weak-dephasing perturbation theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams based on symmetry, and show $D(t)$ evolution for different initial states in both small (4-site) and large (40-site) systems, demonstrating stabilization regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and symmetries to analyzing the general dynamics under dephasing. It then details specific mathematical techniques—like linearization in the strong limit or perturbation theory for gap analysis—to derive quantitative results on state decay and stabilization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the dynamical evolution of non-Hermitian systems under extra external dissipation is essential. Dephasing, a major realistic dissipation, is conventionally considered detrimental to information processing. However, its impact on non-Hermitian systems remains largely unexplored. Here, we focus on finite-sized non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) lattice models with alternating gain and loss in real space and examine the dynamical evolution of the trace distance under pure dephasing. By tuning system parameters, this model supports phases with either parity-time or anti-parity-time symmetries, enabling us to explore the interplay between dephasing and different non-Hermitian symmetries. While the trace distance exhibits distinct dynamical behaviors across the different phases in the absence of dephasing, its response to dephasing is largely symmetry-independent but instead initial-state dependent. By varying initial states, we observe that increasing the dephasing strength can either merely accelerate the decay of the trace distance or stabilize it. Interestingly, we reveal two kinds of dephasing-induced stabilization that differ in the strong dephasing limit: a partial stabilization, where the trace distance approaches a finite value smaller than its initial value in the long-time limit, and a complete stabilization, where the trace distance remains at its initial value throughout the entire evolution. By analyzing the equation of motion, we attribute the initial-state dependent dephasing effect to the alternating gain and loss in the system and confirm its absence in Hermitian counterparts. Furthermore, in the anti-parity-time symmetry unbroken phase, we identify a continuous suppression-upon increasing the dephasing strength-of the otherwise exponential decay of the trace distance seen in the absence of dephasing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reviews the complex field of quantum turbulence, focusing on where mean-field theories fail due to strong quantum fluctuations. It synthesizes knowledge from superfluids and lattice models to argue that a complete description requires advanced many-body techniques beyond simple Gross-Pitaevskii approximations. The work highlights open questions regarding universal scaling laws in these non-equilibrium quantum regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges classical turbulence phenomenology with modern many-body physics, suggesting new theoretical frameworks needed to describe non-equilibrium dynamics in ultracold atomic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the theoretical challenge of understanding turbulent hydrodynamics in quantum many-body systems when strong quantum fluctuations are present, moving beyond mean-field descriptions.

**Main result.** It establishes that while mean-field theories (like GP) capture classical inertial ranges, a full description requires accounting for strong quantum fluctuations, especially near critical points or at low dimensions.

**Method.** The analysis synthesizes knowledge from continuum hydrodynamics (Euler/GP equations), lattice models (Bose-Hubbard), and field theory approaches to identify regimes where approximations break down.

**Model / system.** The primary systems are superfluids, modeled using the Gross-Pitaevskii equation for mean-field analysis, but requiring extensions to second quantization or lattice Hamiltonians when fluctuations dominate. Specific examples include bosons in periodic potentials and low-dimensional condensates.

**Key observables.** Energy spectrum scaling ($\sim k^{-5/3}$), quantized circulation, density fluctuation magnitude ($\delta n / \langle nangle$), and the existence of multiple inertial ranges.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), inter-vortex spacing ($\lambda$), healing length ($\xi$), and the ratio of quantum fluctuations to mean density ($\delta n / \langle nangle$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The paper contrasts regimes: near equilibrium (classical/mean-field) versus far from equilibrium where strong quantum fluctuations necessitate going beyond standard approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy cascade in classical vs. quantum turbulence, showing expected spectral deviations and phase diagrams for Bose-Hubbard models separating superfluid and Mott insulator phases.

**Paper structure.** The discussion progresses from reviewing mean-field descriptions (GP equation) to identifying limitations when fluctuations are strong, then detailing lattice models (Bose-Hubbard), and finally outlining the necessity of quantum kinetic theory for a complete picture.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We discuss phenomenology associated with turbulent hydrodynamics in quantum fluids from a condensed-matter perspective. We begin with weakly-interacting superfluids, often modeled by a mean-field theory governed by the Gross-Pitaevskii equation. Considering the effect of quantum fluctuations beyond the mean-field approximation, we propose a study of many-body quantum effects in turbulent hydrodynamics, especially near zero temperature. We motivate examples of quantum many-body systems where such effects may be uncovered. These include bosons confined in a periodic potential in low spatial dimensions (one and two), and the associated quantum critical point of the superfluid-insulator transition, realized in present-day ultracold-atom and quantum computing platforms. We conclude by listing a set of (open) questions that may be answered using modern quantum many-body techniques. This article is part of the theme issue 'Frontiers of turbulence and statistical physics'.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports an experiment demonstrating how to monitor the entanglement generated between two squeezed vacuum states mixed at a beam splitter. Instead of relying solely on inseparability criteria, the authors use a novel measure called 'Quantumness' ($\Xi$). This approach allows them to quantitatively track and report on experimental losses by measuring the deviation from $\Xi$'s expected conservation across the optical setup.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a robust, quantitative method ($\Xi$) to benchmark continuous variable entanglement experiments by tracking non-classical features through complex optical components, which is highly relevant for developing scalable quantum light sources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to monitor entanglement generated by mixing two independent squeezed vacuum states using a beam splitter while accurately quantifying experimental imperfections.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'Quantumness' ($\Xi$) as a superior diagnostic tool that remains conserved under ideal beam mixing, allowing for precise quantification of losses due to experimental imperfections compared to standard entanglement witnesses.

**Method.** They compare the quantumness ($\Xi$) calculated before and after passing through the beam splitter; deviations from conservation quantify the degradation caused by imperfect components.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses a quantum-optical setup involving two independent squeezed vacuum states generated via Optical Parametric Oscillators (OPOs) and mixed at a balanced beam splitter to create an entangled Two-Mode Squeezed Vacuum state.

**Key observables.** Quantumness ($\Xi$), quadrature variances ($	ext{Var}(x), 	ext{Var}(p)$), and the difference in quantumness ($\Delta\Xi$) before and after mixing.

**Important parameters / regimes.** OPO pump powers ($P_a, P_b$); the degree of squeezing relative to the Standard Quantum Limit (SQL).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that for Gaussian states, $\Xi$ faithfully tracks non-classicality, and that deviations from $\Xi$ conservation directly quantify experimental losses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup schematic, show input squeezed quadratures below SQL, plot entanglement confirmation using Duan's criterion, and crucially compare calculated quantumness before and after beam mixing across varying pump powers.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical system (OPOs $ightarrow$ BS), introduces Quantumness ($\Xi$) as a diagnostic tool superior to inseparability criteria, derives the conservation law for ideal mixing, and then presents experimental measurements comparing input vs. output quantumness under varying pump powers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report on an experiment in which two independent squeezed vacuum states get entangled by mixing them with a balanced beam splitter. We follow standard practice and use an inseparability criterion to quantify their entanglement. However, this only allows us to witness the entanglement, but not to determine the deleterious effects of experimental imperfections due to the beam splitter mixing and the associated mode-mismatch and detection imperfections. We therefore introduce an alternative framework suitable for continuous variable systems using the states' quantumness, $Ξ$. We show that, under ideal circumstances, $Ξ$ is a conserved quantity under beam mixing. This allows us to benchmark the experiment's performance by comparing the states' quantumness $Ξ$ after the beam splitter mixing with $Ξ$ before. Such a comparison is not possible with entanglement witnesses, as the input states are unentangled. This highlights the main strength of our approach: its ability to generally quantify the quantumness of multi-mode continuous variable states and use this to probe different stages in an experiment.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates how to measure the intrinsic exchange phase of anyons in fractional quantum Hall systems using an antidot interferometer. By applying a non-equilibrium Keldysh formalism, the authors predict that the transmission phase will show unique non-monotonic behavior near resonance crossings. This signature allows for the direct extraction of the fundamental topological exchange phase $\pi
u$ from measurable interference patterns.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical pathway to measure fundamental topological properties (anyon statistics) using measurable transport quantities like interference phase shifts, which is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to directly measure the bare exchange phase ($	heta$) associated with anyons, which has been theoretically inaccessible despite prior measurements of fractional charge and braiding phase ($2	heta$).

**Main result.** The authors predict that the transmission phase will exhibit a non-monotonic evolution when tuning the antidot through a resonance. This allows for the extraction of the bare exchange phase $	heta = \pi
u$ from the difference between interference current plateaus.

**Method.** The study employs a systematic, non-equilibrium Keldysh formalism applied to an electronic T-matrix calculation, incorporating level broadening and antidot occupation effects.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a quantum antidot embedded within a Fabry-Perot interferometer structure, designed to host quasiparticles behaving as anyons in fractional quantum Hall systems. The analysis models tunneling through this resonant structure.

**Key observables.** Transmission phase ($\delta$), interference current ($I_{d,AB}$), and non-equilibrium antidot occupation ($n(V_g)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anyon statistics parameter $
u$ (e.g., 1/3) and the gate voltage $V_g$, which tunes the system through resonance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that electrostatic screening by an external gate is necessary to isolate the bare statistical phase, and it uses perturbative expansions in tunneling couplings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-monotonic evolution of the transmission phase ($\delta$) near resonances for anyons compared to monotonic behavior for electrons. Other figures detail antidot occupation dynamics and current calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical setup (antidot interferometer) to developing a rigorous Keldysh formalism that handles non-equilibrium effects, culminating in the prediction of phase signatures ($\delta$) that isolate the exchange phase $	heta$ via plateau analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quasiparticles in fractional quantum Hall systems are anyons, carrying a fraction of the electron charge. Exchanging two of them gives rise to a fractional exchange phase. While the fractional charge and the braiding phase -- twice the exchange phase -- have been measured, the exchange phase itself has remained inaccessible. We study a quantum antidot embedded in a Fabry-Perot interferometer. Within a systematic non-equilibrium Keldysh treatment that consistently includes the occupation and level broadening of the antidot, we find that the transmission phase evolves non-monotonically when a gate voltage tunes the antidot through a resonance, in contrast to the monotonic evolution for electrons. The bare exchange phase can be extracted from the difference between the phase plateaus.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper connects higher-order Exceptional Points (EPs) in non-Hermitian systems to the behavior of genuine multipartite entanglement (GME). By exploiting permutation symmetry, the authors derive a general framework showing that GME exhibits a characteristic fractional-power scaling directly proportional to the inverse order of the EP. This establishes a fundamental link between spectral degeneracies and quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges spectral theory (non-Hermitian physics) and quantum information theory (entanglement), providing a powerful diagnostic tool: measuring entanglement scaling can reveal the underlying degeneracy order of the system's Hamiltonian.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to develop a general theoretical framework linking higher-order non-Hermitian degeneracies (Exceptional Points, EPs) to the scaling behavior of genuine multipartite entanglement (GME). It seeks to establish a fundamental connection between these concepts in interacting quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The study shows that GME inherits the spectral nonanalyticity near an $n$-th order EP, exhibiting a characteristic fractional-power scaling ($\Delta C \propto \delta^{1/n}$). Furthermore, it establishes that realizing higher-order EPs requires specific multi-body interaction channels.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes permutation symmetry to decompose the Hilbert space of identical qubits and then analyzes the spectral response near an EP using perturbation theory, leading to a Puiseux series expansion for both energy eigenvalues and entanglement measures.

**Model / system.** The model consists of interacting identical-qubit systems governed by a non-Hermitian Hamiltonian $\hat{H}$, which includes single-qubit terms and arbitrary $l$-body interaction terms (e.g., XYZ Heisenberg interactions). The system's structure is constrained by permutation symmetry.

**Key observables.** Genuine Multipartite Entanglement (GME), spectral response, fractional-power scaling ($\delta^{1/n}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the Exceptional Point ($n$), the number of qubits ($N$), and the strength of weak perturbations ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Hamiltonian is assumed to preserve permutation symmetry; the analysis focuses on the leading-order scaling behavior near the EP.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the connection between interacting identical-qubit systems, the block-diagonal structure imposed by permutation symmetry, and the resulting fractional-power response of multipartite entanglement near an EP.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces EPs in non-Hermitian physics. It then leverages permutation symmetry to constrain the Hilbert space and analyze the system's degeneracy limit ($N+1$). The core is developing the framework linking this degeneracy structure directly to the scaling law of GME under weak perturbations, concluding with explicit examples requiring higher-order interactions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exceptional points (EPs) are non-Hermitian spectral singularities exhibiting fractional-power responses, yet their implications for multipartite entanglement of interacting quantum many-body systems remain largely unexplored. Here we develop a general framework that links higher-order non-Hermitian degeneracies to the scaling behavior of genuine multipartite entanglement in interacting identical-qubit systems. Permutation symmetry of the identical qubits decomposes the exponentially large Hilbert space into independent irreducible-representation sectors, thereby constraining the maximal EP order of $N$ qubits to $N+1$ rather than $2^N$. Near an $n$th-order EP, genuine multipartite entanglement inherits the spectral response and generically exhibits a fractional-power scaling under weak perturbations. Explicit examples show that conventional two-body interactions support third- and fourth-order EPs with the corresponding entanglement responses, whereas higher-order EPs with genuine multipartite-entangled coalesced states require additional independent interaction channels, such as three-body interactions. Our results establish a fundamental connection among non-Hermitian degeneracies, multipartite entanglement, and symmetry, extending higher-order EP physics from spectral singularities to genuine many-body quantum correlations.

</details>

**Authors:** Michael A. Rampp, Suhail A. Rather, Pieter W. Claeys
