**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive framework—the Full Dual-Unitary ($	ext{FDU}_k$) hierarchy—to achieve exact solvability for quantum circuits across the entire spacetime. By combining complementary unitarity conditions, the authors overcome previous limitations that restricted solvable regions to finite domains. This opens new avenues for precisely calculating correlation functions and entanglement dynamics in complex, non-integrable quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of an infinite hierarchy of exactly solvable, non-integrable models provides powerful theoretical tools to study complex quantum many-body dynamics where standard integrability methods fail.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to extend the solvability of quantum dynamics beyond the limitations of generalized dual-unitary circuits ($	ext{DU}_k$) to analyze correlation functions and entanglement dynamics across the entire spacetime.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a novel, infinite hierarchy of conditions ($	ext{FDU}_k$) that remedies the finite spacetime restriction, allowing exact analysis of correlations and entanglement in all regions.

**Method.** The work employs tensor network representations, algebraic constraint derivation from diagram consistency, and analyzing asymptotic behavior via quantum channels to characterize solvability domains.

**Model / system.** The system is based on quantum circuits built with dual-unitary gates applied to qubits or qudits in a lattice geometry. The analysis focuses on the structure of these solvable dynamics across spacetime.

**Key observables.** Correlation functions $C(x, t)$, Entanglement Line Tension ($	ext{ELT}$), and the solvability condition itself (FDU$_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The level of unitarity ($k$), the velocity $v$, and the local Hilbert space dimension ($q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing complementary dual-unitary conditions ($	ext{DU}^*_k$) to supplement the original hierarchy, and simplifying complex tensor network structures by considering extremal paths.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the logical inclusion of solvability domains ($	ext{FDU}_k = 	ext{DU}^*_k + 	ext{DU}_k$), show the piecewise linear nature of the ELT for $	ext{FDU}_k$ circuits, and depict tensor network diagrams.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the limitations of $	ext{DU}_k$, proposes the complementary conditions ($	ext{DU}^*_k$) to form $	ext{FDU}_k$, derives explicit formulas for correlators in different spacetime path domains (thin/thick), and finally analyzes entanglement scaling via the ELT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dual-unitary circuits have emerged as a paradigm of exactly solvable yet non-integrable quantum dynamics. Recently, a generalization of dual unitarity attempting to extend the phenomenology of exactly solvable circuits has been introduced through a hierarchy of conditions, with dual unitarity as the first level. However, beyond the second level the proposed generalized dual-unitary hierarchy ceases to be solvable in the whole spacetime. We present an infinite hierarchy of solvability conditions remedying this problem. These new conditions can be combined with the generalized dual-unitary hierarchy to obtain circuits for which correlation functions and entanglement dynamics can be analyzed exactly in the whole spacetime. We show that this novel hierarchy possesses non-trivial solutions at every level. Our results demonstrate that dual unitarity can be systematically extended while preserving solvability, opening up investigations of exactly solvable non-integrable systems with more general properties.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Quantum Split-Step Fourier Algorithm (QSSF) to simulate nonlinear optical waveguides while explicitly tracking quantum entanglement. By monitoring observables like von Neumann entropy and purity, the authors demonstrate that resonant radiation generates significant, quantifiable entanglement between spectral components. This method proves invaluable for diagnosing quantum correlations in ultrafast light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, quantitative tool—an information-theoretic diagnostic—to study quantum correlations in complex, non-equilibrium physical processes like supercontinuum generation, which is highly relevant to advanced quantum optics research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a numerical framework to quantify quantum correlations, specifically entanglement, generated between different spectral bands during nonlinear optical wave propagation.

**Main result.** The Quantum Split-Step Fourier Algorithm (QSSF) successfully demonstrates that resonant radiation generation leads to a steadily increasing von Neumann entropy and loss of purity in the selected spectral band. Furthermore, this highly correlated state is found to be effectively dominated by only a few Williamson modes despite occupying many Fourier bins.

**Method.** The method employs the QSSF algorithm, which combines split-step propagation (Strang splitting) for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a commutator-preserving Bogoliubov evolution for Gaussian quantum fluctuations. This allows tracking second moments and entanglement measures dynamically.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as light propagating in nonlinear optical waveguides governed by the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation (NLSE). The analysis linearizes quantum fluctuations around a strong classical mean field, leading to an effective Bogoliubov-deWitt-Gross evolution equation.

**Key observables.** Von Neumann entropy ($S_{vN}$), purity, covariance matrices, symplectic eigenvalues ($
u_k$), and the effective number of occupied Williamson modes ($K_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinearity coefficient ($\gamma$), dispersion parameters ($eta_3$), propagation distance ($z$), and the spectral window size.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes Gaussian quantum fluctuations, allowing the use of Bogoliubov theory. The method relies on linearizing the full quantum NLSE around a strong classical mean field and maintaining commutator-preserving evolution during splitting steps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral evolution showing resonant radiation (RR) growth; plots demonstrate the monotonic increase of entanglement entropy ($S_{vN}$) and photon population ($ar{N}_{	ext{RR}}$); and visualizations confirm that the quantum state compresses into a few dominant Williamson modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QSSF algorithm by detailing its components: splitting methods, Bogoliubov evolution for linear steps, and handling nonlinear coupling. It then applies this framework to simulate resonant radiation in waveguides, calculating various information-theoretic diagnostics like entropy and purity over propagation distance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce the Quantum Split-Step Fourier (QSSF) algorithm for nonlinear optical waveguides, a numerical framework that combines split-step propagation of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a commutator-preserving Bogoliubov evolution of Gaussian quantum fluctuations. The method propagates the classical mean field together with the Bogoliubov matrices $U$ and $V$, from which reduced second moments, covariance matrices, symplectic eigenvalues, and entropic measures are constructed for arbitrary spectral windows. Applied to soliton-driven resonant radiation, QSSF shows that the selected radiation band acquires a steadily increasing von Neumann entropy and a corresponding loss of purity, quantifying its entanglement with the rest of the spectrum in the lossless Gaussian setting. The analysis also reveals a surprisingly pronounced low-dimensional structure: although the radiation occupies many Fourier bins, its reduced Gaussian state is dominated by only a few Williamson modes. QSSF therefore provides a practical information-theoretic diagnostic for quantum correlations in nonlinear frequency conversion, supercontinuum generation, and multimode squeezed-light formation in ultrafast waveguide platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys the theoretical landscape of 2D phase transitions, detailing established concepts like the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition. It critically updates this knowledge by incorporating recent findings on how non-equilibrium active matter systems deviate from these classical predictions, pointing toward new avenues for understanding melting.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial bridge between classical equilibrium statistical mechanics (like BKT theory) and modern non-equilibrium physics, which is highly relevant for understanding complex many-body dynamics far from equilibrium.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The review comprehensively analyzes two-dimensional (2D) phase transitions, focusing on the BKT transition and crystal melting, while extending the discussion to non-equilibrium active matter systems.

**Main result.** The paper contrasts equilibrium predictions (like HMW and BKT theory) with novel phenomena observed in active matter, suggesting that non-equilibrium dynamics fundamentally alter the expected phase transition behavior.

**Method.** It synthesizes results from Renormalization Group (RG) theory, continuum descriptions of topological defects (Coulomb gas), finite-size scaling analysis, and comparisons between different thermodynamic ensembles (NVT vs NPT).

**Model / system.** The primary models include the XY spin model for continuous symmetry breaking, 2D crystalline solids undergoing melting, hard disk systems, and active Brownian particles (ABPs) in non-equilibrium settings.

**Key observables.** Topological defect charge ($q$), stiffness ($ho_{	ext{eff}}$), helicity modulus ($\gamma$), order parameters (translational $S$ and bond-orientational $\Psi$), and the behavior of P-V curves.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), system size ($L$), interaction potential range ($n$ in $r^{-n}$), and activity/self-propulsion speed ($	ext{activity}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The review notes that early theories rely on continuum descriptions, and numerical simulations are limited by finite-size effects and critical slowing down.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate topological defects in spin systems and 2D crystals; one figure shows the calculation of translational correlation functions via reciprocal vector scanning; another details phase diagrams for hard polygons showing various melting routes (e.g., $	ext{S} 	o 	ext{TX} 	o 	ext{R}_X$).

**Paper structure.** The review progresses logically from foundational theory (HMW/BKT) to detailed analysis of passive systems (melting via KTHNY), then explores the influence of particle shape and interaction potential range, culminating in a discussion of non-equilibrium active matter.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In 1966, Hohenberg, Mermin and Wagner proved that long-wavelength fluctuations destabilize the long-range order of continuous symmetry in two-dimensional (2D) systems. Later in the 1970s, Berezinskii, Kosterlitz and Thouless developed the BKT theory describing an unconventional phase transition between quasi-long-range and short-range order in 2D systems driven by the binding-unbinding of topological defects, which has become a fundamental topic in statistical mechanics, condensed matter physics, and soft matter physics. One of the most important applications of the BKT theory is the melting of 2D crystals, whose mechanisms are not yet fully understood. Recently, this topic has been extended to the area of active matter, where the non-equilibrium nature leads to novel phenomena that deviate from the Hohenberg-Mermin-Wagner theorem. In this review, we first focus on the recent theoretical and computational progress in the 2D melting problem in passive systems, and then summarize the inspiring results obtained from non-equilibrium systems. The review closes with comments on several promising directions for predicting 2D melting scenarios and for understanding the non-equilibrium nature in 2D active matter systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the recurrence behavior in a diatomic FPUT lattice by distinguishing between classical FPUT energy trapping and a new resonance-driven mechanism. The authors prove that resonant interactions between optical and acoustical modes can induce robust, long-lived energy exchange, which is mathematically modeled using reduced integrable PDEs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a detailed theoretical framework for energy localization and long-time dynamics in nonlinear lattices, directly addressing concepts of energy transfer and quasi-periodicity relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the recurrent behavior of solutions within a diatomic Fermi–Pasta–Ulam–Tsingou (FPUT) lattice subjected to cubic anharmonic potentials.

**Main result.** Two distinct recurrence types are found: classic FPUT recurrence and a novel type driven by three-wave resonant interactions between optical and acoustical modes, which is unique to diatomic lattices.

**Method.** The analysis combines linear mode decomposition (finding optical/acoustical branches) with numerical simulations of energy density evolution in Fourier space, culminating in the derivation of reduced integrable PDE models in the continuous limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a diatomic FPUT lattice with alternating masses and cubic anharmonic potential. The dynamics are analyzed using normal modes derived from the Hamiltonian, leading to coupled equations for specific resonant Fourier modes.

**Key observables.** Energy distribution among Fourier modes over time; recurrence period ($T$); ratio $|E_0 - E_{\min}| / E_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinear strength ($\alpha$); mass ratios ($m_2/m_1$); initial excitation conditions (e.g., exciting specific resonant triads).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes that energy outside the interacting resonant triad is negligible, and in the continuous limit, it derives integrable PDE systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy density recurrence over time for both resonant and non-resonant conditions; Figure 4 shows the log-log relationship between recurrence period and nonlinear strength $\alpha$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the diatomic model and linear modes, to identifying two distinct recurrence mechanisms (classic vs. resonance-induced), developing reduced models for the resonant triad, and finally comparing these behaviors across different potentials and mass fluctuations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the diatomic FPUT lattice with cubic anharmonic potential, and analyze the recurrent behaviour of its solutions. We find that two distinct types of recurrence occur. One type is the classic FPUT recurrence; for such recurrence, we find that the relation between recurrence period and nonlinear strength is similar to that in the monatomic case. The other type, which cannot exist in the monatomic lattice, is the recurrence due to the interactions between modes in the two branches of the dispersion relation. Indeed, we prove the existence of the optical-acoustical-acoustical resonant interaction between three Fourier modes for which a recurrent behavior in the distribution of the energy is observed. In addition, we develop a reduced Fourier-space dynamical model that reproduces the same recurrent behavior. We assess the robustness of our results through numerical simulations of the diatomic Toda lattice and the diatomic granular chain; in both cases, the same recursive behavior is observed. Finally, in the continuous limit, we derive from the diatomic model a system of three coupled PDEs which are known to be integrable.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an exact mean-field theory for a growing social group model whose dynamics are driven by consensus inference rather than thermal equilibrium. By reformulating the process in log-odds space, the authors derive critical exponents and demonstrate that non-equilibrium growth can organize complex phase transitions using tools analogous to those from statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, non-equilibrium analogue to classical critical phenomena studies, showing how concepts like universality and scaling can emerge from purely growth-driven, information-theoretic processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes critical phenomena and collective behavior in a growing social group model that is intrinsically non-equilibrium, focusing on how consensus builds over time.

**Main result.** It establishes an exact mean-field theory for this growth process, deriving critical exponents and showing that the system's dynamics can be rigorously analyzed using tools from information geometry and RG theory without assuming a Hamiltonian structure.

**Method.** The analysis reformulates the stochastic process as a gradient flow on logarithmic time ($	au = \ln N$), leading to an effective potential and fixed-point equations solvable via log-odds (arctanh) transformations.

**Model / system.** The model is a growing social group where membership updates are governed by noisy, consensus-driven admissions based on multiple independent evaluations ($m$). The dynamics are Hamiltonian-free, making it a non-equilibrium system.

**Key observables.** Polarization ($\phi$), effective potential ($F_{	ext{eff}}$), critical exponents ($eta=1/2, \gamma=1, \delta=3$), and the rate of entropy production $\langle\dot{s}angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Evaluation reliability ($\alpha = 1 - 2\eta$), number of evaluators ($m$), and prior bias field ($h$). Criticality occurs when $m\alpha$ overcomes growth dilution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mean-field limit (all-to-all evaluation) and uses mathematical tools like martingale convergence theory to prove convergence to deterministic ODEs in log time.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the effective potential $F_{	ext{eff}}(\phi)$ showing transitions, phase diagrams mapping out different selection rules (DS, PA), and comparison plots contrasting the nested arctanh structure with the standard Ising model.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a systematic three-layer framework: core theory (Landau potential/Ising comparison), mathematical foundations (Pólya-urn martingale convergence/RG flow), and complementary perspectives (irreversibility via entropy production).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an exact analytical reformulation of a growing social group model -- a Hamiltonian-free nonequilibrium process in which a group grows by noisy, consensus-driven admission. Cast as a gradient flow on logarithmic time, the fixed-point structure collapses to a single self-consistent equation: $\arctanh(φ^*) = m \cdot \arctanh(αφ^*)$, where $φ$ is the polarization, $α=1-2η$ the evaluation reliability, and $m$ the number of evaluators. The equation has a direct log-odds interpretation: each verdict contributes log-likelihood ratio $2\arctanh(αφ)$; unanimity accumulates $m$ independent evidence pieces. The dynamics thus constitutes an exact mean-field theory of self-consistent inference, ordering when the collective gain $mα$ overcomes the dilution of growth. We develop a systematic three-layer framework: core theory (Landau-like effective potential, comparison with the mean-field Ising model, and features without equilibrium counterpart), mathematical foundations (criticality from correlated verdicts, Pólya-urn martingale convergence, and an RG-like flow with group size as scale), and complementary perspectives on irreversibility and information geometry. A frozen-$N$ Freidlin--Wentzell quasipotential yields Kramers-type escape estimates for metastable states, while Monte Carlo simulations collapse onto a parameter-free deterministic master curve on logarithmic time. Systematic comparison with the mean-field Ising model reveals shared critical exponents but a nested arctanh structure unique to growth. These results provide a detailed analytical characterization of a minimal model of growth-driven collective behavior and map which elements of the equilibrium critical toolbox -- suitably reinterpreted -- survive without a Hamiltonian.

</details>

### [Picosecond Schrödinger cat states for ultrafast optical quantum processing](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24002v1)

**Summary.** The research demonstrates a method to generate high-quality, multi-photon Schrödinger cat states using picosecond optical pulses. By overcoming previous rate limitations, they achieve an effective cat-state amplitude close to the theoretical requirement for fault-tolerant quantum error correction codes like GKP states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering hurdles—specifically the temporal mode bandwidth—that limit the scalability of photonic quantum information processing by demonstrating high-rate generation of complex non-Gaussian resources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is generating non-Gaussian quantum states, such as Schrödinger cat states, at a high repetition rate necessary for fault-tolerant optical quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated multi-photon generalized photon subtraction in picosecond temporal modes, achieving an effective cat-state amplitude ($\alpha_{	ext{eff}} = 1.69$) relevant for cat-code architectures and GKP state breeding.

**Method.** State generation is achieved via interfering two squeezed vacuum states on a beam splitter, heralded by photon-number-resolving detection (TES), and characterized using pulsed homodyne detection matched to ultrashort pulses.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses broadband Type-0 PPLN waveguides to generate squeezed light, which is then processed optically. The system operates in picosecond temporal modes ($\sim 10$ ps) at a high repetition rate (5 MHz pump).

**Key observables.** Effective cat-state amplitude ($\alpha_{	ext{eff}}$), Wigner function negativity (indicating non-Gaussianity), and the number of negative regions observed for multi-photon heralding.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Picosecond temporal mode width, $\alpha_{	ext{eff}} = 1.69$, target GKP amplitude ($\approx 1.77$), and high repetition rate capability (5 MHz).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reported $\alpha_{	ext{eff}}$ values are extracted without full loss correction; the study also compares experimental results to ideal, lossless simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the apparatus setup, phase-dependent quadrature distributions showing interference fringes, and reconstructed Wigner functions exhibiting negative regions for multi-photon heralding events.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the rate bottleneck in state generation to detailing the experimental setup (squeezed sources, VBS, PNRD), presenting measurements of $\alpha_{	ext{eff}}$ and Wigner function negativity, and finally discussing the implications for GKP lattice breeding.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Gaussian states are essential resources for universal, fault-tolerant optical quantum computing, but their generation rate remains limited by low heralding probabilities and operation in nanosecond temporal modes. Here, we demonstrate multi-photon generalized photon subtraction in picosecond optical wave packets, establishing the state-generation capability required for high-rate operation by addressing the temporal-mode bottleneck that has constrained the achievable rate. Two interfering ultrashort squeezed vacua are heralded by photon-number-resolving detection with a high-speed transition-edge sensor and characterized by pulsed homodyne detection matched to 10-ps temporal modes at a 5-MHz pump repetition rate. We reconstruct Wigner functions without loss correction that exhibit up to four distinct negative regions for four-photon heralding, together with an effective cat-state amplitude of $α_{\mathrm{eff}} = 1.69$. This amplitude approaches the range of practical relevance for fault-tolerant cat-code architectures and for adaptive breeding toward logical-qubit generation, while the picosecond temporal mode establishes a platform compatible with high-rate, scalable time-multiplexed photonic architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** The research presents a method to enhance entangled photon source brightness by spectrally engineering the spontaneous parametric down-conversion process using detuned pumping. This technique yields a highly usable, narrow-band source that successfully generates and distributes polarization entanglement over several kilometers of free space. The findings establish spectral control as a practical route for developing robust quantum communication hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimental blueprint for creating compact, high-performance quantum light sources whose spectral properties are actively controlled, which is crucial for scaling up quantum communication networks beyond laboratory settings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing entangled photon sources with high spectral brightness suitable for practical quantum communication and networking applications.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a spectrally engineered SPDC source achieving high spectral brightness ($0.507 	ext{ MHz}\cdot	ext{mW}^{-1}\cdot	ext{nm}^{-1}$) and distributing polarization entanglement over a $2.56 	ext{ km}$ free-space link.

**Method.** The method involves spectrally engineering the SPDC process by using an ultra-narrowband laser detuned from degeneracy, which concentrates photon pair generation into a narrow bandwidth while maintaining collinear operation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a type-0 spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) source pumped in a ppKTP crystal. The process generates entangled photon pairs, which are then used to create polarization entanglement via a Sagnac interferometer and tested over a free-space quantum channel.

**Key observables.** Coincidence rate ($44.6 	ext{ kHz}$), detected spectral brightness ($0.507 	ext{ MHz}\cdot	ext{mW}^{-1}\cdot	ext{nm}^{-1}$), entanglement fidelity, and temporal correlation over distance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump power ($15 \mu	ext{W}$), free-space channel length ($2.56 	ext{ km}$), spectral bandwidth ($\Delta\lambda_{bp} = 5.87 	ext{ nm}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation of spectral brightness does not account for all optical transmission losses, and the degradation in daytime fidelity is attributed to background noise rather than source performance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup (collinear pumping, Sagnac interferometer), the spectrally split nature of the photon pairs using JSI calculations, temporal correlation confirmation over distance, and quantum state tomography results showing entanglement fidelity.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the spectral engineering technique to enhance brightness, demonstrates the source's ability to generate polarization entanglement via a Sagnac setup, and validates this distribution capability by transmitting the entangled pairs over a long free-space link while measuring temporal correlations and fidelity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entangled photon sources with high spectral brightness are important resources for photonic quantum information processing, particularly in quantum communication and quantum networking where usable photon flux of entangled photons is often constrained by channel loss and source inefficiency. Here, we demonstrate a spectrally engineered type-0 spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) source with enhanced spectral brightness for entanglement distribution. By pumping a 30-mm ppKTP crystal with an ultra-narrowband laser slightly detuned from degeneracy, photon-pair generation is concentrated into a narrow spectral bandwidth while retaining the strong nonlinear interaction of type-0 phase matching. The source produces a coincidence rate of 44.6 kHz corresponding to a detected spectral brightness of 0.507 MHz/mW/nm. We further integrate the source into a Sagnac interferometer to generate polarization-entangled photon pairs and demonstrate entanglement distribution through a 2.56 km free-space round-trip channel. Our results show that spectral engineering provides a practical route to compact, spectrally bright entangled-photon sources for quantum communication applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how topological defects form during the non-equilibrium condensation of a coherently coupled Bose gas. By analyzing defect statistics using advanced tools like Poisson Point Processes and Spatial Form Factors, the authors demonstrate that the resulting spatial organization exhibits universal geometry beyond standard Kibble-Zurek scaling. This establishes a fundamental statistical description for defect formation in such quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantitative framework for characterizing the emergent geometry of defects in interacting quantum systems, extending fundamental concepts like KZM into regimes involving multiple coupled components. The use of SFF connects condensed matter physics to spectral analysis techniques from quantum chaos.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the universal spatial statistics of topological defects formed during the non-equilibrium condensation of a coherently coupled Bose gas, aiming to establish physics beyond standard Kibble-Zurek mechanism (KZM) predictions.

**Main result.** The defect positions are found to follow a homogeneous Poisson point process (PPP), revealing a universal stochastic geometry that persists even when considering composite topological defects. This universality is confirmed by analyzing Voronoi tessellation and the Spatial Form Factor (SFF).

**Method.** The analysis employs the stochastic projected Gross-Pitaevskii equation (SPGPE) framework, combining theoretical scaling arguments with advanced tools from stochastic geometry like PPP modeling, Poisson-Voronoi tessellation, and SFF calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is a coherently coupled Bose gas in two hyperfine states undergoing a quench driven by a linear variation of the chemical potential. The dynamics are modeled using the SPGPE for a 2D system.

**Key observables.** Topological defects (elementary and composite Full Quantum Vortices, FQVs); defect density ($ho$); equilibration time ($t_{eq}$); Poisson point process statistics; Voronoi cell area distribution; Spatial Form Factor (SFF).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quench rate ($	au_Q$), coupling strength ($\Omega$), and the ratio of components' interactions ($g_{12}/g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are projected onto low-energy modes via SPGPE, and the underlying spatial statistics are assumed to be well described by a Poisson Point Process (PPP).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate condensate norm growth and equilibration time scaling; they also show pair-distance distributions, nearest-neighbor spacing histograms conditioned on topological charge, and SFF plots comparing numerical results against analytical PPP predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the non-equilibrium dynamics (determining $t_{eq}$), then analyzing defect numbers ($\langle N angle$) via KZM scaling, and finally delving into spatial statistics using PPP, Voronoi tessellation, and SFF to prove universal geometric organization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the universal spatial statistics of point-like topological defects formed during the nonequilibrium condensation of a coherently coupled Bose gas using the stochastic projected Gross-Pitaevskii equation. The symmetry-breaking transition is driven by a linear quench of the chemical potential, leading to stochastic vortex nucleation in the individual condensate components. When the two components are considered together, these elementary defects may combine across components to emerge as composite topological defects known as full quantum vortices. Beyond the mean defect density predicted by the Kibble-Zurek mechanism (KZM), we investigate the spatial organization of both the elementary and composite defects and show that their positions are well described by a Poisson point process, revealing a universal stochastic geometry. This universality is further described through Voronoi tessellation, whose cell-area statistics follow Poisson-Voronoi predictions. We also introduce the spatial form factor for characterizing the vortex configurations and demonstrate the emergence of a characteristic dip-ramp-plateau structure. Our results establish universal stochastic geometry of topological defects beyond conventional Kibble-Zurek scaling and identify it as a fundamental feature of nonequilibrium condensation in coherently coupled Bose gases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the implementation of a high-fidelity two-qubit CZ gate using novel P-mon superconducting qubits. By leveraging mediator modes and cross-Kerr interactions, they achieve robust coupling while suppressing unwanted crosstalk. The use of advanced pulse shaping techniques like HD-DRAG is crucial for reaching state-of-the-art gate fidelities necessary for scalable quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed blueprint for engineering coherent quantum gates in solid-state superconducting circuits by exploiting engineered multimode physics, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing scalable superconducting quantum processors by engineering qubit interactions that suppress decoherence channels, particularly unwanted ZZ-type coupling.

**Main result.** The authors successfully implemented a high-fidelity CZ gate (99.62% fidelity) using P-mon qubits coupled via mediator modes, demonstrating a path toward scalable architecture.

**Method.** Gate performance was optimized by comparing pulse shaping techniques (Gaussian vs. HD-DRAG) and analyzing the system dynamics using Lindblad master equations to minimize coherent errors.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is superconducting P-mon qubits, which are multimode circuits featuring protected qubit modes coupled via flux-tunable mediator modes and a bus resonator. The Hamiltonian describes these interactions, allowing for controlled coupling via cross-Kerr effects.

**Key observables.** CZ gate fidelity (99.62%), residual ZZ interaction strength (< 3.6 kHz), single-qubit fidelities ($\approx 99.97\%$), and the accumulated entangling phase $\phi_{ZZ}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate time (180 ns), drive detuning ($\Delta d$), mediator coupling strengths ($g_{BB}$), and coherence times ($T_1, T_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the dispersive limit for initial derivations and relies on advanced pulse shaping (HD-DRAG) to mitigate coherent errors arising from spectral overlap.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level diagrams showing cross-Kerr shifts, circuit schematics of coupled P-mons, and comparisons of gate fidelity versus pulse length using both Gaussian and HD-DRAG pulses.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the protected nature of the P-mon qubit, then detailing how mediator modes enable controlled coupling. It moves through Hamiltonian derivation, experimental characterization (measuring couplings), and finally focuses on optimizing gate fidelity using advanced pulse shaping techniques like HD-DRAG.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

To scale superconducting quantum processors, it is essential to achieve long coherence times while engineering interactions that do not introduce additional decoherence channels. In superconducting qubit systems, this can be realized using multimode circuits that feature a protected qubit mode alongside a distinct mediator mode. Building on this concept, our recently developed P-mon qubit provides intrinsic protection against decoherence from the readout environment. We extend this approach to controlled two-qubit interactions, by exploiting the mediator modes of P-mons for on-demand coupling. Because direct interactions between the qubit modes are strongly suppressed, unwanted $ZZ$-type interactions are significantly reduced to below $3.6(5)~\text{kHz}$ in the idle state. When tuning the coupled mediator modes on resonance, the cross-Kerr interaction between the qubit and the hybridized mediator modes leads to a qubit-state dependent frequency shift. By selectively addressing these transitions, we implement a $180~\text{ns}$ long CZ gate and determine a fidelity of $99.62(4)~\text{%}$. These results represent a significant step toward a scalable superconducting architecture that maintains high performance at scale.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel, fully fiber-integrated quantum buffer capable of storing and retrieving quantum states across the entire telecom C-band. By utilizing Cross-Phase Modulation in a dual-Sagnac cavity, it overcomes major trade-offs associated with existing memory technologies. This robust platform enables high-rate synchronization for global photonic quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an all-optical, room-temperature solution for quantum memory that directly addresses the bandwidth/storage trade-off, which is critical for scaling photonic quantum networks beyond current limitations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The realization of a scalable quantum internet requires on-demand, high-fidelity buffering of asynchronous photonic signals across the telecom band.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a universal, fully fiber-integrated quantum buffer achieving ultra-low loss (0.46 dB), long storage times ($\sim 18 \mu$s), and broadband operation ($>12.5$ THz).

**Method.** The system uses an actively switched dual-Sagnac cavity driven by Cross-Phase Modulation (XPM) to store quantum states encoded in time-bin, frequency-bin, or polarization degrees of freedom.

**Model / system.** The platform is based on two Sagnac interferometers integrated into optical fiber. Storage relies on the $\chi^{(3)}$ nonlinearity inducing a phase shift via XPM from an intense control pulse onto the quantum signal pulse.

**Key observables.** Input/output loss (0.46 dB), storage time ($\sim 18 \mu$s), operational bandwidth ($>12.5$ THz), and state fidelity ($>95\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Storage lifetime, Time-Bandwidth Product ($2.2 	imes 10^8$), and the ability to address multiple temporal modes (e.g., 200+).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system relies on the wavelength-dependent behavior of the beamsplitters and assumes that XPM provides sufficient switching contrast.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the working principle using Sagnac interferometers, show time-bin entanglement interference patterns (CHSH violation), and demonstrate selective input/delayed retrieval of multiple temporal modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for quantum buffering to detailing the XPM mechanism in a dual-Sagnac setup. It then rigorously characterizes performance metrics—loss, storage time, and fidelity—before demonstrating advanced capabilities like multi-DoF preservation and high-density temporal multiplexing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The realization of a scalable quantum internet relies on the ability to temporally align asynchronous photonic signals through on-demand buffering. While matter-based quantum memories achieve long storage times, their extremely narrow bandwidths and cryogenic requirements pose significant barriers to integration with existing telecommunications infrastructure. Conversely, current all-optical memories operate at room temperature but are hampered by high input/output losses and a lack of universality across different photonic degrees of freedom. Here, we demonstrate a universal, fully fiber-integrated quantum buffer operating over the full telecom C-band that overcomes these fundamental trade-offs. By implementing an actively switched dual-Sagnac cavity driven by cross-phase modulation, we achieve an ultra-low input/output loss of 0.46 dB and a storage time exceeding 18 $μ$s. The device exhibits an operational bandwidth exceeding 12.5 THz ($\sim$100 nm), covering the full telecom C-band. We show the simultaneous buffering of over 200 temporal modes with the ability to address them either collectively or one by one. We demonstrate high-fidelity storage for all three degrees of freedom compatible with optical fiber propagation, namely time-bin, frequency-bin, and polarization qubits, along with faithful preservation of entanglement, confirming the platform's true universality. These results provide a robust, room-temperature solution for the high-rate synchronization of multidimensional quantum states, clearing a major hurdle for the deployment of global photonic quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces Tailored Hilbert Space (THS), a novel theoretical framework to study quantum phase transitions by modifying the model's effective Hamiltonian. By tailoring the Hilbert space using specific reference states, the authors can extract precise critical exponents and correlation lengths for models like the XY and XXZ chains using extremely small lattices. This provides an efficient, universal route for characterizing QPTs in many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO physicists studying quantum phase transitions (e.g., in cold atom arrays or superconducting circuits), this offers a powerful computational tool that bypasses the need for large system sizes or complex finite-size extrapolations when characterizing critical behavior.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop an efficient method to analyze quantum phase transitions (QPTs) and extract critical exponents, overcoming limitations associated with finite-size scaling.

**Main result.** A novel Tailored Hilbert Space (THS) scheme successfully yields precise critical points and universal exponents for models like the 1D XY and XXZ chains using very small lattices. This method robustly characterizes QPTs across different universality classes.

**Method.** The core technique involves modifying the system Hamiltonian by adding a projection term based on a tailored reference state $|\Psi_1angle$, allowing probing of criticality without traditional finite-size scaling procedures.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to quantum spin models, specifically the 1D XY model and the XXZ chain. The framework is generalizable to other many-body lattice systems exhibiting QPTs.

**Key observables.** Critical exponents ($
u$, $eta$), critical points ($h_c$, $\Delta_c$), correlation lengths ($\xi$), local order parameters ($\hat{m}_{z,i}$), and topological invariants.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (small lattices like N=12 to 50), magnetic field/coupling constants ($h$, $\phi$, $\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that ground states belonging to distinct quantum phases occupy distinct, measurable regions within the tailored Hilbert space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy spectra showing conical band crossings at criticality (XY model), and plot correlation length scaling ($\xi \propto |h - h_c|^{-
u}$) for both XY and BKT transitions in the XXZ model.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces THS, applies it to the 1D XY model to find critical exponents, generalizes the technique to the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition in the XXZ chain, and discusses its universal applicability across different QPT types.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Finite-size scaling and the renormalization group form the central toolkit   for analyzing quantum phase transitions (QPTs). In this Letter, we introduce   a novel Hilbert-space tailoring scheme to probe quantum critical phenomena.   Applied to the second-order QPT of the one-dimensional (1D) XY model, our method   yields precise critical points and exponents on lattices containing merely 50   unit cells. We further establish the universal applicability of this   framework via investigations of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless   transition in the 1D XXZ chain: critical parameters are recovered   with as few as 12 lattice sites. This technique may open an alternative,   efficient route to universally characterize QPT across many-body lattice systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on a novel dual-isotope MOT technique for dysprosium using phase modulation via Electro-Optic Modulators. By precisely tuning the EOM drive frequency, the authors achieve simultaneous and controllable trapping of different isotopes in a narrow-line regime. This method provides a highly efficient and versatile tool for studying mixtures of atomic species.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work showcases advanced optical techniques (EOM phase modulation) applied to cold atoms for precise isotopic control, which is crucial for quantum simulation platforms requiring multiple atomic species.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is characterizing a narrow-line Magneto-Optical Trap (MOT) capable of simultaneously loading and trapping two different isotopes of dysprosium using phase modulation.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated full control over the isotopic mixture in the MOT by tuning the Electro-Optic Modulator (EOM) drive frequency, offering a cost-effective method for controlling mixtures of atomic species.

**Method.** They utilized high-power, free-space EOMs to generate optical sidebands at detunings specific to two different isotopes, enabling simultaneous trapping on narrow transitions.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses Dysprosium (Dy) atoms, specifically targeting bosonic isotope mixtures like ${}^{164}	ext{Dy} - {}^{162}	ext{Dy}$ and ${}^{162}	ext{Dy} - {}^{160}	ext{Dy}$. Trapping occurs on narrow-line transitions at 626 nm, preceded by cooling at 421 nm.

**Key observables.** Trapped atom number ($N$), center-of-mass (CoM) shift, and the relative population imbalance between isotopes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Isotope shift ($\Delta I_S \approx 962 	ext{ MHz}$), EOM drive frequency ($\Omega = \Delta I_S / 2$), and modulation depth ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique relies on using non-resonant EOMs with external electronics to overcome bandwidth limitations inherent in built-in LC circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the electronic structure, the setup geometry, and show how the EOM drive frequency controls the isotopic mixture and causes measurable spatial displacements of the two isotope clouds along the gravity axis.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the development of a dual-isotope MOT using sidebands from EOMs. It characterizes performance by measuring atom numbers vs. modulation depth, demonstrating control over the relative population imbalance, and mapping CoM shifts to measure $\Delta I_S$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report on the characterization of a narrow-line magneto-optical trap (MOT), where two different isotopes of the dysprosium (Dy) atom can be simultaneously loaded and trapped. We rely on phase modulation via high-power, free-space electro-optic modulators (EOMs) to generate optical sidebands at the correct detuning for two different isotopes. This technique is applied on the slowing transition at 421 nm and the MOT transition at 626 nm. Using modular resonant electronic circuits, we match the sideband separation to the isotope shift among the two target isotopes, realizing mixtures of the bosonic isotopes 164Dy -162Dy and 162Dy -160Dy. We exploit the interplay between radiation forces and gravity in the narrow-line MOT to control the spatial position of the two isotopes with the EOM drive frequency. We demonstrate full control of the isotopic mixture with the EOM drive, offering new and cost-effective capabilities for controlling mixtures of atomic species that can be trapped in a narrow-line MOT.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a method to drastically improve the performance of fiber-coupled SPDC sources by exploiting mode-selective nonlinear interference in a cascaded two-crystal setup. The technique overcomes inherent trade-offs between brightness and heralding efficiency by more than an order of magnitude. Both theoretical modeling using Laguerre-Gauss modes and experimental validation via SFG confirm that this architecture enables high-purity, high-brightness quantum light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of quantum light sources by engineering complex spatio-spectral correlations through nonlinear optics, which has direct implications for quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming intrinsic trade-offs (brightness vs. heralding efficiency vs. spectral purity) in single-mode-fiber-coupled Spontaneous Parametric Down-Conversion (SPDC) sources.

**Main result.** By utilizing nonlinear interference in a cascaded two-crystal setup, the authors demonstrate an improvement of over one order of magnitude in the brightness–heralding efficiency trade-off while maintaining near-unity spectral purity.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling using spectrally resolved Laguerre–Gauss modal decomposition with experimental validation via Sum-Frequency Generation (SFG) measurements.

**Model / system.** The system is a cascaded two-crystal type-II SPDC source. The analysis models the process by incorporating nonlinear interference effects across both spectral and spatial degrees of freedom using Laguerre-Gauss modal bases.

**Key observables.** Pair-collection probability (Brightness), Heralding efficiency, Spectral purity ($P$), Joint Spectral Amplitude (JSA).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The improvement factor is $>10$-fold; high heralding efficiency ($H \geq 0.97$) and spectral purity ($P > 0.99$) are key targets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analysis uses the plane-wave approximation, but results extend to complex spatial modes using LG decomposition; the enhancement relies on dispersion compensation in the cascaded structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between single-crystal and two-crystal PMFs, showing interference effects that suppress sidelobes and enhance performance. Experimental setup diagrams detail the use of tunable lasers and nonlinear crystals for measurement.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the fundamental trade-off in SPDC sources to developing a theoretical model based on nonlinear interference in cascaded systems. It then quantifies the improvement over single-crystal limits, discusses spectral/spatial mode decomposition, and concludes with experimental validation using SFG measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Single-mode-fiber-coupled spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) sources are a key resource for photonic quantum technologies, but in single-crystal geometries brightness, heralding efficiency, and spectral purity remain constrained by intrinsic trade-offs. Here, we show how nonlinear interference in a cascaded two-crystal type-II SPDC source can be used to engineer the modal structure of SPDC emission, improving the brightness--heralding-efficiency trade-off by more than one order of magnitude beyond the single-crystal limit. We further demonstrate two routes to near-unity spectral purity while retaining high brightness and/or heralding efficiency, even with standard periodically poled crystals, and study the additional advantages of aperiodic poling with Gaussian phase matching. Using a spectrally resolved Laguerre--Gauss modal decomposition, we show that these improvements arise from mode-selective interference of spatial-spectral SPDC modes within the nonlinear interferometer. We experimentally validate the model through sum-frequency-generation measurements of the spatial-spectral state.

</details>

The thermodynamic instability occurs at their intersection (red dot). c) Diagrams which are subtracted from the impurity response in b), see text: 1 ○the single boson exchange spin-transverse diagram sp of the generalized susceptibility, 2 ○the single-boson sp of the two-particle irreducible...</sub>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes how charge, spin, and pairing instabilities are related by symmetry in the Hubbard model. It shows that these responses exhibit degeneracies due to pseudospin symmetry, confirming simultaneous CDW/SC instabilities numerically. The framework provides a powerful tool for understanding competing orders in strongly correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on Hubbard model physics, the rigorous mathematical framework linking multiple response functions through underlying symmetries provides a general tool for understanding correlated quantum matter, which is relevant to many-body dynamics in AMO systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates degeneracies between charge, spin, and pairing susceptibilities that arise from underlying symmetries in strongly correlated electron systems.

**Main result.** The analysis analytically derives mutual relations showing that these three response functions are degenerate for the unfrustrated Hubbard model, a degeneracy lifted by geometrical frustration or symmetry breaking ($t'$).

**Method.** The work combines analytical derivations exploiting pseudospin symmetries (SU(2)P) with advanced numerical techniques like Cluster Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (CDMFT) and Bethe-Salpeter Equation (BSE) analysis.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Hubbard Hamiltonian, studied on a bipartite lattice. The analysis focuses on how competing orders—charge density waves (CDW), spin density waves (SDW), and superconductivity (SC)—interact through their respective susceptibilities.

**Key observables.** Charge susceptibility ($\chi^{ch}$), spin susceptibility ($\chi^{sp}$), and pairing/superconducting susceptibility ($\chi^{pp}$) for various form factors (s, d, sext).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Hubbard interaction strength ($U$), hopping parameters ($t, t'$), doping levels, and momentum transfer vectors ($q_{ph}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analytical results are rigorously derived for the unfrustrated, bipartite Hubbard model; numerical extensions rely on approximations like CDMFT.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate symmetry relations between response functions (Fig. 1), show concomitant divergences of d-wave CDW and SC susceptibilities at $T_c$ in CDMFT (Fig. 2a), and map out how introducing $t'$ gradually lifts these degeneracies (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing exact symmetry relations using pseudospin symmetry, to demonstrating the degeneracy numerically via CDMFT simulations, and finally analyzing how breaking symmetries ($t'$) or moving beyond simple lattice limits affects this fundamental relationship.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strongly correlated electron systems often display competing or even intertwined ordering tendencies, hinting to extremely close or degenerate many-electron energies. While degeneracies are directly rooted in the underlying symmetries of the problem under investigation, their multifaceted effects on different response functions and their mutual relations often remain elusive. Here we put this subject on a rigorous basis by investigating the degeneracies of charge, spin, and pairing susceptibilities for the unfrustrated, bipartite Hubbard model. Exploiting its pseudospin symmetry, we analytically derive the mutual relations between these response functions for generic spatial modulations, highly relevant, e.g., for the competition of stripe and superconducting orders. By means of two-particle numerical simulations we demonstrate the occurrence of a simultaneous $d$-wave pairing/$d$-density wave (loop current) instability in the vicinity of the metal-insulator transition, driven by short-ranged spin fluctuations for the two-dimensional case. We show how this degeneracy is gradually lifted by geometrical frustration, which favors superconductivity. Our study provides a general tool for revealing symmetry relations in correlated electron systems and establishes a unifying perspective on the nature of their intermingled charge/loop current, pairing, and spin orders.

</details>

**Authors:** Vaughn Sohn, Changhun Oh  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24469v1>

The symbol ⊕denotes the SUM gate, ⊖denotes the inverse-SUM gate, and the filled circle indicates the control mode. (c) Distance-5 surface-GKP code. White nodes denote data qubits. The protocol then selects an action a = (aZ, aX), where aP determines whether the P-type surface-code stabilizers are measured or skipped. Measured components are obtained from surface-code stabilizer measurements, while skipped components reuse the previous syndrome value. The resulting syndrome history is then used for MWPM decoding after T rounds.</sub>

The corresponding time-like edge, shown as the gray dashed line, is retained with weight w = 0, so paths such as (1) and (2) can pass through the skipped interval without additional penalty.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an adaptive scheme for Quantum Error Correction on Surface-GKP codes designed to reduce measurement overhead. It determines, at each step, whether the noise from performing a full syndrome measurement outweighs the benefit of fresh information. By intelligently skipping measurements when reliability is high, it maintains low error rates while significantly reducing resource consumption.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use analog information from one part of the quantum process (inner GKP correction) to guide resource management in another (outer surface code syndrome extraction) is a key concept for optimizing physical implementations of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is mitigating the significant overhead and noise introduced by repeated syndrome extraction circuits required for fault-tolerant quantum error correction.

**Main result.** An adaptive skipping scheme was proposed that reduces stabilizer measurements while maintaining logical error rates comparable to or lower than full measurement baselines, especially when active circuit noise dominates idle noise.

**Method.** The authors developed a decision rule comparing the reliability of reusing previous syndrome information (inferred from analog GKP outcomes) against performing a new noisy extraction.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on the Surface-GKP code, which uses GKP states within an outer surface code structure. The analysis models noise using Gaussian displacement channels and Binary Symmetric Channels for syndrome corruption.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates ($\chi_L$), number of surface-code stabilizer measurements, and effective standard deviations ($\sigma_A^Z, \sigma_A^X$) for edge weights in the decoding graph.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameters include active syndrome-extraction noise ($\sigma$), idle noise ($\sigma_{idle}$), and GKP state preparation noise ($\sigma_{gkp}$). The decision depends on comparing these relative strengths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol uses an approximation for the global success probability, restricts decisions to four global actions, and assumes that zero-weight assignment correctly models skipping without penalty in the decoding graph.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the code architecture, the noisy syndrome extraction process as a BSC, finite-round performance comparisons between adaptive vs. full measurement baselines across noise regimes, and decoding graphs showing zero-weight time-like edges for skipped rounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the overhead problem to modeling the noise (BSC/Gaussian channels), developing the quantitative decision score based on success probabilities ($	ext{p}_{	ext{skip}}^{	ext{succ}}$ vs. $	ext{p}_{	ext{meas}}^{	ext{succ}}$), and finally simulating the adaptive strategy's performance across various noise regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum error correction requires repeated syndrome extraction to address errors induced by the syndrome-extraction circuit itself. However, repeated syndrome extraction incurs significant overhead in terms of gate count and ancilla consumption (e.g., Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) states). Moreover, noisy syndrome extraction can itself inject additional errors into the data qubits. To address these issues, we propose a concrete adaptive skipping scheme for the surface-GKP code, a representative GKP-concatenated architecture, that uses analog information naturally generated during inner GKP correction. At each round, the scheme selects one of four actions: measuring both Z-type and X-type surface-code stabilizers, measuring only one type, or skipping both types and reusing previous syndromes. The decision is based on a reliability comparison between reusing the previous syndrome value and performing a new noisy syndrome extraction. Using circuit-level simulations, we show that the adaptive skipping scheme can reduce the number of surface-code stabilizer measurements while maintaining logical error rates comparable to or lower than those of the full-measurement baseline. The improvement is most pronounced when gate and measurement noise are larger than idle noise, so that avoiding unnecessary syndrome extraction reduces the noise injected into the code. These results indicate that analog information from inner GKP correction can be used not only to improve decoding but also to reduce the measurement overhead of outer-code syndrome extraction.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes an advanced method to solve the NP-hard phase optimization problem in RIS systems by designing QAOA angles offline using theoretical limits from spin-glass models. The approach successfully transfers these pre-calculated, channel-independent angles to simulate performance across various complex wireless fading environments. It suggests a viable path toward near-optimal power aggregation achievable only on future fault-tolerant quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges quantum optimization theory (spin glasses, QAOA) with a highly relevant applied physics problem (wireless communications/RIS), providing concrete benchmarks for future fault-tolerant quantum hardware applications in electromagnetics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is optimizing the aggregate received power for Reconfigurable Intelligent Surfaces (RIS) by setting discrete per-element phases, which is an NP-hard combinatorial optimization task.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that an offline, channel-independent angle dictionary derived from infinite-size spin-glass limits can achieve near-optimal performance across various realistic fading channels for small system sizes ($N \le 16$).

**Method.** They formulate the problem as a QUBO/HUBO and employ QAOA with angles pre-optimized offline using techniques derived from Basso et al.'s infinite-size Gaussian ensemble benchmark.

**Model / system.** The system is an RIS array whose phase optimization objective ($\max \|A e^{j	heta}\|^2$) is mapped onto a spin Hamiltonian. The theoretical framework relies on relating this to the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model and its infinite-size limits.

**Key observables.** Performance ratios ($r_b, r_m$), which compare achieved power to the classical optimum; single-shot success probability ($P_{0.9}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** QAOA depth ($p$), number of elements ($N$), dictionary size ($L=2^M$), and channel type (Rayleigh, Rician/LOS, etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is designed for the Fault-Tolerant Quantum (FTQ) regime; it relies on reducing the high-order Hamiltonian to a manageable order-2 Ising surrogate.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare performance ratios ($r_b, r_m$) vs. QAOA depth ($p$) for both order-2 and order-4 models across different channel types (e.g., Fig. 7 compares $N=12$ transfer).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem, derives the necessary Hamiltonian formulation, details the offline angle dictionary construction using infinite-size limits ($V_p$), and validates this approach by testing its robustness across multiple complex channel models via numerical simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reconfigurable intelligent surfaces (RIS) maximize received power by setting per-element phases. Discrete-phase optimization is NP-hard in the worst case, while the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) applied to RIS faces limited phase alphabets, either per-problem angle optimization or uncharacterized training cost exposed to barren plateaus, and no scalable performance benchmark. We introduce a $2^{M}$-phase $θ$ dictionary for optimizing power $\|\mathbf{A} \, e^{jθ}\|^{2}$ having $K \times N$ channel matrix $\mathbf{A}$ and QAOA angle offline optimization with instance and size-independent infinite-size limit of the mixed-$q$ Gaussian ensemble of Basso et al. Our design bounds the spin-Hamiltonian interaction order to at most quartic for any $M$, and the deployed order-2 reduction lies below the even-$q\!\ge\!4$ regime in which constant-level QAOA limitations are proved. We perform analytical, state-vector, matrix-product-state and Pauli-path-simulation numerical studies for $N=K \leq 100$ and QAOA depth $p=9$, verifying offline angle transfer to Rayleigh, Rician/line-of-sight, cascaded double-fading and spatially-correlated RIS channels at $N\!\in\!\{5,12\}$. We observe performance reaching a near-optimal multi-start single-flip local-search reference for $N\!\le\!16$ under order-2 modeling with $2^{5}{=}32$-phase dictionary while the order-4 model shows a performance ceiling below the classical reference. The approach suggests a route to near-optimal large-$N$ performance on future fault-tolerant (FTQ) quantum computers, which enable the higher-depth QAOA circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a novel, tractable measure called the Bipartite Nonlocal Magic Resource (BNMR) to quantify irreducible nonstabilizerness in bipartite entanglement. By mapping states to a minimal spectral core, the authors prove that BNMR depends only on the Schmidt spectrum, leading to predictions about its distribution for random states and revealing deep structural relationships between global and local quantum resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful new diagnostic tool for quantifying nonlocal quantum correlations beyond standard entanglement measures. The spectral link established between BNMR and the Schmidt spectrum offers a highly structured theoretical framework applicable across various quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is evaluating the Bipartite Nonlocal Magic Resource (BNMR), a measure of irreducible nonstabilizerness in bipartite entanglement, which is computationally intractable due to full Hilbert space optimization.

**Main result.** The BNMR is shown to be an intrinsic function solely of the nonzero Schmidt spectrum, leading to sharp localization at symmetric bipartitions for random states and revealing a hierarchy collapse in GHZ states where global and local resources coincide.

**Method.** The authors introduce a canonical encoding framework that reduces the state's complexity by mapping it to a minimal core defined by its Schmidt spectrum. They then derive the quadratic response of BNMR under spectral perturbations around zero.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on arbitrary pure bipartite quantum states $|\Psiangle_{AB}$ in a many-body system, particularly examining Haar-random states and generalized GHZ states across various bipartitions.

**Key observables.** Bipartite Nonlocal Magic Resource (BNMR), Schmidt spectrum ($\lambda$), Global Nonlocal Magic Resource ($M_{	ext{NL}}$), and the quadratic response of BNMR to spectral perturbations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Schmidt rank ($r$), system dimensions ($d=2$), bipartition asymmetry ($\gamma = r/R$), and perturbation magnitude ($\|\eta\|^2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that BNMR is an intrinsic, compact measure invariant under local stabilizer operations, allowing for dimension reduction to a minimal encoding core.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the canonical encoding process, showing how complex states reduce to a minimal core. They also compare the calculated quadratic response of BNMR for Haar-random states against theoretical bounds and show the end-to-end encoding structure for GHZ states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the intractability of BNMR, then introducing the canonical encoding to establish its spectral dependence (Proposition 1). It derives the perturbative scaling law (Proposition 2), applies this to random and specific states (Haar-random, rank-2), and concludes by analyzing special cases like GHZ states to show resource collapse.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bipartite nonlocal magic resource (BNMR) quantifies the irreducible nonstabilizerness residing in bipartite entanglement, yet its evaluation is intractable due to the full Hilbert space optimization. Here, we introduce a canonical encoding framework that exactly confines the BNMR of an arbitrary bipartite pure state within a minimal encoding core. This dimension reduction proves that pure-state BNMR is an intrinsic function of the nonzero Schmidt spectrum, extending its invariance from local unitary transformations to local isometries. Leveraging this spectral link, we derive the leading quadratic response of BNMR under spectral perturbations around its zeros. We apply this quadratic response to Haar-random states, deriving and numerically validating the BNMR profile: its distribution is sharply localized at the symmetric bipartition and exponentially suppressed toward asymmetric cuts, in stark contrast to the broadening Page curve of entanglement. Finally, we provide a closed-form expression for the BNMR of Schmidt rank-2 states, uncovering a hierarchy collapse in generalized GHZ states where bipartite and global nonlocal magic resources coincide exactly.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details the engineering and experimental implementation of a state-of-the-art pulsed EPR spectrometer utilizing superconducting microstrip resonators. By integrating advanced pulse sequences like RDSE, the system achieves high stability and sensitivity necessary for measuring distances in multiple, small, biologically relevant samples simultaneously.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum optics/AMO physics (pulsed spectroscopy, cavity QED concepts applied to resonators) with biophysics. The use of superconducting circuits to enhance sensitivity for molecular measurements is highly relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a high-sensitivity, stable, and automated Electron Paramagnetic Resonance (EPR) system capable of measuring distance distributions in biologically relevant samples using superconducting resonators.

**Main result.** The novel superconducting resonator design enables multi-sample measurement cartridges while achieving Signal-to-Noise Ratios (SNR) comparable to higher frequency Q-band systems, pushing biophysical EPR into more practical settings.

**Method.** The authors employed advanced pulsed EPR techniques like Double Electron-Electron Resonance (DEER), utilizing sophisticated pulse sequences such as RDSE and CPMG echo trains for signal enhancement.

**Model / system.** The core platform is a custom X-band pulsed EPR spectrometer built around a patterned thin film planar superconducting microstrip resonator. This system operates in a cryogen-free environment, allowing precise control over microwave fields to probe spin systems.

**Key observables.** Double electron-electron resonance (DEER) distances between paramagnetic centers, Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), and Rabi pulse strengths ($\pi/2$ Gaussian pulses).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating Frequency: 9.5 GHz; Sample Volume: $3.5 \mu	ext{L}$; Superconducting material enabling operation at 50 K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis sometimes neglects complex subspace structure associated with finite temperature spin ensembles (Tavis-Cummings model).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the resonator geometry, system characterization via VNA measurements, and distance measurement results comparing various advanced pulse sequences (RDSE vs. 4p-DEER) for T4-Lysozyme.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the engineering innovations of the superconducting resonator setup, presents methods for automated multi-sample acquisition, validates performance using DEER on protein samples, and discusses achieving high SNR through advanced pulse sequences.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present innovations that enable the use of superconducting resonators for high sensitivity, high bandwidth pulsed electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) measurements on biologically relevant samples with enhanced stability and throughput. A custom-built X-band pulsed EPR spectrometer with AWG and digital IF capability generated by an FPGA was used to control a novel patterned thin film planar superconducting microstrip resonator capable of generating Rabi fields sufficient to achieve 6 ns pi/2 Gaussian pulses using a 100 W solid-state HPA. The system allows automated sequential calibration, measurement, and analysis of five 3.5 uL samples contained in a sample cartridge. Performance was validated through measurements of double electron-electron resonance (DEER) distances in a variety of spin-labeled protein samples with biologically relevant concentrations, including measurements below 10 uM. The results enable broadening the scope of applications for both superconducting resonators and the use of EPR in biotechnology.

</details>

**Authors:** Murod Mirzhalilov, Jacob Freyermuth  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.23831v1>

**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `exotic spin models in light-matter` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `spintronics-quantum-optics interface` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5**
