**Summary.** This work analyzes domain wall motion in ferrimagnets driven by oscillating magnetic fields. By deriving collective-coordinate equations that explicitly include inertial coupling terms, the authors predict a frequency-controlled rectification of the domain-wall drift velocity. This mechanism offers a novel way to control magnetization dynamics using time-varying fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the derivation of generalized inertial terms (Coriolis/centrifugal) from a Lagrangian formalism is highly relevant to understanding non-linear dynamics in quantum systems, such as those modeled by effective Hamiltonians for trapped ions or superconducting circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how domain walls move within ferrimagnetic materials when subjected to a linearly oscillating in-plane magnetic field.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a rectified, finite average domain-wall velocity proportional to the drive frequency at low frequencies, which is suppressed above a critical cutoff frequency.

**Method.** The authors derived collective-coordinate equations of motion from an effective Lagrangian, incorporating inertial terms like Coriolis and centrifugal forces, and confirmed results via micromagnetic simulations.

**Model / system.** The model describes ferrimagnetic domain walls in two coupled sublattices ($\mathbf{M}_i$), whose dynamics are governed by LLG-like equations projected onto collective coordinates $\{X(t), \Phi(t)\}$.

**Key observables.** Average domain-wall velocity ($\langle\dot{X}angle$), wall angle ($\Phi$), and position ($X$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Oscillation frequency ($\omega$), net spin density ($\delta s$), and the static out-of-plane bias field ($H_z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a rigid collective-coordinate ansatz for the domain wall, and approximations are made regarding terms smaller than $M_s H / A_0$ away from compensation.

**Figures summary.** Figures confirm that the DW velocity shows a linear dependence on frequency below a cutoff and is suppressed above it; the drift velocity is shown to be proportional to the net spin density ($\delta s$).

**Paper structure.** The paper derives effective equations of motion by projecting the full LLG dynamics onto collective coordinates, analyzes the resulting inertial couplings (Coriolis, Euler), and then applies this framework to an oscillating field case to derive the frequency-dependent drift velocity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate ferrimagnetic domain-wall dynamics under linearly oscillating in-plane magnetic fields. Starting from a ferrimagnetic Lagrangian that retains the field-dependent contribution to the kinetic energy, we derive collective-coordinate equations for the domain-wall position and azimuthal angle. This formulation makes explicit the field-induced inertial terms, including Coriolis-, Euler-, and centrifugal-like contributions, and clarifies how they couple translational and azimuthal dynamics. For an oscillating in-plane field supplemented by a weak static out-of-plane bias field, we show that periodic switching of the wall angle can be rectified into a finite average domain-wall velocity. At low drive frequencies, where the wall angle follows the oscillating field, the induced drift is proportional to the drive frequency rather than the field amplitude, while above a cutoff frequency the wall angle can no longer follow the drive and the average motion is suppressed. Micromagnetic simulations confirm the analytical predictions and demonstrate that the magnitude and direction of the drift can be tuned by the net spin density. These results identify linearly oscillating fields as a mechanism for frequency-controlled domain-wall motion in ferrimagnets and highlight the role of inertial spin dynamics near magnetic compensation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the challenge of achieving symmetric mass generation for chiral fermions on a lattice by modifying the standard model. It introduces a Hubbard interaction term that renders the gapping mechanism perturbatively relevant. Through theoretical analysis and DMRG simulations, the authors confirm that this setup successfully opens an excitation gap without breaking any underlying symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical realization of interacting topological phases in 1D systems, using techniques (bosonization, DMRG) highly relevant to strongly correlated electron physics and quantum impurity models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses how symmetric mass generation (SMG)—opening a fermion gap without spontaneous symmetry breaking—can be achieved in a minimal 1D lattice model.

**Main result.** By combining a modified chiral fermionic model with a Hubbard-type interaction, the authors demonstrate that SMG occurs when the effective Luttinger parameter $K$ is sufficiently small ($K < 2/5$).

**Method.** The analysis combines bosonization techniques to analyze low-energy continuum limits and Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) calculations for numerical verification.

**Model / system.** The system involves interacting chiral fermions described by the anomaly-free 3–4–5–0 model on a tangent-fermion lattice. The Hamiltonian is modified by adding a Hubbard-type density-density interaction to make the gapping term relevant.

**Key observables.** Opening of an excitation gap ($\Delta$) while maintaining a non-degenerate ground state, and the smoothing of power-law singularities in the momentum occupation factor $n_\alpha(k)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Luttinger parameter $K$, which must satisfy $K < 2/5$ for the interaction to become relevant.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that tuning the Hubbard interaction can make the gapping perturbation visible at accessible system sizes, and it utilizes tangent fermions to avoid fermion doubling issues.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the chiral fermion dispersion relation, compare gap opening signatures (SMG vs. SSB), and show DMRG results demonstrating the smoothing of Luttinger-liquid singularities in the gapped regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reformulates the model to address lattice artifacts (doubling) and interaction irrelevance; it then uses bosonization to analyze scaling dimensions, showing how the Hubbard term renormalizes the coupling; finally, DMRG calculations confirm the gap opening signature characteristic of SMG.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Symmetric mass generation is the interaction-induced opening of a fermion gap without spontaneous symmetry breaking. The anomaly-free 3-4-5-0 model of Wang and Wen provides a minimal one-dimensional setting for this phenomenon, but a direct lattice realization faces two obstacles: fermion doubling for local chiral discretizations and perturbative irrelevance of the six-fermion gapping interaction. We address both obstacles. First, we formulate the model on a strictly one-dimensional tangent-fermion lattice, where a nonlocal hopping produces a single chiral branch without a mirror partner while retaining an efficient tensor-network representation. Second, we add a Hubbard-type density-density interaction (Luttinger parameter $K$) that reduces the scaling dimension of the 3-4-5-0 interaction from $5$ to $5K$, making it relevant for $K<2/5$. Density-matrix renormalization group calculations show the opening of an excitation gap in this regime without the appearance of a degenerate ground state, the hallmark of symmetric mass generation.

</details>

### [The impact of population heterogeneity on the redundancy principle](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24411v1)

**Authors:** Sergei Fedotov, Daniel Fears, Daniel Han  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24411v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

The lines show the analytical results and the histogram shows Monte Carlo simulations. Inset: mode shortest arrival time 𝑡∗as a function of population size 𝑁for both populations.</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes how population heterogeneity enhances transport efficiency in processes governed by redundancy principles. By modeling random walkers with rates drawn from statistical distributions, the authors show that this inherent variability leads to 'ensemble self-reinforcement.' This effect drastically shortens the characteristic time scales (FPTs) compared to idealized homogeneous systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biology, the mathematical framework—analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics, superdiffusion, and parameter averaging over distributions—is highly relevant to open quantum systems where environmental noise or system parameters are stochastic.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how population heterogeneity affects the redundancy principle, specifically analyzing first passage times (FPTs) in biological search processes.

**Main result.** Population heterogeneity leads to ensemble self-reinforcement, causing the modal and minimum FPTs to be an order of magnitude smaller compared to homogeneous ensembles with identical mean rates.

**Method.** The analysis uses master equations for random walks, employing subordination techniques and averaging over Gamma distributed random variables representing population parameters.

**Model / system.** A discrete random walk model on a semi-infinite lattice is used to simulate biological search processes (e.g., sperm movement). The system's rates ($\gamma_\pm$) are modeled as independent Gamma distributed random variables, introducing heterogeneity.

**Key observables.** Minimum FPT density ($f_{\min}(t)$), ensemble-averaged FPT density ($ar{f}_m(t)$), and the variance of the mean position $	ext{Var}(ar{X}(t))$ (showing ballistic superdiffusion).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters governing the distributions of jump probabilities ($q$) and rates ($\gamma$), particularly their means, which are compared between heterogeneous and homogeneous limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes walker parameters are drawn from fixed distributions at initialization; it simplifies the process to one spatial dimension on a semi-infinite lattice.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare FPT density ($ar{f}_m(t)$) and minimum FPT density ($f_{\min}(t)$) for heterogeneous versus homogeneous models, visually demonstrating the significant reduction in arrival time modes for the heterogeneous case.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the master equation for the random walk, calculates ensemble averages using subordination techniques, analyzes spatial self-reinforcement via recurrence relations, and finally compares the resulting FPT distributions between heterogeneous and homogeneous populations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Biological signaling is often governed by extreme value statistics, where a rapid response relies on the fastest few out of a large redundant group of searchers. While extreme first passage time (FPT) theory is well established for homogeneous ensembles, its sensitivity to population heterogeneity remains open. We show that averaging over a heterogeneous population of memoryless random walkers gives rise to ensemble self-reinforcement. This heterogeneity drastically changes both the FPT and minimum FPT densities relative to a homogeneous ensemble with identical mean rates. The modal and minimum FPTs are an order of magnitude smaller for heterogeneous populations relative to homogeneous ones. Our exact analytical predictions establish that population heterogeneity is a parameter that biology can exploit and not merely noise to be averaged away.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes cyclic denoising as a novel, physics-inspired attack to audit generative AI models for data memorization. By repeatedly cycling noise addition and removal, the authors reveal that training data is stored in ultrastable attractors within the model's latent space dynamics. This method offers a gradient-free way to probe the underlying structure of learned knowledge.

**Why it may be interesting.** The analogy between model memorization dynamics and the annealing/stability analysis in disordered solids (like spin glasses or structural glasses) provides a powerful physical framework for understanding deep learning artifacts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is understanding how specific training examples (memorized data) are encoded and recoverable from large generative diffusion models.

**Main result.** Cyclic denoising reveals that memorized content manifests as ultrastable, long-lived attractors in the model's latent space dynamics, which can be extracted without prompts or gradients.

**Method.** The authors use cyclic denoising—repeated forward noising followed by reverse denoising at controlled noise amplitudes ($\gamma$)—to treat sampling as a stroboscopic dynamical system probe.

**Model / system.** The model is an image diffusion model (e.g., Stable Diffusion v1.4, DDPM), whose latent space dynamics are analyzed. The process is mathematically analogous to perturbing and annealing disordered physical systems.

**Key observables.** Stroboscopic cosine similarity ($\cos(z_n, z_{n-1})$) over thousands of cycles; the persistence and stability of attractors after conditioning removal.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cycling amplitude $\gamma$ acts as a control parameter, tuning the perturbation strength to reveal different regimes (trivial fixed points vs. deep attractors).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that deeply memorized training data corresponds to dynamically stable, long-lived attractors within the model's generative landscape.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate trajectories in latent space showing transitions from trivial absorbing states (low $\gamma$) through semantic basin hopping (intermediate $\gamma$) to deep, persistent attractors corresponding to specific images (high $\gamma$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces cyclic denoising as a physics-inspired probe, contrasts it with existing 'generate-and-filter' attacks, demonstrates the amplitude dependence ($\gamma$ yielding transition), and confirms the ultrastability of recovered memories even when conditioning is removed.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce cyclic denoising -- repeated forward and reverse diffusion at controlled noise amplitudes -- as an extraction attack for image diffusion models. Inspired by random organization in disordered solids, cyclic denoising exposes regions of the learned distribution that are largely inaccessible to standard sampling. The dynamics drive samples toward attractors with a broad stability spectrum. The deepest attractors are ultrastable: they regenerate after near-total corruption and persist through thousands of noising-denoising cycles. Many of these attractors correspond to memorized training images, including stock photographs, brand watermarks, and web-crawl artifacts. The attack requires only sampler-level control, with no gradients, weight inspection, prompts, captions, or prior knowledge of the training data. Unlike generate-and-filter attacks, which rely on large-scale prompted generation and post-hoc similarity or membership-inference filtering, our main protocol is fully unconditioned. We demonstrate the phenomenon in Stable Diffusion v1.4 and in a pixel-space DDPM, showing consistent behavior across latent- and pixel-space diffusion models. Across noise amplitudes, we observe a yielding-like transition: low-amplitude cycling produces trivial absorbing fixed points or limit cycles, while larger amplitudes induce rearrangements, basin hopping, and long-lived trapping in structured memorized attractor basins. We also observe hierarchical partial absorption, prompt-stabilized basins, and cross-initial-condition universality of the recovered attractor set. Our results therefore show that cyclic denoising is both a physics-inspired probe of generative landscapes and a practical tool for memorization auditing, with implications for privacy, copyright compliance, and model fingerprinting.

</details>

Rescaled distributions P(s) s3/2 vs s/spred c for three parameter points (θ = 0.2; 4–12 × 104 closed cascades each) collapse onto one master curve with the pre- dicted cutoff spred c = 2/(1−beff)2. Maximum-likelihood expo- nents: τ = 1.502(8), 1.492(6), 1.498(7) (1σ).</sub>

</details>

(A) Magnetization–field loops at three drive rates; loop area equals the dissipated work per cycle (quasi-static driving hugs the reversible curve; fast driving collapses the response). (B) Period-averaged entropy-production rate versus drive rate, by two independent estimators (loop area; trajectory medium- entropy sum), rising to a bounded plateau — positive everywhere, divergent nowhere, in contrast to the queue (Fig. 1A). β = 1, τ0 = 1, h0 = 1.5 kBT.</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a statistical mechanical criterion for determining when complex adaptive systems lose their ability to recover from irreversible information processing. It models the system's failure as a structural collapse driven by sustained overload, linking computational capacity limits directly to thermodynamic constraints like entropy production and path dependence.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on information theory, the framework uses concepts of non-equilibrium thermodynamics, entropy production bounds, and phase transitions ($	ext{M/M/1}$ queueing analogies) that are central to understanding open quantum systems dynamics far from equilibrium.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes adaptive systems that couple inference (information processing) to irreversible action under sustained non-equilibrium driving, aiming to define the structural limits of 'recoverable operation.'

**Main result.** Loss of recoverability is a structural consequence of capacity saturation when informational flux exceeds integrative capacity, leading to first-order, hysteretic collapse.

**Method.** The analysis uses thermodynamic analogies and queueing theory concepts (e.g., feasibility margin, stability ratio) to characterize the system's operational regimes.

**Model / system.** The model treats information processing as a finite-capacity decoder subject to an informational load ($R_{self}$) versus an integrative capacity ($C_{self}$). The dynamics are governed by irreversible commitments that generate synthetic entropy.

**Key observables.** Feasibility Margin ($M(t)$), Stability Ratio ($	ext{SR}(t)$), Synthetic Entropy Production ($\dot{S}_{	ext{syn}}$), and the critical ratio $	ext{CR}=1$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The offspring rate ($\alpha$), the grounding fraction, and the time scale separation ratio ($\zeta = 	au_{	ext{cert}}/	au_{	ext{upd}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Irreversible commitments eliminate counterfactual options (path dependence), and the analysis is substrate-agnostic, focusing on regime behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how the stability ratio ($	ext{SR}$) diverges approaching a critical boundary ($\Gamma: 	ext{CR}=1$), and show critical slowing down of autocorrelation time near this boundary.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the thermodynamic load/capacity relationship, establishing diagnostics like $	ext{SR}(t)$, deriving structural collapse conditions (first-order transition), and finally analyzing the consequences in closed feedback loops.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study adaptive systems coupling inference to irreversible action under sustained nonequilibrium driving. Treating information processing as a thermodynamic load, we model them as finite-capacity decoders whose irreversible commitments eliminate counterfactual options, and characterize recoverable operation by a feasibility margin and a stability diagnostic fixing when irreversible action is admissible. Under sustained overload -- induced flux exceeding effective integrative capacity -- loss of recoverability and divergence of the diagnostic arise as structural consequences of capacity saturation, independent of optimization objective, control policy, or substrate. Added capacity alone does not restore recoverability: absent certification or gating, higher throughput accelerates non-recoverable loss, with high-throughput AI a concrete application. Making the feedback explicit -- each uncertified commitment spawning on average alpha new candidates -- turns the continuous transition first-order: lucid and collapsed states coexist in a cusp-organized bistable region with closed-form spinodals, collapse pre-empts the continuous divergence at finite stability ratio, recovery is hysteretic, and for alpha >= 1 load reduction alone cannot restore operation. Cascade sizes are bounded by the grounded fraction of input: a genealogy-times-congestion factorization sets a cutoff that grows as grounding shrinks, with the mean-field exponent tau = 3/2 recovered away from the boundary and each cascade carrying a Landauer-priced burst of synthetic entropy; event-driven simulations confirm the cutoff law and phase structure. This supplies the statistical mechanics of the metastable failures seen in distributed systems. The analysis is constraint-based and substrate-agnostic, establishing recoverable dissipation as a necessary criterion for decoder stability in high-flux regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously investigates the prevalence of non-Markovian dynamics by testing numerous mathematical witnesses across randomly generated two-step quantum processes. It concludes that memory effects are the generic feature of open quantum evolution, while also developing tools to distinguish between genuine quantum memory and classical forms of information backflow.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantitative measure for how often the simple Markov approximation fails in realistic physical scenarios, which is crucial for designing accurate open system models in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates how frequently quantum dynamics exhibit memory effects (non-Markovianity) compared to simple, history-independent Markovian evolution.

**Main result.** For randomly generated two-step qubit dynamics, the vast majority of processes are non-Markovian, suggesting that memory effects are the generic case in open quantum systems.

**Method.** The authors analyze the space of two-step quantum maps by testing multiple mathematical criteria (e.g., CP-indivisibility, trace distance increase) to detect deviations from Markovian behavior.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on open quantum dynamics for qubits, modeled as a sequence of two time steps ($D=(E_1, E_2)$). The analysis compares these discrete maps against the continuous-time Lindblad/GKSL master equation framework.

**Key observables.** Non-Markovianity witnesses include CP-indivisibility, violation of Data Processing Inequality (DPI), increase in Bloch volume ($V_{	ext{Bloch}}$), and entanglement measures ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The maximal Kraus rank ($k_{	ext{max}}$) is a key parameter used to restrict the space of dynamics being analyzed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on sampling random two-step dynamics or restricting the channels to specific forms, such as mixed-unitary (MU) maps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the relationships between various definitions and witnesses for non-Markovianity, and show how the fraction of quantum memory depends on the maximal Kraus rank ($k_{	ext{max}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general prevalence of non-Markovianity using random sampling across multiple criteria. It then refines this by examining specific subclasses (like mixed-unitary dynamics) and distinguishing between quantum memory, genuine backflow, and classical memory effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A profound understanding of decoherence and dissipation in quantum dynamics is crucial for the realistic modeling of the evolution of quantum systems. In open quantum dynamics one distinguishes between a memoryless, so-called Markovian evolution and dynamics incorporating memory effects, termed non-Markovian. In this work we study how prevalent memory effects are in the set of all such dynamics. We thus investigate how often a Markovian description is applicable. This question is approached by investigating randomly generated two-step qubit dynamics with respect to different concepts and witnesses of non-Markovianity. We observe that almost all dynamics are non-Markovian, and only a small (yet finite) fraction is Markovian. Furthermore, we study how this proportion changes when considering certain subclasses such as lower rank or mixed-unitary dynamics. Importantly, our results shed light on the relative ratios of -- and interrelations between -- the sets of dynamics that are non-Markovian with respect to different criteria. Finally, we investigate the fraction of dynamics in which the memory effects are necessarily of quantum nature and establish a connection between two recently developed concepts that characterize the quantumness of memory in non-Markovian dynamics.

</details>

### [Interaction-Enhanced Ergotropy in Phase-Driven Andreev Bound State Quantum Batteries](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24456v1)

**Authors:** Disha Verma, B. Vigneshwar, R. Sankaranarayanan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24456v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

**Summary.** The paper models an interacting Andreev Bound State system as a quantum battery whose stored energy is quantified by ergotropy. By analyzing the charging process driven by superconducting phase ramps, the authors demonstrate that inter-unit interactions profoundly modify energy storage. Crucially, they show that optimal energy extraction requires stopping the cycle at a specific intermediate phase rather than completing the full Josephson period.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects fundamental concepts of superconducting junctions (ABS) with non-equilibrium thermodynamics (ergotropy), providing a concrete theoretical model for controlling quantum energy harvesting using purely electromagnetic/phase control, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how superconducting phase dynamics and interaction-induced hybridization can be harnessed to create controllable quantum energy storage in Andreev Bound State (ABS) systems.

**Main result.** Interaction significantly enhances the extractable work (ergotropy), revealing that optimal charging requires interrupting the Josephson cycle at a specific, interaction-dependent phase rather than completing the full cycle.

**Method.** The research uses theoretical modeling to calculate ergotropy, quantifying extractable work by comparing the system's state against the instantaneous eigenbasis of the battery Hamiltonian during time evolution.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a minimal quantum battery composed of two interacting ABS units realized in superconducting normal-superconductor-normal (SNS) junctions. The dynamics are governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian incorporating both phase-driven terms and an interaction term ($H(t) = H_{ABS}(t) + H_{int}(J)$).

**Key observables.** Ergotropy ($W$), which measures extractable work; the charging dynamics $W(\phi)$; and the comparison between optimal partial-cycle ergotropy ($W_{opt}$) and final-cycle ergotropy ($W_{final}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting phase ($\phi$); Junction transparency ($T$, especially high-transparency limit $T=0.98$); Interaction strength ($J$); and the ramp speed ($v$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** A two-level truncation is used for dynamics restricted to the subgap sector, and the analysis treats $J$ as an effective phenomenological parameter.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase evolution of ergotropy ($W(\phi)$) showing oscillations due to interaction; comparisons between optimal vs. final work show divergence at strong coupling; and heatmaps visualize phase-selective charging windows dependent on interaction strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system by first defining the Hamiltonian and ergotropy measure, then simulating dynamics across varying interaction strengths ($J$), followed by a detailed phase-resolved analysis comparing partial versus full Josephson cycle extraction protocols.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a phase-driven quantum battery composed of two interacting Andreev bound state (ABS) units, providing a minimal superconducting platform for coherent energy storage. By analyzing the ergotropy dynamics under a superconducting phase ramp, we show that the interplay between avoided-crossing excitation and interaction-induced hybridization strongly modifies the charging process. In the high-transparency regime relevant for graphene SNS junctions, the interaction enhances the stored extractable work and generates pronounced oscillatory charging dynamics associated with coherent redistribution between coupled ABS sectors. The phase-resolved evolution further reveals optimal charging windows during the Josephson cycle, indicating the possibility of phase-programmable energy extraction through partial-cycle operation. Overall, our results identify interaction-assisted avoided-crossing dynamics as a microscopic mechanism for controllable energy storage in superconducting quantum batteries.

</details>

**Summary.** The research uses STM/STS on moiré graphene to study Chern insulators under magnetic fields. The key findings are the direct visualization of symmetry breaking in these states, leading to reconstructed unit cells at fractional fillings. Furthermore, the authors observe quantum melting and phase separation transitions driven by topological defects, providing deep insight into strongly correlated electron behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides direct experimental evidence of complex many-body phenomena—such as charge density wave formation, topological defect dynamics, and quantum melting—in a tunable solid-state platform, offering insights into strongly correlated electron physics beyond simple band theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the interplay between band topology (Chern insulators) and strong electron-electron interactions in moiré quantum materials, focusing on visualizing symmetry breaking and quantum melting phenomena.

**Main result.** The authors directly visualize Symmetry Broken Chern Insulators (SBCIs) that reconstruct the moiré unit cell at fractional fillings. They also observe evidence for quantum melting via topological defect proliferation and phase separation transitions.

**Method.** Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) and Spectroscopy (STS) are used to image electronic states, complemented by analyzing the spatial structure factor $S(\mathbf{q}|\mathbf{r})$ of the tunneling current.

**Model / system.** The physical system is bilayer graphene aligned with hexagonal BN ($	ext{h-BN}$), forming a moiré superlattice. The physics is governed by Hofstadter's spectrum, which describes electronic states under combined magnetic fields and lattice periodicity.

**Key observables.** Incompressible gaps in STS spectra; spatial modulation of tunneling current $\delta|I_{dc}|$; emergent lattice structures (Kagome, stripe); density of topological defects (dislocations).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field ($B$) and flux ratio ($\phi/\phi_!$), gate voltage ($V_G$), and filling factor $s$. Measurements are performed at low temperatures ($T_{eff} = 210 	ext{ mK}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping observed gaps and band structures to topological indices $(C, s)$ derived from the Wannier diagram formula.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail direct imaging of CI states showing emergent lattice reconstructions (e.g., Kagome for $(-4, 1)$), phase maps illustrating defect proliferation during quantum melting for stripe phases, and diagrams showing phase separation boundaries.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using Wannier diagrams to mapping out various $(C, s)$ topological states. It then moves into detailed real-space imaging to confirm symmetry breaking (reconstruction) and finally analyzes dynamic transitions like quantum melting and phase separation as doping changes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the presence of a magnetic field, electronic states of moiré quantum materials develop a Hofstadter spectrum that provides a unique setting for studying the interplay between band topology and strong electron-electron interaction. Using scanning tunneling microscopy, we study Hofstadter's states in bilayer graphene aligned with hexagonal BN and directly visualize the formation of interaction-driven symmetry breaking Chern insulators. Our measurements reveal the formation of phases that double, triple or quadruple the moiré unit cell at fractional filling of the Hofstadter bands, as well as states with complex intra-unit-cell wave functions. We visualize two distinct quantum phenomena in different Chern states, including quantum melting driven by the appearance and proliferation of topological defects, and a quantum transition co-occurring with phase competition and separation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the topological phase diagram of a non-Hermitian lattice model by studying its exceptional points (EPs) under periodic versus open boundary conditions. It demonstrates that long-range hopping terms significantly alter the EP structure in OBC compared to PBC, while confirming the existence and characterization of topologically protected edge states using winding numbers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-Hermitian physics, a field highly relevant to open quantum systems, wave propagation in lossy media, and engineered photonic/electronic structures where gain/loss are inherent.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the exceptional point (EP) structure and topological properties of non-Hermitian lattice models, specifically focusing on how long-range hopping terms modify these features under different boundary conditions.

**Main result.** The model exhibits second-order EPs whose dependence on next-nearest-neighbor hopping ($m$) differs significantly between periodic and open boundary conditions; furthermore, topological edge states are confirmed via winding number analysis in OBC.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis combining numerical computation (condition number, Jordan decomposition) with analytical tools like the winding number framework and M-Riemann algebra formalism to map out parameter space degeneracies.

**Model / system.** A generalized non-Hermitian Rice-Mele model described by a 1D tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating intra-cell hopping ($t$), inter-cell hopping ($t'$), next-nearest-neighbor hopping ($m$), and gain/loss terms ($\pm i\gamma$).

**Key observables.** Exceptional Points (EPs), topological invariants (winding number $W_j$), edge state eigenvalues, and the Directional Inverse Participation Ratio (dIPR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping ratios ($t/t'$, $m/\gamma$), boundary conditions (PBC vs. OBC), and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on second-order EPs, and specific symmetries are assumed or broken to simplify the topological classification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate EP structures as lines/ellipses in parameter space for both PBC and OBC; phase diagrams map out edge state regimes using winding numbers and dIPR values.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian, first establishing bulk properties under PBC (independence of $m$), then contrasting this with OBC where $m$ actively generates new EPs. Finally, it uses topological invariants and localization measures to confirm boundary-induced edge states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exceptional points are degeneracies unique to non-Hermitian systems, where eigenvalues and eigenvectors coalesce, rendering the Hamiltonian defective. We investigate the exceptional-point structure and topological properties of a generalized non-Hermitian Rice-Mele model with balanced gain and loss, as well as next-nearest-neighbor hopping. The system hosts only second-order exceptional points under both periodic and open boundary conditions. Under periodic boundary conditions, the exceptional points in parameter space lie on lines and ellipses that are independent of the next-nearest-neighbor hopping, since the latter enters the bulk Hamiltonian only as an identity contribution. Under open boundary conditions, this independence is broken: the next-nearest-neighbor hopping not only shifts the energy of existing exceptional points but also generates new ones, with a specific condition signaling a topological gap closing observed only in the open-boundary spectrum. At special parameter points, multiple simultaneous second-order exceptional points yield degenerate configurations whose degeneracy grows with system size. Exceptional point locations are identified numerically via the condition number of the eigenvector matrix and confirmed by Jordan decomposition. The topological phase diagram, computed via a winding number framework for non-Hermitian systems without symmetry protection, reveals sectors with zero, one, and two edge states; the bulk-boundary correspondence is confirmed, and the non-Hermitian skin effect is absent.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies advanced causal inference techniques to molecular dynamics simulations of protein folding. By quantifying directional information flow between Principal Components, the authors demonstrate that collective motions exhibit a directed causal structure, suggesting that slow structural fluctuations dictate subsequent faster movements within the protein's conformational landscape.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biomolecules, the methodology—using information-theoretic measures like IG to infer directed relationships in high-dimensional time series—is a powerful tool applicable to analyzing complex non-equilibrium dynamics in many-body systems beyond proteins.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether different Principal Components (PCs) derived from protein dynamics simulations causally influence each other over time, moving beyond simple correlation measures.

**Main result.** The causal inference approach reveals directed information flow ($	ext{PC1} 	o \{	ext{PC2}, 	ext{PC3}\}$) in both ubiquitin and NTL9, suggesting that the slowest collective mode drives subsequent faster fluctuations. This directional structure is not captured by conventional covariance-based analyses.

**Method.** The core method employs a causal-discovery framework based on Information Imbalance (IG), which quantifies predictive asymmetry between time series derived from MD simulations.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the dynamics of proteins, specifically ubiquitin and NTL9, simulated using Molecular Dynamics (MD) trajectories. The analysis reduces the high-dimensional conformational space into a set of collective variables represented by Principal Components (PCs).

**Key observables.** Pairwise Integrated Information Transfer (IGT), Brüschweiler collectivity index ($\kappa_k$), and time-lagged covariance matrices $\Gamma(	au)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time lags ($	au$) up to the microsecond scale, and the magnitude of the Imbalance Gain ($	ext{IG}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The inferred causal links are putative; they suggest necessary but not sufficient conditions for true causality due to potential unobserved confounders.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate autocorrelation functions showing decorrelation timescales, pairwise IG curves demonstrating directional asymmetry (e.g., $	ext{PC1} 	o 	ext{PC2}$), and resulting causal graphs mapping the strongest information flow paths.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces PCA/TICA limitations, details the application of the Information Imbalance framework to calculate directed couplings between PCs for two proteins (Ubiquitin and NTL9), presents results showing directional influence from slow modes to fast modes, and concludes by discussing implications for understanding protein dynamics organization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Principal component analysis (PCA) is widely used to characterize collective protein motions from molecular dynamics (MD) simulations. While PCA identifies the dominant modes of structural fluctuation, it does not reveal whether different principal components (PCs) causally influence each other. Here, we investigate this question using a recently introduced causal-discovery framework [Del Tatto et al, PNAS 2024], which allows to infer putative causal asymmetries between high-dimensional time series. We apply this approach to long-timescale MD trajectories of two proteins. By analyzing relationships among PCs, we construct directed networks describing how PCs influence one another across time scales. These directional relationships, whose existence is a necessary condition for the presence of a causal link, are not captured by conventional covariance-based analyses and provide information that is complementary to PCA and Time-lagged Independent Component Analysis (TICA). Our results suggest that our causal inference approach can uncover previously hidden aspects of the dynamical organization of protein motions and offer a new perspective on this very popular class of collective variables.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a microscopic framework to calculate the $\omega$-effect—a signature of CPT violation in neutral mesons—by modeling the environment as a multimode squeezed graviton bath. It replaces older phenomenological models with calculable anomalous correlators derived from the squeezing kernel. The result shows that this effect is a generic consequence of non-classical gravitational environments interacting coherently with particle decay processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, calculable mechanism for generating decoherence/CPT violation signatures ($\omega$-effect) in particle physics systems by linking it directly to measurable quantum features (squeezing) of gravitational fields, bridging high-energy theory with quantum optics concepts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the $\omega$-effect, a phenomenon that probes CPT violation induced by interactions with inaccessible quantum-gravitational degrees of freedom in entangled neutral-meson systems.

**Main result.** A non-zero $\omega$ parameter is predicted, quantifying an exchange-symmetric admixture in the meson state. This effect requires both flavor-changing weak interactions and the presence of anomalous correlators characteristic of a squeezed gravitational environment.

**Method.** The authors derive this effect by performing an open-system calculation involving tracing over the quantum graviton bath, replacing previous phenomenological stochastic variables with calculable graviton correlation functions derived from the Takagi decomposition of the squeezing kernel.

**Model / system.** The system involves entangled neutral mesons (e.g., kaons) interacting with a multimode squeezed gravitational field generated by an axion cloud around a Kerr black hole, modeled using concepts from Chern-Simons gravity and supermodes.

**Key observables.** The parameter $\omega$, which measures the small exchange-symmetric admixture in the otherwise antisymmetric EPR state of the mesons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing amplitudes ($r_\alpha$), squeezing phases ($\phi_\alpha$), weak-interaction flavour-mixing matrix elements, and the energy splitting/decay rates of the kaons ($\Delta\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on treating the gravitational bath as a squeezed vacuum state (requiring anomalous correlators $\langle b_\alpha b_eta angle 
eq 0$) and utilizes approximations like the Born approximation and RWA.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the physical problem ($\omega$-effect) to deriving the necessary quantum gravitational input (squeezed graviton state via Takagi decomposition), and finally calculating the resulting meson evolution using an open-system master equation approach.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The $ω$-effect in entangled neutral-meson systems provides a sensitive probe of CPT violation induced by quantum-gravitational environments. In open quantum systems, interactions with inaccessible gravitational degrees of freedom can render the reduced meson dynamics non-unitary, causing the CPT operator to become ill-defined, even when the underlying microscopic Hamiltonian is CPT invariant. We present a microscopic derivation of the $ω$-effect arising from a multimode squeezed gravitational environment generated by an axion cloud around a Kerr black hole. Using the Takagi decomposition of the associated complex symmetric squeezing kernel, the graviton field is expressed in terms of independent squeezed supermodes possessing anomalous correlators. These correlators provide a microscopic quantum counterpart of the stochastic fluctuations that appear in earlier D-particle foam descriptions of the $ω$-effect, replacing phenomenological variances of flavour-changing D-particle recoil by calculable graviton correlation functions. After tracing over the graviton bath, the anomalous correlators and the weak-interactions-induced mixing combine to generate transitions between the antisymmetric and symmetric two-meson sectors. This results in a small exchange-symmetric admixture, parametrised by $ω$, in the otherwise antisymmetric EPR state. We obtain an explicit expression for $ω$ in terms of a sum over Takagi supermodes weighted by their squeezing amplitudes and phases together with the weak-interaction flavour-mixing matrix element. The resulting framework suggests that the $ω$-effect may be a generic signature of non-classical states of gravitational environments, extending beyond the specific axion-cloud scenario considered here. The observability of the $ω$-effect from other astrophysical and microscopic black-hole sources is discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a theoretical framework showing how injecting controlled fermionic interactions ('doping') into otherwise simple matchgate circuits can generate high levels of quantum randomness. By mapping the problem to classical Markov chains, they derive rigorous bounds on the depth needed to achieve unitary $k$-designs. This offers a controlled pathway from integrable physics toward complex quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an analytically tractable method to bridge the gap between integrable (free-fermion) quantum systems and highly random, complex dynamics required for universal quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses how structured, free-fermion dynamics (like those in matchgate circuits) can be modified by injecting non-Gaussian interactions to generate the necessary randomness for achieving unitary quantum designs.

**Main result.** They establish that doping these circuits allows for controlled formation of approximate unitary 2-designs in polylogarithmic depth, with convergence rates governed by classical Markov chain dynamics and spectral gaps.

**Method.** The analysis uses the matchgate commutant framework to map the quantum design problem onto a solvable classical birth-death Markov chain, allowing for rigorous bounding of mixing times and required gate counts.

**Model / system.** The system is based on 'doped matchgate circuits,' which start with free-fermion dynamics but are perturbed by non-Gaussian gates (fermionic interactions) to induce controlled entanglement growth towards Haar randomness.

**Key observables.** Unitary $k$-design formation, spectral gaps ($\Delta$), mixing times ($t_{	ext{mix}}$), and frame potentials ($F^{(k)}(E)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The doping depth (number of non-Gaussian gates) and the spatial structure of the doping (global vs. local/brickwork).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is most tractable in the globally scrambled regime, and the results rely on projecting dynamics onto the matchgate commutant.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic doping protocols comparing global versus localized scrambling; they also show data collapse of frame potentials for $k=2$ and $k=3$, confirming relaxation towards Haar behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the convergence rate to quantum designs by first establishing the mapping to a classical Markov chain, deriving bounds using spectral gap analysis, comparing global vs. local doping protocols, and finally providing explicit scaling laws for different design orders ($k=2, 3, 4$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Matchgate circuits realize free-fermion dynamics: they are efficiently classically simulable, yet cannot on their own generate the generic randomness required for universal computation or unitary design formation. We study a controlled route beyond this integrable limit by doping matchgate circuits with non-Gaussian gates-physically, the injection of fermionic interactions into an otherwise free system. Using the matchgate commutant framework, we obtain analytic control over unitary $2$-design formation. For globally scrambled dynamics, the design problem maps exactly onto a classical birth-death Markov chain with an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck continuum limit, recasting the emergence of quantum randomness in terms of spectral gaps and mixing times and yielding rigorous bounds on the number of non-Gaussian gates needed for approximate $2$-designs. These bounds hold for a broad class of parity-preserving non-Gaussian gates, independently of microscopic details, with numerics indicating that the same mechanism governs higher-order designs. Used as local building blocks in a glued-circuit architecture, they yield approximate parity-preserving $2$-designs in polylogarithmic depth with a sparse non-Gaussian gate count, with implications for Page-like entanglement growth and fermionic classical-shadow protocols. Finally, locality reshapes this picture: in local brickwork dynamics, design formation is diffusion-limited and far slower. Our results establish doped matchgate circuits as a controlled, analytically tractable route from free fermions to interaction-generated quantum designs.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose using the Speed-Fisher Information as a universal measure to characterize chaos by observing how a system's stationary state responds to slow external driving. They show that chaotic dynamics cause this information to diverge inversely with the driving speed, while regular dynamics result in finite or vanishing responses. This provides a powerful link between spectral properties and fundamental concepts of irreversibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, measurable diagnostic tool—the speed-Fisher information—to distinguish between regular and chaotic dynamics in quantum systems without relying solely on traditional metrics like Lyapunov exponents. This has direct implications for understanding thermalization rates in open or driven quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified diagnostic tool for characterizing chaos in both classical and quantum systems by analyzing their response to slow, non-adiabatic driving.

**Main result.** Chaos manifests as a divergence of the speed-Fisher information ($I_{ar{v}}(\mu)$) as the driving protocol time approaches zero ($\mu 	o 0$), with the degree of divergence controlled by the low-frequency spectral weight of system observables.

**Method.** The analysis centers on calculating the Speed-Fisher Information, derived from the fidelity or relative entropy change during slow cyclic perturbations. This is related to the low-frequency behavior of correlation functions via Fourier transforms.

**Model / system.** The framework applies broadly to classical and quantum Hamiltonian systems (e.g., Ising models) and extends naturally to non-Hamiltonian classical flows. The analysis uses stationary states $ho_{-\infty}$ subjected to time-dependent perturbations $H(t)$.

**Key observables.** Speed-Fisher Information ($I_{ar{v}}(\mu)$), Relative Entropy ($	ext{D}_{	ext{KL}}$), and the low-frequency spectral weight of correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The driving speed $\mu$, which is taken to zero in the quasi-static limit; parameters determining system integrability (e.g., coupling constants $J, A$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations relating entropy change to variance ($\langle \Delta K_0 angle \approx rac{1}{2} 	ext{Var}(\Delta K_0)$) and assumptions about the decay rates of spectral weights in different dynamical regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of thermodynamic drag (or $I_{ar{v}}(\mu)$) vs. $\mu$ for both regular (finite/saturating) and chaotic (diverging) quantum systems, alongside corresponding classical spectral functions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the diagnostic tool ($I_{ar{v}}$), derives its connection to thermodynamics ($	ext{D}_{	ext{KL}}$), applies it across various physical models (quantum/classical/non-Hamiltonian), and systematically analyzes scaling laws based on the low-frequency spectral behavior of observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a unified perspective on classical and quantum chaos based on the stability of a system's stationary states under slow driving. We probe this sensitivity via the system's susceptibility to the average protocol speed, which we call the ``speed-Fisher information," and relate it to irreversible entropy production in the system. We show that chaotic dynamics manifests as a divergence of the speed-Fisher information with the protocol time, and that this response is controlled by the perturbation's low-frequency spectral weight. This approach to chaos applies to both classical and quantum Hamiltonian systems, and naturally extends to non-Hamiltonian classical flows. We illustrate this framework with simple classical and quantum examples, along with a non-Hamiltonian flow that qualitatively exhibits analogous low-frequency spectral behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously determines the macroscopic shape adopted by self-repelling polymers under a constant external force field. It achieves this by solving an equivalent variational problem, showing that the optimal geometry is governed by a geodesic defined by a height-dependent Finsler metric. The results provide quantitative predictions for different field strengths, bridging classical physics (catenaries) with lattice constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical polymer physics, the mathematical machinery—using Finsler metrics derived from statistical mechanics potentials to determine optimal paths—is highly relevant for understanding effective Hamiltonians in complex many-body systems or quantum field theories where path integrals are involved.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study determines the typical macroscopic geometry of self-repelling polymers subjected to a constant external force field.

**Main result.** The limiting free energy is found by solving a variational problem, and the resulting macroscopic shape is characterized as a geodesic of a height-dependent Finsler metric. The analysis reveals universal limits for small (catenary) and large (polygonal) field strengths.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced techniques from the calculus of variations, comparing primal and dual variational formulations to establish the unique global minimizer that governs the system's macroscopic behavior in the thermodynamic limit.

**Model / system.** The model consists of self-repelling polymers on a 2D lattice (Z^2), encompassing models like the simple random walk and SAW. The system is subjected to an external constant force field, leading to path weights governed by interaction potentials and the external potential.

**Key observables.** Limiting free energy density ($\Psi$), rate function ($J(v)$), macroscopic shape (geodesic $\Gamma^*$), and monomer measure concentration ($m^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Force field strength ($g$), fixed length/displacement constraints, and the lattice structure ($\mathbb{Z}^2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on establishing analytic properties of the free energy function and using techniques like coarse-graining and passing to limits in both space and time parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate simulations comparing computed geodesics to classical catenaries for small fields, and show concentration histograms confirming that empirical measures converge to the deterministic variational measure.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining thermodynamic quantities (free energy, rate function) via ensemble averages. It then tackles the core problem using primal/dual variational formulations, proving equivalence between them. Finally, it establishes the limiting free energy by bounding the partition function from above and below using coarse-graining techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a general class of self-repelling polymers on $\mathbb Z^2$, including the simple random walk, the self-avoiding walk and the repulsive Domb-Joyce model, in the presence of a constant force field acting on each monomer. Conditioning the polymer to have fixed length and fixed endpoints, we identify the limiting free energy and prove that typical trajectories concentrate exponentially near a deterministic macroscopic shape. This shape is characterized as the unique minimizer of a variational problem and can be interpreted as a geodesic of a height-dependent Finsler metric. We also analyze two limiting regimes with universal features: for small field strength, in the symmetric case, the geodesic is close to a classical catenary, while for large field strength it converges to a universal polygonal shape governed by the nearest-neighbor lattice constraint.

</details>

### [Electrical-Circuit Simulation of the Uhlmann Phase](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24559v1)

**Authors:** Yu-Huan Huang, Yu Wang, Jia-Chen Tang, Xu-Yang Hou, Hao Guo  
**Type:** both · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24559v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

**Summary.** The paper develops a method to simulate the Uhlmann geometric phase—a concept describing geometry in mixed quantum states—using only classical electrical circuits. By mapping the complex quantum evolution onto an admittance matrix, the authors designed and simulated an active RC network that accurately reproduces the phase's topological transition at a critical purity value.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges abstract concepts from quantum information theory (geometric phases in mixed states) with tangible, classical experimental hardware (RC circuits), providing a novel platform for testing fundamental physics principles outside traditional quantum optics setups.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to find a simple, accessible physical platform—specifically classical electrical circuits—to simulate the complex dynamics associated with the Uhlmann geometric phase for mixed quantum states.

**Main result.** The authors successfully mapped the abstract Uhlmann evolution onto an active RC circuit network, demonstrating that this classical system faithfully reproduces both the local dynamics and the global topological transition of the mixed-state geometric phase.

**Method.** The theoretical framework involved reformulating the Uhlmann parallel-transport condition into a linear differential equation, which was then transformed into a time-independent, real-valued dynamical matrix suitable for circuit implementation.

**Model / system.** The system models mixed quantum states governed by density matrices ($ho$) and their evolution under the Uhlmann condition. The physical realization uses an active RC network built from multi-input inverting integrators and resistors to emulate the required linear dynamics.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the Uhlmann geometric phase ($\Phi_U$), calculated via the argument of the normalized overlap ($I$) between initial and final states. The magnitude $|I|$ also shows a critical dip.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The purity parameter $r$ controls the dynamics, with a critical transition occurring at $r_c = \sqrt{3}/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that the complex Uhlmann dynamics can be recast into a real-valued system via rotating-frame transformations and subsequent decomposition suitable for analog circuit implementation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare LTspice simulations (blue solid) against exact theoretical solutions (red dashed), showing how the measured phase $\Phi_U$ jumps from zero to $\pi$ at the critical purity $r_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first reformulating the abstract quantum dynamics into a linear matrix form, then applying transformations (rotating frame, real decomposition) to yield a time-independent generator ($M_8$), and finally detailing the mapping of this matrix onto specific components of an active RC circuit for simulation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Uhlmann phase extends the concept of geometric phases to mixed quantum states through a parallel-transport condition on purification amplitudes, but its experimental realization has so far required sophisticated quantum platforms with carefully engineered auxiliary degrees of freedom. In this work, we reformulate the Uhlmann parallel-transport condition as a linear matrix differential equation and vectorize it to obtain an effective dynamical generator. This generator can be directly mapped onto the admittance matrix of a classical RC circuit, thereby translating the Uhlmann dynamics into the evolution of circuit node voltages. We illustrate the mapping using the equatorial-loop model and, via a rotating-frame transformation followed by a real decomposition, derive a time-independent, real-valued dynamical system suitable for analog implementation. LTspice simulations of the resulting active RC network faithfully reproduce the Uhlmann geometric phase and its topological transition at the critical purity, demonstrating that classical electrical circuits offer a simple and accessible platform for probing mixed-state geometric phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel optical technique using Surface Force Balance interferometry to map composition and track interfaces within nanoconfined liquids. By analyzing refractive index profiles during controlled evaporation, the authors successfully characterize meniscus geometry changes as the system transitions from surface tension control to strong confinement effects. This method provides a powerful tool for quantifying interfacial thermodynamics at the nanoscale.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily experimental, the quantitative analysis of phase transitions and crossover regimes under extreme confinement provides valuable data for developing theoretical models in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and interface physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a non-invasive method for nanoscale composition mapping and characterizing phase boundaries in nanoconfined liquids.

**Main result.** The technique successfully tracks the time evolution of meniscus geometry during liquid evaporation, revealing a crossover from surface tension-dominated to confinement-dominated regimes at small separations.

**Method.** It utilizes Surface Force Balance (SFB) combined with multiple beam interferometry analysis to reconstruct spatially resolved refractive index profiles within confined fluids.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on liquid confinement between mica surfaces, analyzing the meniscus geometry during dynamic evaporation of a heptane droplet. The method allows for composition profiling by mapping variations in the effective film thickness and refractive index.

**Key observables.** Refractive index profiles ($\mu(x)$), mean refractive index ($\mu_D$), effective film thickness ($e_{eff}$), meniscus geometry (catenoid vs. quartic fit), and evaporation rate ($	ext{-}dV/dt$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanoscale separations (down to $\approx 10$ nm height), surface tension effects, confinement-induced composition variations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting interference fringes derived from the crossed-cylinder geometry and assumes local uniformity of properties for certain model simplifications.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SFB setups, interferograms showing fringe patterns, calculated refractive index profiles ($\mu(x)$ vs. $D_0$), and time evolution plots comparing catenoid fits to observed meniscus geometries during evaporation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the methodology for optical mapping using TLI/SFB, applies it first to air calibration (detecting wetting capillaries), then uses it to track dynamic liquid evaporation, analyzing geometric crossovers and quantifying interfacial thermodynamic effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In confined space, deviations from bulk structure and properties are expected due to additional thermodynamic variables. In particular, composition variations arising from surface interactions may lead to additional phases and altered phase transitions. Here, we introduce a non-invasive method for nanoscale composition mapping in confined liquids using the surface force balance (SFB). The method extends conventional SFB analysis from apex measurements to spatially resolved reconstruction of refractive index profiles within confined fluids. When multiple phases are present, the refractive index profiles provide direct access to the position and geometry of the nanoconfined fluid interfaces. We describe the interferogram analysis in detail and establish its range of validity through two model scenarios. First, measurements in air demonstrate the precision of the method and allow detection of a nanometric wetting capillary. Second, we analyse dynamic evaporation of a confined heptane droplet down to 0.1 pL volume. The method provides a time-resolved reconstruction of the meniscus geometry throughout the evaporation process. Although evaporation continuously drives the system out of equilibrium, the meniscus remains well described by a catenoidal geometry down to heights of approximately 80 nm. At smaller separations, systematic deviations from the catenoidal profile emerge, indicating a crossover from a surface tension-dominated regime to a confinement-dominated regime. Overall, we demonstrate composition profiling as a framework to analyse confinement-induced composition variations and to quantify interfacial thermodynamic effects at the nanoscale.

</details>

**Authors:** F. Javier García de Abajo, Alejandro Manjavacas  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24723v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a quantum electrodynamics framework to calculate vacuum friction torque on rotating particles. It shows that this torque depends critically on particle symmetry and temperature, yielding distinct power-law scalings ($\Omega^7$ vs linear $\Omega$) depending on the thermal regime. The findings highlight fundamental symmetries protecting certain geometries from vacuum dissipation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics by quantifying dissipation mechanisms (vacuum friction) that couple mechanical rotation to the zero-point energy of the electromagnetic field, which is a key area in advanced AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research develops a quantum theory to calculate the rotational vacuum friction torque exerted on small, nonabsorbing particles rotating in a vacuum.

**Main result.** The calculated torque exhibits strong dependence on particle symmetry and temperature; for anisotropic particles at zero temperature, the torque scales as $\Omega^7$, while axisymmetric particles are protected from this type of photon-assisted friction.

**Method.** The calculation relies on applying Fermi's golden rule to second-order processes (two-photon emission/absorption) derived from an electric-dipole interaction Hamiltonian coupling particle excitations and rotation to the quantized electromagnetic vacuum field.

**Model / system.** The system is a small, nonabsorbing particle with internal bosonic excitations ($\epsilon_j$) undergoing rotation ($\Omega$) in a vacuum at temperature $T_0$. The analysis uses quantum electrodynamics principles applied to rotational dynamics.

**Key observables.** Rotational friction torque ($M$), absorbed power ($P_{abs}$), and the scaling of these quantities with $\Omega$ and $T_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rotation frequency ($\Omega$), temperature ($T_0$) characterized by $	heta_0$, internal excitation gap ($\epsilon_g$), and particle anisotropy/symmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The particle is nonabsorbing (real polarizability); approximations are made regarding the relative magnitudes of $\Omega, 	heta_0,$ and $\epsilon_g$ to simplify the calculation into distinct temperature regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the contributing physical processes (2PE, 2PA, PS) and plot the calculated absolute strength of rotational vacuum friction versus rotation frequency for various temperatures and particle shapes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the interaction Hamiltonian and applying Fermi's golden rule to calculate transition rates. It then derives general expressions for the torque in terms of temperature-dependent sums, simplifying these into distinct asymptotic limits ($\Omega^7$ at low $T$, linear $\Omega$ at high $T$), and finally analyzing symmetry protection.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A nonabsorbing particle rotating in vacuum can lose angular momentum only by converting mechanical energy into electromagnetic radiation. Here, we develop a quantum theory of rotational vacuum friction for small lossless particles and show that axial symmetry qualitatively changes the leading dissipation channel. At zero temperature, the frictional torque scales as $M\proptoΩ^7$ with rotation frequency $\ Omega$ in anisotropic particles due to the emission of correlated photon pairs whose frequencies sum to $2Ω$, while a contribution to the torque linear in $\ Omega$ is found at finite temperature. In contrast, axisymmetric particles are protected against photon-assisted friction regardless of temperature.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores using nanoscale structural imperfections, specifically dislocation networks in STO thin films, to control phonon behavior. By exciting the material with THz pulses and measuring the resulting X-ray diffraction changes, the authors show that symmetry breaking generates novel collective vibrational modes. This opens a new avenue for designing functional materials by controlling their real-space topology.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work demonstrates how engineered structural defects can act as powerful tuning knobs for lattice dynamics, offering an analogy to controlling quantum states or excitations by manipulating the underlying geometry of a solid-state system.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how controlling symmetry breaking at nanoscale interfaces, such as dislocation networks, can be used to manipulate phonon mode mixing and generate novel collective dynamics in polar materials.

**Main result.** Symmetry breaking due to dislocations induces a mixture of acoustic and optical modes, leading to measurable dynamic electric polarization and the generation of previously unobserved collective THz-frequency modes.

**Method.** The research combines ultrafast time-resolved X-ray diffraction (tr-XRD) experiments using THz pulses with classical molecular dynamics (MD) simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Strontium Titanate ($	ext{SrTiO}_3$) thin film grown on a complex substrate, engineered to contain an ordered network of interfacial misfit dislocations. Simulations model the lattice response under strain and electric fields.

**Key observables.** Transient diffraction intensity ($\Delta I/I$), out-of-plane vs. in-plane atomic displacements, dynamic electric polarization ($|P|$), and vorticity measures ($\lambda_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** THz frequency regime (e.g., 0.060 to 0.124 THz modes); nanoscale structural features like dislocation spacing; picosecond time scales.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on drawing analogies from fluid dynamics concepts (helicity, $\lambda_2$) and comparing simulation results with experimental measurements despite differences in system size or boundary conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STEM images of dislocation networks, time-resolved XRD intensity changes at Bragg/satellite reflections, simulated atomic displacement patterns comparing pristine vs. dislocated films, and FFT spectra confirming mode mixing.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental setup (tr-XRD on STO/dislocations), presenting key findings regarding symmetry breaking effects observed in diffraction data, supporting these results with detailed MD simulations, and concluding with a generalized pathway for controlling dynamical functional properties via real-space topology.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Manipulating phonons through symmetry is a fundamental approach to alter the dynamic responses of materials. Most often new phases are sought through control of the unit-cell (e.g. via strain, doping, light, etc.). By comparison, there is vast potential to look beyond the unit-cell into higher-order architectures to control wave scattering and interference effects that remains less explored. We describe the THz-induced dynamics of an SrTiO$_{3}$ thin film with a nanoscale ordered interfacial dislocation network probed with 2- and 3-dimensional time-resolved x-ray diffraction and classical molecular dynamics simulations. We find that symmetry breaking at all scales is an effective approach to create a dynamical electric polarization and to control phonon mixing that generates previously unreported collective modes in the THz regime with circular vortex-like displacements. This work opens a new pathway to explore dynamical functional properties that can be extended to magnetic, electric, and ferroelectric systems by controlling the real-space topology via epitaxy.

</details>
