**Summary.** This paper introduces the Double Quasiperiodic MAA (DMAA) model to study the transition from MAA to AA type quasiperiodic potentials. By analyzing diagnostic quantities like IPR and fractal dimension, the authors demonstrate that this evolution reveals multiple localization-delocalization transitions not present in simpler models. The findings are validated using both numerical simulations and continuum models relevant for experimental platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how the nature of localization transitions changes when moving between two closely related quasiperiodic models, which has implications for disordered quantum systems in photonics or cold atoms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to reveal how localization transitions evolve when moving from Mosaic Aubry-André (MAA) models to standard Aubry-André (AA) models.

**Main result.** The Double Quasiperiodic MAA (DMAA) model exhibits new twice and multiple localization-delocalization transitions, which are absent in the original AA model.

**Method.** Numerical calculations were performed using diagnostic quantities like Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), Normalized Participation Ratio (NPR), and Fractal Dimension ($Q$) on both tight-binding and continuum models.

**Model / system.** The Double Quasiperiodic MAA (DMAA) model is a one-dimensional chain governed by a Hamiltonian with double quasiperiodic on-site potentials. The transition between MAA ($eta=0$) and AA ($eta=1$) is controlled by the tunable amplitude factor $eta$.

**Key observables.** Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), Normalized Participation Ratio (NPR), Fractal Dimension ($Q$), and real-space wavefunction distribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Potential strength ($\lambda$), tuning parameter ($eta \in [0, 1]$), and energy ($E_n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the DMAA model providing a continuous framework to study the transition between MAA and AA models.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $Q$ vs. energy for varying $eta$, confirming Mobility Edges in MAA but not AA; other figures plot IPR/NPR changes demonstrating multiple transitions at specific parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the DMAA model, analyzes its behavior across different disorder strengths ($\lambda$) and tuning parameters ($eta$), uses various diagnostic tools (IPR, NPR, Q), and validates findings by comparing tight-binding results with continuum simulations applicable to polariton modes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Despite their potential similarity between the mosaic Aubry-André (MAA) and AA models, the MAA model allows mobility edges (MEs), whereas the AA model does not. Here we develop a new double quasiperiodic MAA (DMAA) model consisting of one primitive MAA with nonzero even-site potentials and the other modified one with both nonzero odd-site potentials and a tunable amplitude factor, to reveal how localization transitions evolve from MAA to AA models. Interplays and competitions among the extended, critical and localized states arising from superpositions of double quasi-periodic MAA potentials enable new twice and multiple localization-delocalization transitions besides the original single localization transition. Our numerical calculations on inverse participation ratio, normalized participation ratio, fractal dimension and real-space wavefunction distribution confirm such localization features. The continuum model simulations on the experimental polariton modes also yield consistent results and hence validate their experimental feasibility. The constructed DMAA model provides a new framework for studying the localization transition processes between two analogous quasiperiodic models and broadens the understanding of Anderson localization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how two coupled quantum modes interact through a variable beam splitter by mapping the evolution onto Wigner's phase space. By deriving the corresponding current, the authors demonstrate that this complex quantum mixing process simplifies mathematically to independent classical rotations in position and momentum spaces. This provides deep geometrical insights into mode mixing dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful geometrical interpretation (rotation) for mode mixing in quantum optics using phase-space methods, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems and linear optical elements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on analyzing the strongly mode mixing dynamics of variable beam splitters by tracking the evolution of the Wigner distribution in phase space.

**Main result.** The dynamics are shown to be equivalent to classical rotations confined separately within the position and momentum planes, allowing for explicit calculation of rotated Wigner distributions using classical trajectories.

**Method.** The authors use the Wigner-transform formalism, deriving a continuity equation for the distribution whose current allows mapping quantum evolution onto classical trajectory analysis.

**Model / system.** The system involves two continuous single-mode quantum systems (two optical modes) interacting via a variable beam splitter. The dynamics are governed by an effective Hamiltonian derived from the unitary beam splitter operator, analyzed in the four-dimensional phase space.

**Key observables.** Wigner distribution W(r, tau) and the corresponding Wigner current J.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to the reflectivity parameters of the variable beam splitters ($\varrho$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes sufficiently small variations in reflectivity to utilize a continuous effective Hamiltonian approximation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference transformation equations (Eq. 17) describing coordinate mapping from initial to final states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the continuity equation for Wigner's distribution under beam splitting, derives the explicit form of the current J, and then uses this structure to show that the dynamics map onto simple classical rotations in phase space coordinates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Beam splitters allow us to superpose two continuous single mode quantum systems. To study the behaviour of beam splitters' strongly mode mixing dynamics we consider variable beam splitters acting on Wigner's phase space distribution, W , the evolution of which is governed by the continuity-equation {\partial τ} W = - {\nabla} J. We derive the form of the corresponding Wigner current, J. J's form allows us to use a classical trajectories-approach to analyze the influence of the two modes on each other. We show that the dynamics for variable beam splitters amounts to a rotation confined within the plane of the two positions together with the same simultaneous rotation confined within the plane of the two momenta. In this way explicit and very transparent expressions for the rotated Wigner distributions and Wigner currents can be given in terms of classical trajectories. This helps us to gain deeper insights and perform geometrical analyses of the mixing of modes at beam splitters.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a comprehensive, pedagogical review of Anderson localization theory by focusing on the exactly solvable Bethe lattice model. It details how disorder drives a true quantum phase transition, characterizing it using advanced Green's function techniques and self-consistent equations. The paper is crucial for understanding the theoretical limits of wave propagation in disordered media.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous analytical treatment of a quantum phase transition using advanced Green's function techniques is highly relevant for understanding localization phenomena in open or disordered quantum systems studied in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Anderson localization, a quantum phase transition where disorder suppresses wave propagation, by analyzing its behavior on the Bethe lattice.

**Main result.** It establishes that the Bethe lattice provides an analytically tractable framework to study this transition, revealing diagnostics like spectral statistics and the divergence of correlation volumes at criticality.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on the resolvent (Green's function) formalism and the cavity approach to derive self-consistent equations for the system's properties.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Anderson Hamiltonian applied to the Bethe lattice, which serves as an infinite-dimensional limit of disordered quantum systems. It also draws analogies to Many-Body Localization (MBL).

**Key observables.** Localization length ($\xi$), full probability distribution of the local density of states, Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), and the crossing of $\langle \ln Z angle$ through zero.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($W$) relative to hopping amplitude ($t$), connectivity/coordination number, and system dimension (comparing finite $d$ vs. infinite Bethe limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of the Bethe lattice assumes an infinite-dimensional, tree-like structure, which simplifies calculations but neglects loop correlations present in real lattices.

**Figures summary.** Not specified; the notes refer to general concepts like schematic representations of configuration spaces (e.g., hypercubes).

**Paper structure.** The review progresses from defining Anderson localization and its diagnostics (Green's functions, IPR) to detailing the mathematical machinery (resolvent/cavity approach) used on the Bethe lattice, culminating in discussions about critical behavior and connections to DPRM.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anderson localization is a paradigmatic disorder-driven quantum phase transition in which interference effects can completely suppress wave propagation. Its formulation on the Bethe lattice -- an infinite tree with fixed coordination number -- provides a unique setting in which the transition becomes analytically tractable. Owing to its exponential volume growth, the Bethe lattice realizes the infinite-dimensional limit of the Anderson problem and also serves as a useful mean-field-like description of localization in many-body configuration spaces.   These lecture notes provide a pedagogical review of Anderson localization on the Bethe lattice, focusing on the resolvent (Green's function) formalism and the cavity approach. We discuss the main diagnostics of localization and show how they are encoded in the statistical properties of Green's functions, with the full probability distribution of the local density of states emerging as the natural order parameter of the transition. We derive the cavity self-consistent equations for the diagonal resolvent and review both their numerical solution by population dynamics and their analytical treatment in the large-connectivity limit. This framework gives access to the critical disorder and the critical behavior near the transition, including the localization length in the insulating phase and the correlation volume that controls the delocalized phase. We also discuss the connection with directed polymers in random media and its interpretation in terms of rare resonant paths and freezing phenomena.   These notes originated from a series of lectures delivered by one of the authors at the Oropa Summer School on "Fundamental Problems in Statistical Physics XVI" (July 2025).

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses molecular dynamics simulations to study Brownian motion near a wall, focusing on the full hydrodynamic response rather than just diffusion. The key finding is that confinement modifies fluid correlations in complex ways, simultaneously suppressing low-frequency mobility while enhancing high-frequency inertial effects. This demonstrates the power of analyzing the velocity autocorrelation function across all relevant time scales.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the rigorous treatment of time-dependent correlations ($	ext{VACF}$) across multiple dynamic regimes—from ballistic to diffusive—is a sophisticated example of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics applicable to open quantum systems where memory effects are crucial.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate how fluid confinement modifies the full hydrodynamic response of Brownian motion, moving beyond established knowledge of simple diffusion corrections.

**Main result.** The study shows that confinement leads to anisotropic velocity correlations and reveals a counterintuitive enhancement of the effective added mass at short times as the particle approaches the wall.

**Method.** Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations are used to resolve the velocity autocorrelation function across a broad hydrodynamic spectrum, linking zero-frequency mobility to high-frequency inertial behavior.

**Model / system.** The system is a neutrally buoyant colloidal particle immersed in an explicit solvent and confined near a rigid planar wall. The dynamics are governed by fluid-particle interactions simulated via MD.

**Key observables.** Velocity Autocorrelation Function (VACF), diffusion coefficients ($D_\parallel, D_\perp$), effective added mass ($\Delta M^*$), and the hydrodynamic memory kernel.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Confinement distance ($\epsilon/R$), time scales ranging from acoustic to diffusive relaxation, and solvent properties (e.g., compressibility).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that MD simulations can accurately resolve the entire spectrum of fluid dynamics, validating the use of VACF as a comprehensive diagnostic tool.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare measured confined VACFs to theoretical predictions showing anisotropy; they also plot normalized diffusion coefficients and relative added-mass deviations against confinement ratios ($\epsilon/R$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem by reviewing hydrodynamic memory, details the MD methodology for resolving the full spectrum (bulk vs. confined), presents results on anisotropic decay rates, and quantifies both mobility suppression and inertia enhancement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hydrodynamic memory governs Brownian motion over a broad range of timescales, from acoustic wave propagation at short times to diffusive relaxation at long times. While confinement-induced corrections to Brownian diffusion are well established, how confinement modifies the full hydrodynamic response remains less explored. In this Letter, we use molecular-dynamics simulations of a neutrally buoyant colloidal particle in an explicit solvent to resolve the velocity autocorrelation function across a broad hydrodynamic spectrum. In the bulk, the simulations recover compressibility, added mass, the hydrodynamic long-time tail, and Stokes-Einstein diffusion without adjustable parameters. Near a rigid wall, the velocity correlations become anisotropic, their algebraic tails are modified, and the diffusion coefficients are reduced. Most importantly, the short-time dynamics reveals a pronounced enhancement of the effective added mass as the wall is approached. As such, the velocity autocorrelation function appears as a central quantity to bridge the zero-frequency mobility and the high-frequency inertial behaviour of a confined Brownian particle.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper establishes a general theoretical framework for optimizing thermodynamic control in complex, nonequilibrium systems by treating competing costs simultaneously. It demonstrates that the optimal trade-off structure is governed by a single intrinsic scale parameter, which simplifies the analysis and reveals deep structural equivalences between different physical setups like active matter and quantum engines.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalized mathematical tool for analyzing non-equilibrium thermodynamics that moves beyond simple efficiency maximization by mapping out the entire achievable performance landscape using multi-objective optimization techniques.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work is developing a general framework for multi-objective thermodynamic control, addressing that single-objective optimization fails to capture the full performance space defined by competing costs.

**Main result.** The optimal protocols are shown to generically consist of smooth branches connected by boundary jumps, and the entire trade-off structure collapses onto a single intrinsic scale parameterizing equivalence classes of optimal strategies.

**Method.** The authors formulate the problem using multi-objective optimization via scalarization ($\Omega = \sum eta_i C_i$), deriving optimality conditions from variational principles applied to generalized cost functionals.

**Model / system.** Two paradigmatic systems are analyzed: an active particle in a harmonic trap governed by an overdamped Langevin equation, and a cyclic quantum-dot engine operating between two temperature baths. The general framework applies to intrinsically nonequilibrium systems.

**Key observables.** Pareto front, average work ($\langle W angle$), work fluctuations ($	ext{Var}(W)$), power ($P$), entropy production rate ($\dot{\sigma}$), and the intrinsic scale parameterization ($	ext{Pe}_eta$ or $\omega_
u(\gamma)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relative weights of objectives ($eta_i$ or $\gamma$), Péclet number ($	ext{Pe}$), and system-specific coupling constants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the relative weights determine the trade-off structure, and in some cases, relies on specific boundary conditions or low-order polynomial representations for simplification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Pareto front (showing the full set of optimal trade-offs) and corresponding optimal control protocols ($\lambda^*(t)$ or $\epsilon^*(t)$) for both the active particle and quantum dot engine.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the general multi-objective variational framework, applies it first to the active particle model (showing dependence on an effective activity scale), then extends it to the quantum dot engine (analyzing power vs. dissipation trade-offs), culminating in a unified understanding of how system parameters collapse into single governing scales.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermodynamic control is typically formulated as the optimisation of a single objective, yet competing costs rarely admit a common optimum, so single-objective control captures only one corner of the achievable performance space. We develop a general framework for multi-objective thermodynamic control of intrinsically nonequilibrium systems that maps out the full Pareto front of optimal control strategies. We show that Pareto-optimal protocols generically consist of smooth branches connected by boundary jumps, and that the relative weights of the objectives combine with the physical parameters into a single intrinsic scale that alone governs the trade-off. Remarkably, this scale plays a double role: it parametrizes a single functional form that generates the entire front, and it defines control equivalence classes, in which systems with different parameters but the same scale share identical optimal strategies. We illustrate the framework for two paradigmatic systems that are experimentally accessible: transport of an active particle in a harmonic trap, and a cyclic quantum-dot engine. For both, we obtain the Pareto front and optimal strategies in closed form.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces and validates a Dynamical Low-Rank Approximation (DLRA) method to solve the Wigner equation for various quantum systems. By exploiting a separable approximation of the potential, it transforms the computationally prohibitive nonlocal operator into a manageable low-rank form. This results in massive reductions in computational cost while maintaining high accuracy across diverse physical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, scalable numerical framework for simulating quantum dynamics in phase space that bypasses the curse of dimensionality inherent in full-grid methods, which is crucial for studying complex many-body interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient Dynamical Low-Rank Approximation (DLRA) algorithm to solve the Wigner equation, which is complicated by its nonlocal pseudo-differential operator ($\Psi$DO).

**Main result.** The proposed DLRA method achieves a significant computational advantage, reducing runtime and memory requirements by one to two orders of magnitude compared to full-grid approaches across various quantum scattering systems.

**Method.** The core technique involves applying the low-rank approximation (DLRA) to the Wigner function evolution, enabled by assuming a separable decomposition for the difference potential within the $\Psi$DO.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on solving the Wigner equation, which describes the phase-space dynamics of quantum systems. Benchmarks include harmonic oscillators, Gaussian barrier scattering, electron-electron scattering (Fermion systems), and Helium-like atoms.

**Key observables.** Reduced Wigner function ($W(x, k, t)$) evolution, $L_2$ error ($\varepsilon_2$), and $\Psi$DO components ($Z_j, S_{ij}, L_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time step ($\Delta t$) convergence order (observed second-order), approximation rank ($r$), and the separability rank ($R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the difference potential $D_V(x, y)$ can be approximated by a separable form, allowing the $\Psi$DO to be separated into manageable low-rank components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate convergence analysis (error vs. rank/time step) for harmonic oscillators and Gaussian barriers, and show time evolution snapshots of reduced Wigner functions for electron-electron scattering and Helium-like systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, proposes the DLRA method utilizing separable decomposition of the potential, details the implementation via a projector-splitting integrator (Z $	o$ S $	o$ L steps), presents convergence analysis across multiple physical tests, and concludes with computational cost comparisons.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances in dynamical low-rank approximation (DLRA) have demonstrated its effectiveness in high-dimensional simulations. However, existing DLRA algorithms still face significant challenges when handling systems that involve complex collision terms, including the pseudo-differential operator (${\rm Ψ}$) in the Wigner equation, a representative operator characterized by nonlocality. It is deserving to carry out a series of works to develop the DLRA algorithms for solving the Wigner equation. As the first step in this series of works, we propose an efficient DLRA algorithm for the Wigner equation, using a separable decomposition of the difference potential. We combine this separable assumption with two often-used truncations of ${\rm Ψ}$, namely $\mathcal{K}$-truncation and $\mathcal{Y}$-truncation, to obtain a kind of separated representation of ${\rm Ψ}$. Complexity analysis and several challenging experiments, including harmonic oscillators, Gaussian barrier scattering, electron-electron scattering, and a Helium-like system, all of which satisfy the separable assumption, confirm that the proposed DLRA algorithm has significant advantages, achieving a reduction in computational effort by one to two orders of magnitude in both runtime and memory requirements compared to the full-grid approach. It is worth noting that, even in the absence of a predetermined low-rank structure for the solution, DLRA can still serve as a numerical scheme that balances efficiency and accuracy.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates the Kondo-Heisenberg model to understand how magnetism and superconductivity compete. Using advanced quantum simulation methods like NQS, it demonstrates that the system undergoes a fundamental change in its electronic Fermi surface structure. This transition region hosts robust $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductivity, suggesting pairing is mediated by residual antiferromagnetic correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental questions in strongly correlated electron physics, particularly the mechanism of unconventional superconductivity arising from competing magnetic orders, which is a major focus area for theoretical condensed matter physicists.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the interplay between Fermi surface changes, antiferromagnetism, and unconventional $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductivity in a two-dimensional Kondo-Heisenberg model.

**Main result.** The model exhibits a transition from a small to a large electronic Fermi surface as the Kondo coupling increases, with $d$-wave superconductivity appearing robustly in the crossover region between magnetic and heavy Fermi liquid states.

**Method.** The research employs advanced quantum many-body techniques, primarily Neural Network Quantum States (NQS) variational optimization, supplemented by mean-field calculations for comparison.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional Kondo-Heisenberg model on a square lattice, describing conduction electrons interacting with localized spin moments via exchange and Kondo couplings. The Hamiltonian includes terms for hopping, Heisenberg exchange ($J$), and Kondo coupling ($JK$).

**Key observables.** Fermi surface enclosed area (small vs. large), $d_{x^2-y^2}$ pairing correlator ($\Delta$), staggered magnetization ($m_s^2$), and conduction electron momentum distribution ($n(\mathbf{k})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kondo coupling ($JK$) and Heisenberg coupling ($J$); the analysis is performed at fixed doping (e.g., $\delta=1/4$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of NQS methods allows for calculations away from half-filling, avoiding the sign problem encountered by auxiliary-field QMC; mean-field approaches are used for comparison but are noted to overestimate magnetic order.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of order parameters ($m_s^2$ vs. $\Delta$) across different coupling regimes, showing a superconducting dome peaking near the quantum critical point where the Fermi surface changes character.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps the ground-state phase diagram by analyzing how electronic and magnetic order parameters evolve as tuning parameters (Kondo/Heisenberg couplings) are varied. It contrasts results from NQS with mean-field theory to establish physical regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the two-dimensional Kondo-Heisenberg model on a square lattice, with the conduction electrons away from half-filling, using neural network quantum states. Mapping the ground-state phase diagram as a function of the Kondo and Heisenberg couplings, we identify (i) at weak Kondo coupling, antiferromagnetic Néel order with a Fermi surface whose enclosed area counts only the conduction electrons and is insensitive to the Néel order, and (ii) at strong coupling, a heavy Fermi liquid with a Fermi surface whose enclosed area counts both the conduction electrons and the spins. In the crossover between these regimes, we find $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductivity, evidenced by off-diagonal long-range order in the pair-pair correlations and a pairing-amplitude dome that coexists with the underlying magnetic phase. Our results establish Fermi volume change and unconventional superconductivity as intrinsic features of the two-dimensional Kondo-Heisenberg model.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes superconductivity in a trilayer Hubbard model near $1/3$ filling using the FLEX approximation. It finds that while weak interactions yield symmetric superconducting behavior, strong correlations induce a significant electron-hole asymmetry favoring hole doping. This suggests that spin fluctuations play a crucial role in determining the pairing symmetry and stability of the superconducting state.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on correlated electrons, the detailed analysis of pairing mechanisms ($\Delta$) and susceptibility ($\chi_s$) provides insights into how collective excitations (spin fluctuations) mediate pairing in strongly interacting quantum systems, which has parallels to dynamics studied in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electron-hole asymmetry of superconductivity near $1/3$ filling in the trilayer Hubbard model.

**Main result.** Strong correlations (large U) induce a pronounced electron-hole asymmetry, favoring superconductivity when the system is hole-doped relative to $1/3$ filling.

**Method.** The research employs the Fluctuation Exchange Approximation (FLEX) combined with solving the linearized Eliashberg equation to calculate superconducting parameters.

**Model / system.** The model is the trilayer Hubbard model, featuring three coupled layers and an onsite interaction U. The analysis focuses on band fillings near $1/3$ where bonding and nonbonding bands are nearly half-filled.

**Key observables.** Superconducting coupling constant ($\lambda$), gap function ($\Delta$), spectral function ($A(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$), and dynamical spin susceptibility ($	ext{Im } \chi_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ratio ($U/t_{\perp}$), doping level (hole-doped vs. electron-doped relative to $1/3$ filling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation used is the Fluctuation Exchange Approximation (FLEX) theory, and calculations involve solving the linearized Eliashberg equation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $\lambda$ dependence on doping showing asymmetry for large $U/t_{\perp}$, and gap functions ($\Delta$) confirming $s_{\pm}$-wave structure in the hole-doped case versus local pairing at strong coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model and methodology (FLEX, Eliashberg), then comparing weak vs. strong coupling limits to identify the emergence of electron-hole asymmetry, and finally analyzing gap functions and spin susceptibilities across different doping regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study within the fluctuation exchange approximation the trilayer Hubbard model where three layers of the Hubbard model are coupled by large interlayer hoppings so that the overlap of the bonding, nonbonding, and antibonding bands is relatively small. We pay special attention to the band fillings close to 1/3, for which the bonding and nonbonding bands as a whole are close to half filling. For relatively small values of the onsite $U$, superconductivity roughly exhibits electron-hole symmetric behavior, as expected for a nearly half-filled two-band system. By contrast, an asymmetry appears when $U$ becomes large, where superconductivity is more favored in the hole-doped regime, i.e., in the regime where electrons are removed from 1/3 filling. We attribute this asymmetry to the asymmetric renormalization of the bonding, nonbonding, and antibonding bands when $U$ is large.

</details>

### [Auxiliary Schmidt Rank as a Resource for Photonic Bell Measurements](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24591v1)

**Authors:** Pradip Laha, Peter van Loock  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24591v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5** · `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2606.24591_figures/2606.24591_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Resource hierarchy for deterministic high-dimensional Bell-state decoding. The bare two-photon/vacuum-mode class R0(d) admits no conclusive generalized Bell-label out- come for d &gt; 2 [42]. Same-photon auxiliary entanglement can realize a single Bell-label functional at rΦ ⩾⌈d/2⌉, but de- terministic decoding in the static passive two-photon model requires full auxiliary Schmidt rank.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2606.24591_figures/2606.24591_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Deterministic no-go mechanism for rΦ &lt; d. Same- mode two-photon outcomes are rank deficient and must van- ish in any deterministic analyzer. This enforces the rank split aµ+bµ ⩽rΦ, which makes every off-diagonal contraction rank deficient as well; hence, no fine-grained outcome can carry a Bell-state label.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** The paper establishes a rigorous resource threshold theorem showing that achieving deterministic Bell measurements for qudits requires the auxiliary entangled state's Schmidt rank ($r_\Phi$) to match the dimension of the system ($d$). This proves that entanglement is a necessary and quantifiable resource beyond simple passive optical elements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental resource theorems quantifying how entanglement (via Schmidt rank) must be supplied to overcome inherent limitations of passive linear optics when performing high-dimensional quantum state measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research determines the minimum auxiliary Schmidt rank required for photonic systems to achieve deterministic Bell measurements when passive linear optics are used.

**Main result.** Deterministic discrimination of all $d^2$ Bell-state labels requires the auxiliary Schmidt rank ($r_\Phi$) to be at least equal to the qudit dimension, i.e., $r_\Phi \ge d$.

**Method.** The analysis uses rank constraints derived from analyzing effective system-level detection matrices ($\Gamma_\Phi(P)$) formed by contracting over the auxiliary state.

**Model / system.** The model involves two photons carrying unknown qudits ($S_A, S_B$) interacting with a fixed auxiliary entangled state $|\Phiangle_R$ via passive linear optics and Photon-Number-Resolving (PNR) detection.

**Key observables.** Bell-state labels $(p, q)$, the success probability of Bell measurement ($p_{	ext{BM}}$), and the rank of the effective detection matrix $\Gamma_\Phi(P)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The qudit dimension $d$ and the auxiliary Schmidt rank $r_\Phi$. Thresholds include $r_\Phi \ge \lceil d/2 ceil$ for single outcomes and $r_\Phi \ge d$ for full determinism.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ideal PNR detection, a fixed auxiliary state $|\Phiangle$, and the use of only passive linear optics without active operations.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize resource hierarchies showing thresholds for bare algebra ($R_0(d)$), single functional ($\lceil d/2 ceil$), and deterministic decoding ($d$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of passive optics, then deriving rank bounds on detection matrices using auxiliary state entanglement, culminating in the proof that $r_\Phi=d$ is necessary and sufficient for full Bell-label distinguishability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum communication and fusion-based quantum computation, photonic Bell measurements are fundamentally limited when only passive linear optics is employed. While for qubits, some Bell states can be unambiguously identified with static beam splitters and no extra photons or entanglement, additional auxiliary photons or at least additional auxiliary degrees of freedom with a certain level of additional entanglement are needed to approach or attain a complete, deterministic Bell measurement. Here, we prove an exact resource threshold when the same two photons carry system qudits of dimension $d$ and a fixed auxiliary entangled state $Φ$, possibly distributed over several additional degrees of freedom, with total Schmidt rank $r_Φ$. We show that a single conclusive Bell-label functional can occur for $r_Φ\geqslant\lceil d/2\rceil$, but deterministic discrimination of all $d^2$ Bell-state labels requires $r_Φ\geqslant d$. A maximally entangled rank-$d$ auxiliary state achieves the bound by local Bell-basis sorting between each photon's system and auxiliary degrees of freedom. Thus, the auxiliary Schmidt rank is a certified resource for ancilla-photon-free, embedded photonic Bell measurements.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.23809v1>

To preserve the graph-state basis, homogeneous two-qubit gates must respect this bipartition: nodes in one partition apply the gate in one orientation, while nodes in the opposite partition apply the corresponding gate with reversed control–target direction. The allowed gate pairs implement- ing this rule are listed in Table I.</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2606.23809_figures/2606.23809_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Action of a CNOT gate on a graph state. When a CNOT gate is applied with control qubit i and target qubit j, the graph is transformed by toggling edges between the control vertex and each neighbor of the target vertex. Concretely, for every vertex (k ∈N(j)) with (k ̸= i), an edge (i, k) is added if it is absent and removed if it is present. All other edges remain unchanged.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2606.23809_figures/2606.23809_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3. Action of the XCX gate on a graph state. When an XCX gate is applied between qubits i and j, it induces a complementation between the neighborhoods of the two vertices. Specifically, for every pair of vertices (k ∈N(i)) and (l ∈N(j)), an edge (k, l) is added if it is absent and removed if it is present. All other edges remain unchanged.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2606.23809_figures/2606.23809_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 4. Action of the XCX gate on a leaf vertex and its compensation by a CZ gate. Consider an XCX gate applied between qubits i and j, where i is a leaf vertex. Be- cause i has only one neighbor, the XCX operation induces only a single edge toggle between the corresponding neigh- boring vertices. In the configuration shown, this creates the additional edge (b1, b2). The extra edge can be removed by applying a CZ gate between b1 and b2, thereby restoring the original graph structure. Hence, when restricted to a leaf ver- tex, the effect of XCX can be exactly compensated by a CZ operation on the corresponding neighboring qubits, preserv- ing the graph state.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2606.23809_figures/2606.23809_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 5. Bilocal (B) operations on leaf vertices. The 5- qubit T-shaped graph state contains three leaf vertices. Each leaf together with its unique neighbor forms a pair on which a bilocal gate can act across the two copies of the state. Con- sequently, up to three B-group operations may be applied independently, one at each leaf-neighbor pair. For each such pair, the gate acting on the leaf qubit is chosen from the set listed in Table II, while the corresponding operation applied to the neighbor qubit follows the mapping given in Table III. Be- cause each leaf connects to the rest of the graph through a sin- gle edge, these bilocal operations remain confined to the cor- responding...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a systematic framework to efficiently design entanglement distillation circuits for graph states by restricting gates to those that preserve the graph structure. By classifying these 'factorized graph-preserving' operations using concepts like Local Complementation orbits and Minimum Edge Representatives, the authors provide a scalable method superior to standard recurrence protocols under realistic noise conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation, the rigorous topological classification of allowed operations (H/B groups) provides a powerful mathematical tool for understanding constraints in highly entangled many-body systems, which can inform theoretical models in condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Systematic entanglement distillation for arbitrary graph states is challenging due to the rapid growth of the circuit design space.

**Main result.** The authors introduce factorized, graph-preserving Clifford operations and use Minimum Edge Representatives (MERs) over Local Complementation (LC) orbits to design scalable, topology-aware purification circuits that outperform standard protocols under noise.

**Method.** They classify admissible gates into homogeneous (H) and bilocal (B) groups based on the underlying graph structure (bipartiteness or leaf vertices), allowing for a compact, factorized description of the gate set.

**Model / system.** The system involves multipartite entangled graph states $|	ext{G}angle$, which are fundamental to measurement-based quantum computation and stabilizer codes. The analysis focuses on operations that preserve this graph-state basis across local Clifford transformations.

**Key observables.** Output fidelity ($F_{out}$), success probability, and the minimum number of edges in a Local Complementation (LC) orbit representative.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph structure (bipartite vs. non-bipartite), presence of leaf vertices, input entanglement fidelity ($F$), and noise model parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to use MERs to provide a canonical representation for LC orbits, and that the gate set can be factorized into H and B components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the action of CNOT gates on graph states (Fig. 1), the structure of homogeneous gates on bipartite graphs (Fig. 2), and how bilocal XCX operations at a leaf vertex can be compensated by a CZ gate to preserve the state basis (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the problem, defines graph-preserving gates via H and B groups based on graph topology, establishes the framework using LC orbits and MERs for canonical representation, and finally applies this structure to optimize noisy distillation circuits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Graph states form a broad class of multipartite entangled states underlying measurement-based quantum computation, quantum networks, and stabilizer codes. However, systematic entanglement distillation for arbitrary graph states remains challenging because the circuit design space grows rapidly with the number of parties. We introduce a group of Clifford operations that we call "factorized graph-preserving". It enables us to efficiently enumerate and optimize graph-state purification circuits at finite size for realistic noisy hardware. These operations map products of graph-basis states to products of graph-basis states, so their action can be represented as permutations of graph-basis labels. Moreover, this useful gate set admits a compact factorized description determined by simple graph-theoretic features. This structure also allows, after some initial cached precomputation, drastically lower computational complexity for simulating a gate. We further organize these operations over local-complementation (LC) orbits using minimum-edge representatives (MERs), which let us design purification circuits that apply to all locally equivalent graph states (up to a basis change). Using this framework, we optimize noisy finite-size multipartite distillation circuits for several graph-state families. Numerical results show that the resulting graph-preserving circuits can outperform standard recurrence-based purification protocols under realistic gate and measurement noise. Our results establish LC-orbit structure and factorized graph-preserving operations as practical tools for scalable, topology-aware and hardware-constrained graph-state distillation protocol design. Our work can also be interpreted as a graph-based heuristic for finding transversal gates.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously analyzes the probability distribution of particle displacements in Continuous-Time Random Walks (CTRWs), showing that while typical fluctuations follow Gaussian statistics, rare events exhibit pronounced Laplace tails. By developing a rate function framework valid at finite times, the authors provide a powerful theoretical tool to characterize anomalous diffusion in complex media.

**Why it may be interesting.** The robust derivation of non-Gaussian tails (Laplace tails) in a fundamental transport model like CTRW provides insights into anomalous diffusion regimes relevant to complex biological or glassy systems, which is crucial for understanding energy/particle transport dynamics beyond simple Brownian motion.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the deviation of particle displacement distributions from the expected Gaussian behavior (Brownian motion) in complex media, focusing on the emergence of pronounced exponential decay known as Laplace tails.

**Main result.** The probability density function $P(x, t)$ for displacements in Continuous-Time Random Walks exhibits Laplace tails in the large spatial separation limit ($|x| 	o \infty$), which is quantified by a rate function $\chi_{ARF}(q)$.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques from Large Deviation Theory (LDT), utilizing saddle point approximations and developing rate function-like frameworks for both the positional PDF $P(x, t)$ and the renewal probability $Q_t(n)$.

**Model / system.** The model is the Continuous-Time Random Walk (CTRW), which describes particle diffusion in complex environments like glasses or living cells. The process is governed by random waiting times $\psi(	au)$ and subsequent jumps.

**Key observables.** $P(x, t)$ (positional PDF), $Q_t(n)$ (probability of exactly $n$ renewals), and the associated rate functions ($I(y)$ or $\chi(q)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time $t$, spatial separation $|x|$, ratio $q=x/t$, and parameters defining the waiting time distribution $\psi(	au)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on developing rate function frameworks valid for finite times, extending beyond standard asymptotic LDT results. Approximations are made using saddle point methods and Taylor expansions of $\psi(	au)$ near $	au=0$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $Q_t(n)$ across different waiting time PDFs (Erlang, sum of two exponentials) against CLT predictions and the derived Asymptotic Rate Function (ARF), demonstrating matching tail behavior. Other figures plot $P(x, t)$ showing convergence to Laplace tails.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a rate function framework for $Q_t(n)$, analyzes the large deviation regime ($y=n/t$), and then extends this formalism to derive the propagator $P(x, t)$. The core is comparing theoretical predictions (ARF) against known limits (CLT) and numerical simulations across various time scales.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

During Brownian motion, the displacement is normally distributed, a classical fact aligned with the central limit theorem. However, single particle tracking in complex media such as glasses, living cells, and colloidal suspensions often reveals pronounced exponential decay of the displacement distribution, known as Laplace tails. In a short letter, two of us presented the emergence of Laplace tails in the continuous time random walk (CTRW) framework. Here, a detailed complementary study is presented. By exploring the behavior of $Q_t(n)$, the probability that exactly $n$ renewals occur during time $t$, we develop a rate function-like framework for this quantity, valid for finite $t$. We show that $Q_t(n)$ exhibits exponential tails, which in turn give rise to exponential tails of the positional probability density function $P(x,t)$. Favorable comparison to finite-time numerical simulations and asymptotic large deviation rate functions establishes the validity of our results over a wide temporal range.

</details>

**Summary.** This work unifies quantum tomography and retrodiction by demonstrating that they are governed by the same underlying principle: maximizing the log-likelihood of measurement outcomes. The authors derive a generalized Kubo–Mori update, showing it relates directly to the Petz recovery map, thereby providing a robust, gradient-based framework for state reconstruction in quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical link between fundamental concepts in quantum estimation theory (tomography/retrodiction) using modern tools from information geometry and optimization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper unifies quantum tomography (state reconstruction) and quantum retrodiction (inferring past states) by showing they stem from a single underlying principle.

**Main result.** It proves that the Petz recovery map is mathematically equivalent to the gradient update used in maximum-likelihood quantum tomography, establishing a general framework for state estimation.

**Method.** The authors employ techniques from information geometry and optimization theory, deriving iterative updates (KM update) based on maximizing the log-likelihood functional of measurement data.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on general quantum channels $E$ acting on density matrices $ho$, applicable to various measurement scenarios including quantum-to-classical measurements.

**Key observables.** Log-likelihood functional $L_X(\sigma)$, Petz recovery map ($\mathcal{R}_{\sigma, E}(X)$), and the Kubo–Mori (KM) update ($\mathcal{R}_{	ext{KM}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of non-commutativity between measured observables ($[E(\sigma), X]$) dictates the difference between the Petz map and the KM update.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes conditions ensuring positive evidence ($X \ge 0$) and that the output state is invertible or well-behaved for gradient calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic process of the KM update, showing how evidence leads to a corrected estimate, and demonstrate convergence towards the Maximum Likelihood Estimator (MLE) in various setups.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the MLE problem, deriving the gradient-based KM update, establishing its relationship to the Petz map via non-commutativity terms, and finally proving monotonicity properties for both updates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum tomography and quantum retrodiction are traditionally viewed as separate inference tasks: tomography reconstructs quantum states from measurement data, whereas retrodiction infers past quantum states from observed outcomes. We show that the two are manifestations of the same underlying principle. We prove that the Petz recovery map associated with a measurement channel is precisely the gradient update of the log-likelihood used in maximum-likelihood tomography. Consequently, repeated applications of the Petz map monotonically increase the likelihood. Extending beyond measurement channels, we derive a noncommutative generalization of the Petz map from the gradient of a generalized likelihood for arbitrary quantum channels. The resulting iterative procedure maximizes the likelihood and provides a general framework for quantum tomography, establishing a direct bridge between retrodiction, recovery maps, and statistical inference.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Wavelet Matrix Product States (wMPS) as a novel, highly accurate variational method for solving continuous Quantum Field Theories. By leveraging the multi-resolution properties of Daubechies wavelets, it allows systematic refinement of approximations without needing explicit lattice discretization. This technique is shown to accurately compute key observables like energy density and correlation functions for models such as the Lieb-Liniger system.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, systematic numerical tool for studying continuum quantum systems that avoids the pitfalls of standard lattice discretization, offering variational bounds on ground state energies relevant to many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a variational method to solve continuum quantum field theories (QFT) by mapping them onto discrete tensor network representations.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that using Wavelet Matrix Product States (wMPS) with Daubechies wavelets allows for systematic, highly accurate approximation of QFT ground states, showing convergence towards Continuous MPS results.

**Method.** They employ a variational approach utilizing wMPS, which are built on wavelet bases and optimized iteratively through multi-resolution analysis to approximate the continuum Fock space.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to continuum quantum models like the Lieb-Liniger model of non-relativistic bosons. The Hamiltonian involves kinetic and four-point interaction terms derived from the wavelets.

**Key observables.** Energy density, two-point correlation functions, and density-density correlators are computed to track convergence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The resolution scale ($r$) of the wavelets, the bond dimension ($\chi$), and the Daubechies function order ($N \geq 6$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the Hamiltonian is local and that the state can be accurately approximated by a tensor product structure derived from the wavelet basis; it relies on the inclusion property of the chosen wavelets.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the reduction sequence of the Hilbert space ($\mathcal{H} 	o \mathcal{H}_r 	o 	ext{MPS manifold}$), show energy density convergence vs. resolution $r$, and compare optimization routines with and without iterative refinement.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds mathematically by establishing the necessary properties for a suitable wavelet basis (Daubechies functions) to ensure variationality and inclusion. It then details the construction of the Hamiltonian in terms of local coefficients ($K, \Gamma_4$) and presents an iterative algorithm involving Inverse Wavelet Transforms ($	ext{IWT}$) followed by $	ext{ITEBD}$ contraction and projection.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a variational method to solve continuum quantum models with discrete tensor network techniques. The method leverages wavelet matrix product states (wMPS): matrix product states built on top of sufficiently regular ($N\geq 6$) Daubechies scaling functions. These states live in the continuum field theory Fock space, have finite energy density, and can be optimized with standard algorithms, without restriction to free theories. Further, exploiting the multi-resolution analysis built into wavelets, and its quantum circuit description, we can iteratively refine wMPS to obtain accurate approximations at arbitrarily fine length-scales. We showcase the efficiency of the method on the Lieb-Liniger model, computing energy density and correlation functions.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel design framework to drastically reduce the physical space required for generating essential magic states like CCZ and T states in surface code quantum computation. By converting complex gate operations into efficient joint measurements and utilizing specialized four-qubit twisted syndrome measurements, they achieve substantial resource savings while maintaining high error suppression capabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it directly addresses hardware constraints (physical qubit count) in scaling quantum computers. The techniques developed—especially the Pauli rotation conversion and compact measurement implementation—provide concrete engineering blueprints for building fault-tolerant modules.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to reduce the physical resource cost, specifically the spatial footprint, required for implementing magic state distillation protocols within a surface code architecture.

**Main result.** The authors propose a framework that reduces the spatial cost of generating CCZ and T magic states by converting gate-based circuits into compact joint measurement architectures using four-qubit twisted measurements. This results in significant resource savings compared to previous designs.

**Method.** They convert complex, gate-based distillation protocols (like [[8, 3, 2]] CCZ) into sequences of Pauli rotation joint measurements ($\pi/8$ rotations). Redundant auxiliary qubits are eliminated by exploiting the trivial action of Z-type operators on $|0angle$ states.

**Model / system.** The work is situated in the context of fault-tolerant quantum computation using the surface code. The system models the distillation process for non-Clifford resource states, specifically focusing on CCZ and T magic states, which are essential for universal quantum computation beyond Clifford gates.

**Key observables.** Spatial cost (reduced from $396d^2$ to $113d^2$), output error rates ($p_{1,	ext{CCZ}} \simeq 96p_L + 28p_T^2$), and the ability to maintain single-fault detection.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), input magic state error rate ($p_T$), logical memory error probability ($p_L$), and the specific distillation protocol parameters (e.g., [[8, 3, 2]] for CCZ).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes independent error sources between logical memory errors and input magic state errors. Furthermore, it relies on the ability to implement required measurements using local twist regions, keeping ancilla patches compact.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transformation from initial gate-based circuits (Fig 1) to measurement-based ones (Fig 2), showing the final reduced four-qubit circuit (Fig 3). Later figures detail the implementation of the key twisted ZY measurement and compare resource costs across different factory designs.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of high spatial cost in magic state factories. It then details the methodology: converting gate circuits to Pauli rotation measurements, followed by implementing these measurements using novel four-qubit twisted syndrome measurements. Finally, it presents quantitative comparisons showing significant resource reduction for both CCZ and T state factories.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a design framework for reconstructing gate-based magic state distillation protocols as compact joint-measurement architectures implementable with the surface code. The goal is to reduce the surface-code resource cost of a magic state factory while preserving the logical function and error-detection structure of the distillation protocol. We construct a reduced architecture for implementing an eight-to-three CCZ distillation protocol using smaller surface-code patches. The proposed factory preserves the single-fault-detection property and the leading-order error suppression of the protocol, while producing CCZ magic states with lower spatial cost than the design of Gidney and Fowler. The proposed design perspective can also be applied to T-state factories and other multiqubit non-Clifford resource-state factories. Our approach provides a framework for extending the design space of surface-code magic state factories beyond a single CCZ layout optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically distinguishes between singlet and triplet superconducting pairing symmetries in moiré graphene by predicting distinct signatures. The authors leverage symmetry constraints and model both bulk quasiparticle excitations and interface-bound Andreev states. Observing the Zeeman field response of these two types of probes offers a definitive experimental pathway to characterize the underlying pairing mechanism.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter superconductivity, the use of topological protection in Andreev bound states and the detailed analysis of spin/momentum dependence under external fields provides rich theoretical tools applicable to understanding exotic quantum phases in engineered systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to definitively determine the pairing symmetry (singlet vs. triplet) of superconductivity in moiré graphene superconductors, and if triplet, to identify the direction of the d-vector.

**Main result.** Distinct signatures are predicted for singlet versus triplet pairing when probing the superconducting state using Zeeman fields applied either to bulk quasiparticles or at superconductor/insulator interfaces via Andreev bound states (ABS).

**Method.** The analysis combines symmetry constraints ($\mathcal{T}, C_{2z}$) with theoretical modeling of the Bogoliubov-de Gennes Hamiltonian, analyzing both low-energy Density of States (DOS) and phase-sensitive ABS spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The system is magic-angle moiré graphene superconductors operating on the hole-doped side of $
u = -2$, characterized by a normal state constrained by an Incommensurate Kekulé Spiral (IKS).

**Key observables.** Low-energy Density of States $N(E)$ under Zeeman field, and the splitting/behavior of topologically protected Andreev Bound States (ABS) at interfaces.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zeeman field orientation ($\vec{B}$ relative to $\hat{d}$), doping level, and proximity to nodal superconductivity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes robustness in the low-energy limit, relies on symmetry constraints derived from the IKS normal state, and models ABS using a smooth interface approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Zeeman splitting of zero-field ABS peaks for singlet pairing (splitting to $\pm B$) versus the angle-dependent splitting observed for triplet pairing ($\vec{B} \parallel \hat{d}$ vs. $\vec{B} \perp \hat{d}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically constrains the order parameter using symmetry arguments, develops theoretical predictions for two key experimental probes (bulk DOS and interface ABS), and contrasts the expected signatures for singlet versus triplet pairing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The pairing symmetry of magic-angle moiré graphene is a fundamental question that remains unresolved. Combining experimental and theoretical inputs, we constrain the superconducting order parameters that can emerge from the incommensurate Kekulé spiral (IKS) normal state on the hole-doped side of $ν= -2$. Imposing the additional experimental constraint of nodal superconductivity, we are left with the task of distinguishing between singlet or triplet pairing, and of determining the d-vector in the latter case. We propose definitive tests to identify the pairing symmetry based on two classes of experiments using the response of the superconducting state to Zeeman field orientation. The first set of predictions is for spectroscopic and thermodynamic measurements sensitive to low-energy excitations near the nodes. The second set is for phase-sensitive measurements of topologically protected Andreev bound states near boundaries, whose spectroscopy is shown to provide a smoking gun signature of the pairing symmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This review synthesizes recent work showing that topological defects govern mechanical failure in amorphous solids. By analyzing fields derived from particle displacements and vibrational modes, the authors demonstrate that singularities like vortices correlate precisely with yielding transitions. This suggests topology offers a powerful, universal descriptor for plasticity beyond traditional structural models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter mechanics, the reliance on characterizing system states via global topological invariants (like winding numbers) is mathematically analogous to methods used in quantum field theory or analyzing entanglement structures in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The review aims to establish a unified topological description for plasticity and mechanical failure in amorphous solids (glasses), extending concepts from crystalline defects.

**Main result.** Topological defects, such as vortices and hedgehog defects derived from non-affine displacements or vibrational modes, show strong correlations with soft spots, yielding, and shear-band formation in glasses.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical frameworks (e.g., STZ theory, non-affine elasticity) with computational analysis of topological invariants calculated on continuous fields derived from simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is amorphous solids, including metallic and colloidal glasses, studied across various states like jammed or yielding regimes. The analysis focuses on the structural disorder inherent to these materials.

**Key observables.** Topological invariants (winding numbers, hedgehog charges), non-affine displacement fields ($D_{2,	ext{min}}$), low-frequency vibrational modes, and defect density ($	ext{NHTD}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Shear strain ($\gamma$), critical fraction of non-affine particles ($p_c$), and the frequency spectrum ($\omega$) of vibrations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that topological structures provide a robust, universal language to describe mechanical failure in disordered systems, analogous to dislocations in crystals.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate defect visualization (vortices/hedgehog defects) in 2D and 3D fields derived from non-affine displacements and vibrational modes. Key figures correlate the density of these topological defects with the onset of yielding or shear localization.

**Paper structure.** The review progresses by contrasting crystalline defect theory with amorphous systems, introducing concepts like non-affine motion and soft spots, and culminating in a systematic analysis showing how topological charges characterize plasticity during mechanical loading.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological defects play a central role in the mechanical behavior of crystalline materials, yet their relevance to amorphous solids has only recently begun to emerge. Over the last few years, theoretical, computational, and experimental studies have revealed the presence of well-defined topological invariants in vibrational eigenmodes, non-affine displacement fields, and deformation-induced vector fields of glasses. These defects have been shown to correlate strongly with soft spots, localized plastic rearrangements, yielding, and shear-band formation, suggesting a new perspective on the microscopic origins of plasticity in disordered materials. In this review, we provide a comprehensive overview of recent developments in the rapidly growing field of topological defects in glasses. We discuss the underlying theoretical concepts, including Burgers vectors, non-affine plasticity, vibrational modes, and topological invariants, and review recent numerical and experimental advances. Finally, we assess the current achievements, limitations, and open questions, and discuss future directions toward a unified topological description of plasticity and mechanical failure in amorphous solids.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate a highly sensitive Rydberg microwave receiver by exploiting the Doppler effect using a co-propagating laser configuration. This setup significantly outperforms the standard counter-propagating geometry, achieving better sensitivity and requiring much lower local oscillator power. The work highlights the tunability of quantum sensing platforms through careful control of light propagation geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, high-sensitivity platform for microwave detection based on quantum interference effects in Rydberg atoms. The comparison between co- and counter-propagating schemes offers valuable insight into how geometric laser alignment can be engineered to optimize fundamental physical measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reports on enhancing the sensitivity of a Rydberg microwave (MW) receiver by strategically exploiting the Doppler effect in an atomic vapor cell.

**Main result.** By using a co-propagating laser configuration, they achieved a significantly enhanced sensitivity ($35.1 	ext{ nV cm}^{-1} 	ext{ Hz}^{-1/2}$) compared to counter-propagating setups, while also reducing the required local oscillator field by a factor of 17.6.

**Method.** The enhancement is realized by implementing a two-photon Rydberg ladder scheme and utilizing the superheterodyne technique to measure the system's response to applied MW fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves alkali-atom-based Rydberg atoms in a vapor cell, modeled using a four-level ladder system. The analysis compares co-propagating versus counter-propagating laser geometries to exploit Doppler shifts.

**Key observables.** EIT transmission amplitude and the derivative (MW-optical gain) of the transmission spectrum as a function of the local oscillator field strength ($E_{LO}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sensitivity values ($	ext{nV cm}^{-1} 	ext{ Hz}^{-1/2}$), enhancement factors (1.5 times better sensitivity, 17.6 factor reduction in LO field).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the rotating-wave approximation applied to the four-level ladder system.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate EIT spectra, amplitude dependence on $E_{LO}$, and MW-optical gain for both co- and counter-propagating setups, confirming superior performance in the co-propagating case.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical setup and mechanism (Doppler enhancement via co-propagation). It then presents theoretical modeling using master equations and experimental measurements comparing the two propagation geometries to quantify the performance gains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the enhanced sensitivity of the Rydberg microwave (MW) receiver by exploiting the Doppler effect in a vapor cell. A two-photon Rydberg ladder scheme is implemented via the co-propagation of probe and coupling lasers, which enhances the Doppler effect. When an MW field is applied, microwave dressing modifies the velocity-dependent resonance condition, enabling stronger contributions from atoms with non-zero velocities and leading to an enhancement of the EIT transmission. Based on this mechanism, we achieve a sensitivity of $35.1\ \mathrm{nV\ cm^{-1}\ Hz^{-1/2}}$ using the heterodyne technique, which is 1.5 times better than that obtained in the counter-propagating configuration. Meanwhile, the required local oscillator (LO) field is reduced by a factor of 17.6 compared with the counter-propagating configuration, which is advantageous for applications requiring minimal radiation and low power consumption. Moreover, the co-propagating configuration is more amenable to integration or portable sensing platforms because multiple laser fields can be delivered through a single optical fiber.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates the impact of correlated laser frequency noise on Mølmer-Sørensen gates used in trapped-ion quantum computing. By employing perturbation theory, the authors quantify gate infidelity based on the noise's power spectral density. They reveal that the sensitivity to noise is highly dependent on the physical separation between the qubits, offering critical guidelines for improving hardware robustness.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides quantitative predictions on how environmental noise couples to quantum gates in solid-state or trapped-ion architectures, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes how correlated laser frequency noise affects the fidelity of Mølmer-Sørensen (MS) gates implemented on trapped-ion chains.

**Main result.** The gate sensitivity to noise depends strongly on ion separation; medium-frequency noise significantly impacts distant ions more than neighboring ones, while low-frequency noise has a position-independent effect.

**Method.** Perturbation theory is employed to calculate the gate infidelity by integrating over the noise power spectral density using a derived sensitivity function $\tilde{I}(\omega)$.

**Model / system.** The system involves qubit registers formed by trapped ions, where gates are executed via bichromatic laser pulses coupling to collective phonon modes. The Hamiltonian includes terms for ideal gate evolution and correlated noise.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F$), sensitivity function $\tilde{I}(\omega)$, and the resulting gate infidelity $1-F$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise power spectral density $S(\omega)$, ion positions within the chain, and the frequency regime of the noise (low vs. medium).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses perturbation theory for the noise term and assumes that certain radial phonon modes can be neglected if their frequencies differ significantly from the primary axial mode.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate pulse shapes $\Omega(t)$ and the calculated MS gate sensitivity functions $\tilde{I}(\omega)$ for various ion pairs and chain lengths (e.g., 20-ion chains).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical system and Hamiltonian, deriving the general expression for infidelity using perturbation theory, calculating the necessary phonon mode traces, and finally analyzing $\tilde{I}(\omega)$ for different noise regimes and ion separations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyze the impact of correlated laser frequency noise on Mølmer-Sørensen gates in qubit registers based on trapped-ion chains. Using perturbation theory, we calculate gate fidelities in the presence of noise with arbitrary power spectral density for different chain lengths and ion positions in the chain. With our approach, we account for simultaneous excitation of multiple phonon modes during gate operation. We find out that the impact of medium-frequency laser noise depends considerably on the positions of the ions in the chain. In contrast, low-frequency noise has similar effect for different chain lengths and ion positions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically compares the frequency shift measured in CPT resonance using phase-jump modulation versus conventional harmonic modulation. The authors derive and analyze the error signal shift for both methods under a $\Lambda$-system model. They conclude that while mathematically similar at short times, the conventional method is preferred experimentally due to its superior noise performance at higher frequencies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical comparison between two advanced spectroscopic techniques used to measure fundamental properties (like light shifts) in atomic systems, which is highly relevant for experimental quantum optics research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study compares the frequency shift observed in coherent population trapping (CPT) resonance spectroscopy when using phase-jump modulation versus conventional harmonic sinusoidal modulation.

**Main result.** While both techniques yield similar results at short integration times, the authors conclude that the conventional approach is more suitable for light shift suppression due to its ability to operate at higher frequencies and better noise characteristics.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving the quantum Liouville equation under various approximations (e.g., low saturation) and deriving expressions for the error signal shift ($\delta_{as}$ or $\delta_Q$) for both modulation schemes.

**Model / system.** The system is a three-level Lambda ($\Lambda$) configuration driven by a bichromatic optical field, modeled using a Hamiltonian and governed by the quantum Liouville equation. The analysis focuses on ground-state density matrix elements.

**Key observables.** Frequency shift of the error signal ($\delta_{as}$ or $\delta_Q$), which is derived from the difference in absorption measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Integration time ($	au$), modulation parameters ($m, \omega_m$), and relaxation rates ($\Gamma_c, \Gamma_g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes unisotropic relaxation for ground-state elements and neglects Doppler broadening compared to the homogeneous width.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the frequency shift ($\delta_{tot}(	au)$) vs. integration time $	au$, showing agreement between techniques at short times, and plot the dependence of the normalized slope on the parameter $eta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory for both phase-jump spectroscopy and conventional harmonic modulation separately before performing a detailed comparison of the resulting frequency shift formulas and numerical results across varying parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the research of dynamic continuous-wave spectroscopy of the coherent population trapping resonance at the phase-jump modulation. Λ system of levels supplemented by a nonabsorbing state and bichromatic optical field, whose spectral components have different intensities, are considered. We demonstrate that the asymmetry leads to an additional nonlinear shift of the error-signal frequency under unisotropic relaxation of the ground-state density-matrix elements. We also investigate the conventional approach where the frequency difference of the optical field components is harmonically modulated to obtain the error signal. Comparison demonstrates that in the high-frequency modulation regime the corresponding frequency shift is more linear than at the phase-jump modulation for nonshort integration times.

</details>
