**Summary.** The research reports on using optical spectroscopy to characterize ultracold Bose-Einstein condensates of $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms. By measuring spectral features in both dark-field and bright-field configurations, the authors developed a model to extract temperature and atom numbers directly within the trap. The findings establish that dark-field spectroscopy is a highly sensitive and superior tool for characterizing these quantum gases at very low temperatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an advanced, non-destructive, in-situ diagnostic tool for characterizing quantum gas mixtures at extremely low temperatures, offering quantitative metrics (T, $N_{th}$, $N_0$) that complement traditional time-of-flight methods.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize ultracold, partially condensed atomic gases by measuring distinct spectral features arising from both thermal and Bose-Einstein condensed components.

**Main result.** Dark-field optical spectroscopy proves to be a superior technique compared to bright-field measurements for characterizing these systems, showing good agreement with Time-of-Flight imaging for temperature and atom numbers.

**Method.** The experiment uses an in-situ dark-field configuration with single-photon detection and a frequency-agile laser system to measure spectral profiles as a function of probe detuning.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a cloud of $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms trapped in a crossed optical dipole trap. The analysis relies on developing a model for light propagation through this inhomogeneous atomic cloud, incorporating density components ($ho = ho_0 + ho_{th}$).

**Key observables.** Spectral features (intensity profiles) measured as a function of probe frequency detuning ($\delta$); extracted parameters include temperature ($T$), thermal atom number ($N_{th}$), and condensed atom number ($N_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (nK range); the ratio between thermal and condensed fractions; spectral detuning ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a thin-lens approximation for light propagation, which is noted as a potential source of systematic deviation in cold spectra.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup (schematics), simulated BF and DF spectra for partially condensed clouds, and comparisons between spectroscopically derived parameters and those from TOF absorption imaging.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first detailing the measurement setup and spectral diagnostics in both dark-field and bright-field configurations. It then develops the theoretical model for light propagation through the inhomogeneous cloud, followed by a detailed comparison of results against conventional Time-of-Flight measurements to validate the spectroscopic technique.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report on optical spectroscopic measurements of ultracold and partially condensed 87Rb gases, which show distinct spectral features due to the thermal and the Bose condensed components in the frequency domain. These features are detected in-situ by using a dark-field configuration with single-photon sensitivity and a frequency-agile laser system. To interpret the observed spectra, we develop a model for light propagation through an inhomogeneous atomic cloud. This model enables the extraction of temperature and atom number, which we benchmark against conventional time-of-flight absorption imaging. The spectroscopically obtained cloud parameters show enhanced sensitivity to small thermal fractions in nearly pure condensates. We further compare the spectroscopy in dark-field and bright-field configurations, demonstrating the superior performance of the former. Our results reveal previously unexplored spectral structure in optically dense ultracold gases and establish spectroscopy as a tool for characterizing ultracold systems at very low temperatures.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose using spin qubits confined in altermagnetic quantum dots as a novel route to gate control. By exploiting the momentum-dependent spin splitting inherent to altermagnets, they demonstrate high-fidelity single and two-qubit gates purely through electrostatic confinement modulation. This work is significant because it offers a pathway for solid-state quantum computation that circumvents the need for problematic spin-orbit coupling terms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, solid-state platform for realizing qubits whose fundamental operations (single and two-qubit gates) are governed purely by magnetic structure engineering rather than relying on SOC or external fields in complex ways.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes using gate-defined spin qubits within electrostatically confined altermagnetic quantum dots to achieve local, controlled qubit operations without relying on spin-orbit coupling.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that anisotropic altermagnets allow for arbitrary single-qubit rotations and coherent two-qubit entanglement generation by modulating the confinement anisotropy or interdot barrier, respectively.

**Method.** The study employs atomistic real-space tight-binding models combined with time-dependent simulations and truncated Configuration Interaction (CI) methods to calculate quantum dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is based on electrons confined in altermagnetic quantum dots (single or double). The Hamiltonian incorporates hopping terms derived from the d-wave altermagnetic structure, confinement potentials ($	ext{HV}$), and time-dependent gate drives.

**Key observables.** Single-qubit: Qubit energy gap ($\sim$ microwave range) and spin polarization. Two-qubit: Logical concurrence ($C_{	ext{log}}$) and Bell fidelity ($F_{	ext{Bell}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ellipticity parameter $\delta$, gate drive amplitude ($A_Q$), detuning ($\epsilon$), and central barrier modulation strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on an idealized elliptical confinement model, and the control mechanism is shown to function without requiring spin-orbit coupling (SOC).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic setups for single/double dots, show qubit splitting vs. ellipticity ($\delta$), and detail time evolution dynamics showing high fidelity in both single-qubit rotations and two-qubit Bell state preparation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical platform using elliptical confinement to create a spin doublet. It then details single-qubit control via resonant quadrupolar driving, followed by an extension to double quantum dots where central-barrier modulation drives the system into maximally entangled two-qubit states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose gate-defined spin qubits in electrostatically confined altermagnetic quantum dots. Elliptical confinement of the $d$-wave altermagnetic structure produces a low-energy doublet with opposite spin polarization. For the range of parameters used here, the qubit states energy gap lies in the microwave range while the leakage gap remains in the meV range. Even without spin-orbit coupling, time-dependent simulations show that a phase-controlled quadrupolar gate drive about a fixed bias point implements $X_{π/2}$ and $X_π$ rotations by resonantly modulating the confinement anisotropy. We extend the study to two-qubits using a double quantum dot. We show that the double quantum dot spectrum can be cleanly projected onto isolated quantum dot product states with a nonzero nonlocal Pauli block in the effective logical two-qubit Hamiltonian. Resonant central-barrier modulation then drives the logical two-qubit component close to a maximally entangled state. These calculations show anisotropic altermagnetic quantum dots as a route to locally gate-controlled spin qubits without requiring spin-orbit coupling.

</details>

### [A no-go theorem for privacy in distributed sensing using Gaussian states](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23796v1)

**Authors:** Jason L. Pereira, Damian Markham  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.23796v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2606.23796_figures/2606.23796_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1: The basic setup for a distributed sensing problem. For privacy, we want to choose the parametrised unitaries, ui, so that the encoded state only holds information about a linear function of the parameters, and not their individual values.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2606.23796_figures/2606.23796_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2: An illustration of the necessary condition for privacy stated in Theorem 2. If there exists a pair of unitaries, u and v, that collectively act as the identity, when applied to nodes 1 and 2, then u must act as the identity on all possible conditional states after tracing out node 2 and carrying out a measurement on all nodes except for 1 and 2.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2606.23796_figures/2606.23796_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3: Privacy can be restored in a trivial way by allowing each pair of parties to establish pairwise correlations. This can be achieved if each party holds multiple nodes of a graph state, as illustrated here. If each party measures those of their nodes that are correlated with other nodes, they can use the outcomes to encode their remaining node in such a way that their secret parameter remains private.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a no-go theorem proving that perfect privacy cannot be achieved in distributed quantum sensing when Gaussian states are used as resources. The authors develop and analyze a quantitative measure called relative privacy to characterize the maximum achievable information leakage instead of absolute secrecy. This result sets fundamental theoretical bounds for quantum sensing protocols using continuous variables.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical limits on the security of quantum communication protocols in continuous variable settings, which is highly relevant for quantum sensing applications using optical resources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors investigate whether perfect privacy can be achieved when multiple parties use quantum resource states, specifically Gaussian states, to perform distributed sensing.

**Main result.** They prove a no-go theorem showing that perfect privacy is impossible for any distributed sensing protocol utilizing Gaussian states. They instead introduce and analyze a measure of relative privacy.

**Method.** The analysis uses necessary conditions derived from the requirement that the encoded state must only reveal information about the global function, not individual local parameters. This involves analyzing conditional states and Quantum Fisher Information (QFI).

**Model / system.** The system models distributed sensing among N parties sharing a quantum resource state, focusing specifically on continuous variable Gaussian graph states.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) matrix, the relative privacy measure ($R_{xy}$), and the encoded state $|\psi_{	ext{enc}}(\{	heta_i\})angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties $N$, the structure of the graph state (e.g., fully-connected), and the transmissivity of loss channels.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially focuses on unitary encoding channels, though extensions to general channel encoding are discussed; perfect privacy is assumed impossible for Gaussian states.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the basic distributed sensing setup. Figure 3 shows how pairwise correlations can restore some form of privacy in an example setting.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing necessary conditions for private encoding (Theorem 2) to showing these fail for Gaussian states, and finally introduces relative privacy ($R_{xy}$) as a quantifiable measure of achievable information leakage.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the discrete variable setting, entangled resource states allow a set of parties to learn a global function of a set of spatially separated systems, whilst keeping the local parameters of those systems completely private. In the continuous variable setting, distributed sensing has been carried out using Gaussian resource states, but without the same guarantees about privacy. Here, we show that perfect privacy is impossible to achieve for any distributed sensing protocol that uses Gaussian states as a resource. We also introduce a measure of relative privacy, bounding the degree to which any Gaussian distributed sensing protocol can keep local parameters hidden.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes Speculative Window Decoding to boost the speed of error correction in fault-tolerant quantum computers. By simulating performance under varying gate speeds and hardware limitations, it establishes critical design rules: speculation benefits are highly dependent on whether the underlying quantum hardware is fast or slow. The findings guide researchers on when speculative techniques are beneficial versus when simpler, non-speculative methods are superior.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QEC hardware optimization, understanding resource bottlenecks (latency vs. processing power) and algorithmic trade-offs (speculation vs. certainty) is crucial for designing efficient quantum control systems applicable across various physical platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes Speculative Window Decoding (SWD) techniques to improve the real-time performance of error decoding required for Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computing (FTQC). It aims to determine when speculative methods provide the greatest benefit across varying hardware constraints.

**Main result.** Performance heavily depends on gate speed and processor count: non-speculative decoding outperforms SWD under fast gates with low accuracy, while SWD consistently excels for slow gates. A 'Shallow Speculations First' ordering strategy is proposed for optimization.

**Method.** The authors use a modified simulator (SWIPER-SIM) to model the QEC decoding process, comparing speculative versus non-speculative approaches under varying gate speeds and resource limitations.

**Model / system.** The context is Quantum Error Correction (QEC) using syndrome measurements. The analysis compares performance across superconducting qubits (fast gates) against regimes simulating slower quantum technologies.

**Key observables.** Total decoding time, window backlog, average conditional wait time, and UI window count.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate speed (fast vs. slow), processor count, speculation accuracy, decoder latency, and workload parallelism.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Prior work was limited to superconducting qubits; the study extends evaluation to slower gate speeds and varying code/decoder factors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate performance metrics versus processor count for fast/slow gates (Fig 1), total decoding time dependence on latency and accuracy (Fig 2), and comparisons between speculative vs. non-speculative timing under various conditions (Figs 3, 4, 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically evaluates SWD by first establishing the performance landscape across gate speeds and processor counts, then analyzing sensitivity to latency and accuracy, culminating in design principles for optimal speculation strategies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computing is essential for realizing the substantial computational speedups that quantum computing can bring, but it requires real-time error decoding with high performance. Speculative window decoding improves performance by reducing the time spent waiting for dependencies from prior decoding windows.   However, speculative decoders have only been evaluated under the regime of superconducting qubits with fast gate speeds, surface codes, and matching decoders. Since different quantum technologies can have slower gate speeds, we evaluate the performance of speculative decoding under slow gate speeds. We also examine its sensitivity to speculation accuracy, decoder latency, processor count, and workload parallelism, which can vary across different quantum error correction codes, decoders, and hardware platforms.   This work presents design principles for identifying when speculative decoding yields the greatest performance improvements. It also reveals the conditions under which non-speculative decoders outperform speculative decoders.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents advanced theoretical protocols for preparing Matrix Product States (MPS) on a quantum computer. By integrating techniques like oblivious amplitude amplification and quasi-probabilistic decomposition, the authors achieve state-of-the-art scaling in circuit depth while ensuring exact preparation fidelity. This advances the practical feasibility of simulating complex quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides concrete, resource-aware quantum circuit blueprints for simulating complex many-body physics models that are computationally intractable classically.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is developing quantum circuit protocols that can prepare highly entangled Matrix Product States (MPS) with high fidelity and minimal circuit depth.

**Main result.** The authors achieve state-of-the-art, exact preparation protocols for MPS, significantly improving the scaling of circuit depth dependence on both bond dimension ($\chi$) and system size ($L$).

**Method.** They combine techniques like block-encoded correction maps, generalizations of oblivious amplitude amplification (OAAI), and quasi-probabilistic decomposition (QPD) to deterministically implement required isometries.

**Model / system.** The focus is on preparing MPS states $|	ext{A}(L)angle$, which are efficient representations of quantum many-body ground states. The analysis involves tensor network manipulations, renormalization group flow concepts, and circuit depth scaling laws.

**Key observables.** Circuit depth (scaling with $\chi$ and $L$), success probability ($p_{	ext{succ}}$), residual norms ($\|R(q)\|$), and the required block size ($q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), System size ($L$), Error tolerance ($\epsilon$), Correlation length ($\xi$), and Block size parameters ($q, Q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on asymptotic scaling limits (e.g., large $L$ or small $\delta$) and the assumption that certain error terms decay exponentially with block size.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit constructions for TI MPS preparation, comparing different protocols like All-at-Once vs. Split-and-Concatenate methods, and showing optimization landscapes for determining optimal block sizes ($Q \propto \sqrt{L'}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically improves the protocol by first establishing approximate RG methods (Chunk 2), then introducing exact probabilistic corrections (Chunk 3), optimizing the implementation of isometries via amplitude amplification (Chunk 4), and finally refining the depth scaling using quasi-probabilistic techniques (Chunks 5, 6, 7, 8).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Preparing matrix product states (MPS) on a quantum device is a key subroutine in many quantum algorithms. The most competitive methods, based on the renormalisation group, prepare translationally invariant MPS of size $L$ and bond dimension $χ$, up to an error $\varepsilon$, in circuit depth $\tilde O(χ^{4}\log(L/\varepsilon))$ or $\tilde O(χ^{6}\log\log(L/\varepsilon))$. We improve multiple aspects of these methods. First, using block-encoded correction maps, whose post-selection succeeds with constant probability, we render the preparation exact without sacrificing the scaling in $L$. Second, through a generalisation of oblivious amplitude amplification to isometries, we reduce the bond-dimension dependence, improving the depth to $\tilde O(χ^{2}\log L + χ^{4})$ or $\tilde O(χ^{2}\log\log L + χ^{4})$, and even to $\tilde O(χ^{3}\log L)$ for incoherent preparations. Finally, we extend the framework to non-translationally invariant MPS and prove logarithmic-depth exact preparation for independent and identically distributed random tensor sequences. Confirmed by numerical studies, these results constitute, to the best of our knowledge, the most efficient exact MPS preparation protocols in the relevant parameter regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates High-Harmonic Generation (HHG) in two-electron systems, extending the process beyond conventional single-electron limits. By generalizing the Strong-Field Approximation to account for simultaneous double recombination, the authors predict an extended harmonic plateau reaching into the soft x-ray keV regime. This capability is crucial for generating sub-attosecond pulses vital for probing ultrafast dynamics across matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of strong-field physics by modeling multi-electron dynamics in a quantum optical context. The extension into the keV regime opens pathways for soft x-ray spectroscopy using attosecond pulses, which is a major focus area in AMO and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to extend High-Harmonic Generation (HHG) into the keV soft x-ray regime by incorporating two-electron interaction effects.

**Main result.** Two-electron processes allow the harmonic spectrum to significantly exceed the single-electron cutoff, reaching photon energies up to approximately 1.2 keV.

**Method.** The study employs a generalization of the Strong-Field Approximation (SFA) for two active electrons and utilizes the saddle-point method to solve complex momentum integrals derived from the effective action.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a two-electron atomic structure, specifically Helium, driven by an intense few-cycle infrared laser pulse. The dynamics are governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian incorporating both atomic binding and laser interaction terms.

**Key observables.** High-harmonic spectrum ($	ilde{r}(\Omega)$), the effective action ($S_{eff}$), and the two-electron dipole moment $\langle r(t) angle_{2e}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Laser field parameters (e.g., $800 	ext{ nm}$, intense fields); energy scales reaching up to $1.2 	ext{ keV}$; ponderomotive energy ($U_p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the dipole approximation, neglects nuclear motion and relativistic effects, and uses approximations like neglecting rescattering terms in intermediate steps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic process of Non-sequential double recombination (NSDR), show the extended HHG spectra up to 1.2 keV, and display reconstructed attosecond pulse profiles derived from these high-energy harmonics.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the two-electron Hamiltonian and generalizing SFA, through deriving coupled differential equations for single and double electron amplitudes, applying the saddle-point method to solve for the effective action, and finally calculating the Fourier transform of the resulting time-dependent dipole moment to yield the spectrum.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-electron processes can generate high harmonics beyond the conventional single-active-electron cutoff. Motivated by recent experimental evidence of an extended secondary plateau in the helium high-harmonic spectrum [S. Wang et al, Optica, (2023); S. Wang et al, In Print in Nature Photon., (2026)], we present a two-electron generalisation of the strong-field approximation. We analyse the resulting expressions using the saddle-point method and determine the extended cutoff. We find good agreement with classical predictions of cutoff scalings of $4.7$ and $5.5$ times the ponderomotive energy, which significantly exceed the established single-electron scaling of 3.17. We calculate high-harmonic spectra generated via a two-electron process in helium atoms driven by an intense few-cycle infrared laser pulse. Our results demonstrate that the harmonic spectrum extends far beyond the water window, reaching photon energies up to $\approx 1.2\,\mathrm{keV}$ in the soft x-ray region. The large spectral bandwidth can support the generation of sub-attosecond soft x-ray pulses, which are of particular interest for probing ultrafast dynamics across matter, including applications in core-level spectroscopy and biological imaging.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proves that the fundamental rate of communication capacity in interactive quantum protocols is determined equally by two measures: the Quantum Information Cost and the Amortized Quantum Communication Complexity. This equivalence is achieved using a novel type-constrained de Finetti reduction, which simplifies complex worst-case analysis into manageable i.i.d. bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep theoretical tools—the type-constrained de Finetti reduction—that bridge classical probability theory with quantum information bounds, offering a powerful new technique for analyzing resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish an equivalence between two fundamental measures of communication capacity: the prior-free quantum information cost (QIC) and the prior-free amortized quantum communication complexity (AQCC). This addresses a long-standing open question in quantum information theory.

**Main result.** It proves that for interactive quantum communication protocols with classical inputs, the Quantum Information Cost equals the Amortized Quantum Communication Complexity ($	ext{QIC}(\Pi) = 	ext{AQCC}(\Pi)$).

**Method.** The core methodology involves developing and applying a novel type-constrained de Finetti reduction to simplify complex worst-case input analyses into tractable i.i.d. settings.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on quantum interactive communication protocols involving multiple rounds of communication, where the inputs are classical sequences drawn from fixed alphabets. The mathematical framework utilizes density operators and tensor products over Hilbert spaces.

**Key observables.** Quantum Information Cost ($	ext{QIC}$), Amortized Quantum Communication Complexity ($	ext{AQCC}$), total quantum communication cost ($QCC$), and various conditional mutual information terms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The asymptotic limit ($n 	o \infty$) is crucial, as are the approximation parameters $\delta$ (controlling typicality) and the structure of the classical inputs/types.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the protocols operate with classical inputs drawn from fixed alphabets and relies heavily on bounding errors using $\delta$-typical sets derived from de Finetti's theorem.

**Figures summary.** Figure 4 illustrates the general structure of an interactive quantum communication protocol involving multiple rounds ($r$) with classical inputs, which is the system under analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically by first establishing the type-constrained de Finetti reduction for classical distributions (Chunk 1). It then applies this to bound channel norms and state approximations (Chunks 2 & 7), culminating in simulating the full interactive protocol using these bounds to prove the final equivalence $	ext{QIC}(\Pi) = 	ext{AQCC}(\Pi)$ (Chunks 3, 5, & 6).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For many information processing tasks, de Finetti-style theorems can often simplify the analysis in worst-case input scenarios for which the task exhibits some permutation-invariance symmetry, as they can allow for a reduction from an analysis on worst-case inputs to that of i.i.d. inputs. If further information is available on the inputs, it might be advantageous to reflect this information in the de Finetti reduction. In our work, we focus on a form of such constraint, based on the type of the input. This allows us to obtain a conceptually simple proof of a new de Finetti reduction for classical probability distributions, derived from elementary properties from the method of types. We apply our constrained de Finetti reduction to the compression of quantum interactive communication protocols with classical inputs, and prove that the prior-free quantum information cost equals the worst-case input amortized quantum communication cost.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how the connectivity structure of IQP quantum circuits affects their ability to solve complex Hamiltonian optimization problems. It establishes a crucial trade-off: highly expressive, fully connected circuits are prone to vanishing gradients (barren plateaus), while less expressive but stable circuits perform poorly. The authors conclude that an intermediate connectivity, like circular coupling, offers the most robust balance for near-term quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical bottleneck in variational quantum algorithms—the conflict between achieving high representational power (expressivity) and maintaining gradients that allow for efficient classical optimization (trainability).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the fundamental trade-off between optimization performance (expressivity) and training stability (trainability) when using Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial-time (IQP) circuits for solving Hamiltonian optimization problems.

**Main result.** A clear trade-off exists: fully connected circuits offer high expressivity but suffer from rapid gradient decay (barren plateaus), while single-Z circuits are stable but under-expressive; circular connectivity provides the best balance.

**Method.** The authors systematically compared three IQP circuit architectures (single-Z, circular, full) by minimizing expectation values of benchmark Hamiltonians and analyzing gradient variance scaling with system size.

**Model / system.** The model uses IQP circuits as variational ansätze to approximate the ground state energy of various combinatorial Hamiltonians, such as Ising or MaxCut models. The analysis focuses on how circuit connectivity dictates the optimization landscape's properties.

**Key observables.** Relative approximation ratio (optimization performance), gradient variance decay rate (trainability/barren plateau susceptibility), expressibility (Jensen-Shannon distance), and bipartite entanglement entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit connectivity structure (Single-Z, Circular, Full); System size (number of qubits $n$); Optimization parameters ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the trade-off between expressivity and trainability is a fundamental constraint for near-term quantum advantage in optimization tasks.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how connectivity affects expressibility (approaching Haar-random behavior) and entanglement entropy, while gradient variance plots show full connectivity decaying fastest, indicating barren plateaus.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces IQP circuits for Hamiltonian optimization, analyzes performance metrics across different connectivities using benchmark Hamiltonians, and concludes by mapping out the necessary balance between expressivity and trainability required for practical quantum algorithms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial-time (IQP) circuits are promising candidates for near-term quantum advantage due to the conjectured classical hardness of their sampling task. However, their capabilities for optimization remain largely unexplored. We present a systematic investigation of the performance and trainability of IQP circuits for Hamiltonian optimization. Our results reveal a trade-off between optimization performance and circuit connectivity, demonstrating that the circuit structure plays a key role in determining the ability of IQP circuits to reach low-energy states.

</details>

**Authors:** Boyang Chen, Andrea Coladangelo, Yao-Ting Lin, Nikos Skoumios, Justin Tysdal, Yiming Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24736v1>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves a black-box separation demonstrating that quantum pseudorandom states cannot be stretched to arbitrary polynomial lengths. By constructing an adversary oracle, the authors show that 1PRS outputs are fundamentally limited in how much their output length can increase relative to their input key size. This result places strong theoretical constraints on the efficiency and scalability of quantum randomness sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, this work establishes fundamental computational limits on quantum randomness generation, which underpins many theoretical models for quantum computation security.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining if quantum pseudorandom states (1PRS) can be stretched to arbitrarily large polynomial output lengths in a black-box setting, analogous to classical PRGs.

**Main result.** The authors prove a fundamental black-box separation showing that 1PRS cannot be stretched from a short length ($m(n)=1.1n$) to an arbitrarily long one ($\Omega(n^{2+\epsilon})$).

**Method.** The proof constructs a quantum oracle and utilizes advanced techniques involving the Common Haar Random State (CHRS) model, SWAP tests, and OR testers to distinguish between 1PRS of different output lengths.

**Model / system.** The work operates within the framework of quantum information theory, focusing on Single-Copy Secure Pseudorandom States (1PRS) generated relative to a Common Haar Random State (CHRS).

**Key observables.** Distinguishing advantage between 1PRS outputs and Haar random states; the required output length $m(n)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The stretch factor, comparing linear growth ($1.1n$) against super-polynomial growth ($\Omega(n^{2+\epsilon})$); the security parameter $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The separation is established relative to a constructed quantum oracle and holds under specific black-box access models (e.g., isometry access).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 visually categorizes different theoretical cases (Case A and Case B) based on the lengths of available CHRS states ($i$) and the number of copies ($\ell(i)$) used by the generator.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds its argument through several stages: first, establishing the general black-box separation; second, analyzing specific cases (A and B) based on resource availability; and finally, combining these analyses into a unified adversary that proves the impossibility of stretching.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pseudorandom states, introduced by Ji, Liu, and Song (CRYPTO '18), are quantum analogues of classical pseudorandom generators. A fundamental property of classical pseudorandom generators is that their output can be stretched to arbitrary polynomial length. Whether an analogous stretching property holds for quantum pseudorandom states remains unclear.   In this work, we prove the first black-box separation between single-copy secure pseudorandom states ($\mathsf{1PRS}$) with different output lengths. Specifically, we construct a quantum oracle relative to which $\mathsf{1PRS}$ with output length $m(n)=1.1n$ exist, but $\mathsf{1PRS}$ with output length $m(n)=Ω(n^{2+ε})$ do not, for any $ε>0$. Our proof leverages the Common Haar Random State (CHRS) model introduced by Chen, Coladangelo, and Sattath (EUROCRYPT '25), and introduces a technique to bound the effective number of resource CHRS states utilized by any $\mathsf{1PRS}$ generator in this model.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel framework using differential moment relaxations to rigorously certify the limitations of finite-time quantum control. It calculates upper bounds on achievable fidelities and lower bounds on minimum required times for tasks like state preparation and gate synthesis. This provides 'hardware-aware' speed limits, distinguishing true physical constraints from mere optimization shortcomings.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful, mathematically rigorous tools for quantum control that move beyond purely numerical optimization by providing certified limits on performance, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum hardware protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing rigorous mathematical certificates for determining the maximum achievable fidelity (reachability) and the minimum time required to reach a target state or gate in constrained quantum control.

**Main result.** The authors establish differential moment hierarchies that provide computable upper bounds on terminal fidelities and corresponding lower bounds on optimal control times, thereby certifying dynamical limitations.

**Method.** The framework employs moment relaxations applied to noncommutative polynomial optimization problems with implicitly time-dependent differential constraints, formulated as Semidefinite Programs (SDPs).

**Model / system.** The work analyzes controlled quantum dynamics governed by Hamiltonians $H(t) = H_0 + \sum u_j(t)H_j$, applied to tasks like state preparation (Bell/GHZ states), gate synthesis ($	ext{SU}(2)$ evolution), and excitation transfer in spin chains.

**Key observables.** Terminal fidelities ($F_{\Phi^+}, F_{	ext{gate}}, F_{	ext{tr}}$) are the primary observables, alongside the optimal control time $T^\star$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Control constraints ($\sum u_j^2 \le U_{\max}^2$), relaxation order ($d$), and target fidelity threshold ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on moment relaxation techniques, which provide upper bounds; the quality of these bounds depends on the chosen relaxation order $d$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of reachable sets, showing how nested convex outer approximations (from the hierarchy) bound the true set, and compare certified lower/upper bounds for state preparation fidelity vs. time.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a unified framework by first establishing the mathematical machinery (moment relaxations with differential constraints), then applying it to specific physical tasks: state preparation, gate synthesis, and state transfer, demonstrating how structural symmetries reduce computational complexity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Finite-time control is central to quantum technologies, yet rigorous limits on reachable targets and optimal control times remain largely unknown. We develop a framework for finite-time reachability and optimal-time certificates in constrained quantum control based on moment relaxations with implicitly time-dependent differential constraints. For fixed control horizons and control constraints, the method yields rigorous upper bounds on achievable terminal fidelities, lower bounds on the optimal control times required to reach them, and certificate gaps for benchmarking explicit control pulses. We demonstrate the versatility of our framework in three use cases: entangled-state preparation in two and three qubits, one-qubit gate synthesis across different control geometries, and excitation transfer in an $N$-qubit $XX$ chain. Our work establishes differential moment hierarchies as a practical tool for certifying reachability limits and optimal control times in quantum control, providing hardware-aware quantum speed limits while highlighting structure exploitation as a key ingredient for scalable certification.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Structured Concept Evolution (SCE), an LLM-guided evolutionary framework designed to overcome the difficulty of constructing good quantum LDPC codes. By allowing the LLM to mutate high-level algebraic concepts rather than raw matrices, the method discovers diverse and competitive code families over both abelian and non-abelian groups. The results demonstrate a powerful AI tool for advancing the theoretical design of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum error correction codes rather than AMO dynamics, the underlying principle of using advanced AI (LLMs) to navigate complex, high-dimensional combinatorial search spaces for physical constraints is highly relevant to designing optimal quantum control or simulation protocols in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is discovering high-performing quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes because the search space for their construction is vast and discrete.

**Main result.** The authors successfully used an LLM-guided evolutionary framework to discover diverse, competitive families of qLDPC codes spanning both abelian and non-abelian group structures.

**Method.** They introduced Structured Concept Evolution (SCE), which pairs a Large Language Model with a structured algebraic mutation grammar to evolve code concepts at three hierarchical levels: local entries, protograph shape, and base group algebra.

**Model / system.** The focus is on constructing CSS quantum LDPC codes using the lifted-product construction based on finite groups G. These codes are evaluated for error correction capability against depolarizing noise.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate ($\epsilon_L$) vs. physical error rate ($p$), encoding rate ($k/n$), and fitness scores derived from decoding performance (BP+OSD).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rates $p \in \{0.05, 0.06, 0.07\}$; code parameters $[[n, k, d]]$; stabilizer weight threshold $\le 14$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The search relies on LLMs (GPT-5.4 variants) acting as intelligent mutation operators constrained by a structured algebraic grammar.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SCE framework flow, showing parent-to-child mutations across three levels (L1, L2, L3), and plot the block-length dependence of logical failure rates for various discovered code families.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the SCE methodology involving structured concepts and hierarchical mutation grammar, presents results showing diverse code discovery spanning abelian to non-abelian groups, and concludes with performance comparisons across different noise models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computers could outperform classical machines on important problems, but only if the errors that pervade quantum hardware can be corrected at scale. Quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes offer a promising route to this goal by combining sparse parity checks with finite encoding rate and growing distance, but their construction remains a challenging discrete design problem. Here we introduce structured concept evolution (SCE), a search framework that pairs a large language model with a structured algebraic mutation grammar to discover lifted-product code families, a class of CSS qLDPC codes. Instead of asking the LLM to design codes from first principles, SCE evolves structured concepts consisting of algebraic specifications paired with executable programs that realize them, using hierarchical mutations that modify the group algebra, protograph geometry, or base space. Running SCE, we discover a diverse set of competitive code families, ranging from abelian constructions to families over non-abelian groups beyond those underlying standard designs such as bivariate-bicycle codes, and characterize them under code-capacity depolarizing noise with BP+OSD decoding. These results are obtained with lightweight models (GPT-5.4-mini and GPT-5.4-nano).

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Augmented Imaginary-Time Evolution (AITE) to accelerate the notoriously slow convergence of standard imaginary-time evolution algorithms. By incorporating local geometric information derived from energy distribution statistics, AITE achieves superlinear convergence leading to finite-time error extinction. This provides a significant algorithmic improvement for simulating ground states in quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful algorithmic acceleration technique for finding ground states in strongly correlated systems, directly impacting computational methods used in condensed matter physics simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard imaginary-time evolution (ITE) for ground-state preparation converges only asymptotically, leading to slow convergence rates.

**Main result.** The proposed Augmented Imaginary-Time Evolution (AITE) framework achieves superlinear convergence and exhibits finite-time extinction of the energy error, significantly outperforming standard ITE.

**Method.** AITE augments the standard gradient flow by incorporating local geometric information derived from higher-order statistical moments (like skewness) of the instantaneous energy distribution into the descent direction.

**Model / system.** The methods are applied to general quantum many-body systems, with specific numerical tests performed on the H8 chain and the Fermi-Hubbard model. The evolution is governed by imaginary time ($	au$) under a Hamiltonian $\hat{H}$.

**Key observables.** Energy error $E(	au) - E_{	ext{GS}}$, energy variance ($\mu_2$), and skewness coefficient ($\kappa$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral gap ($\Delta = E_1 - E_{GS}$), the imaginary time $	au$, and the target precision $\epsilon^*$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Practical implementation restricts the search for optimal descent operators to the second-order Krylov subspace, and mean-field approximations are used for higher moments.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy error vs. time ($	au$) curves, showing AITE's power-law extinction against ITE's asymptotic exponential decay across various models (H8 chain, Fermi-Hubbard).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem with standard ITE, develops the AITE framework by generalizing the flow using geometric information ($\hat{O}(	au)$), analyzes the convergence rate improvement leading to finite-time extinction, and discusses practical approximations like mean-field moment estimation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Imaginary-time evolution (ITE) underpins a broad family of algorithms for ground-state preparation in quantum simulation and quantum many-body physics. In these methods, convergence is governed by the energy variance of the instantaneous state, causing the flow to approach the ground state only asymptotically. We introduce an augmented imaginary-time evolution (AITE) framework that replaces the standard gradient flow on the energy landscape with a geometrically informed descent along locally optimal directions, which are identified by exploiting the higher-order statistical structure of the instantaneous energy distribution. The resulting flow strictly outperforms standard ITE throughout the entire evolution and exhibits two qualitatively distinct regimes: a superlinear convergence regime, followed by an extinction regime in which the energy error vanishes exactly at a finite imaginary time, in sharp contrast to the asymptotic exponential decay of ITE. Standard ITE is recovered in the zero-skewness limit of AITE, implying that the acceleration extends naturally across the broader ITE algorithmic family.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes the effective channel impairments in free-space CV-QKD when light passes through atmospheric turbulence and is coupled into an SMF. It demonstrates that while the standard protocol (GG02) can be accurately modeled using a scalar coupling law, protocols utilizing a reference signal require tracking differential phase mismatches, which introduces a distinct, measurable noise penalty.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges complex wave optics simulations with information-theoretic security bounds, providing concrete physical constraints (the need for differential observables) that must be accounted for in practical quantum communication system design.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the physically relevant effective channel variable for Continuous-Variable Quantum Key Distribution (CV-QKD) when atmospheric turbulence and Single-Mode Fiber (SMF) reception are involved.

**Main result.** For standard GG02, a realistic scalar law for SMF coupling efficiency suffices; however, pilot-assisted protocols require an additional protocol-dependent reference observable due to signal-pilot phase mismatch.

**Method.** The authors employ full wave-optics simulations (split-step propagation) followed by deriving effective Gaussian channel closures based on analyzing the necessary statistical moments of the optical observables.

**Model / system.** The system is a free-space CV-QKD link propagating through atmospheric turbulence, which is modeled via random phase screens and subsequent finite-aperture collection onto an SMF guided mode. The analysis compares simple mean-loss approximations against more rigorous moment closures.

**Key observables.** Effective coupling efficiency ($\eta_{smf}$), relative phase ($\Delta\phi$), complex coherence indicator ($
u$), and the resulting key rate (bits/pulse).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Atmospheric turbulence strength ($C_2^n$), propagation distance ($L=1000$ m), and signal-pilot time delay ($\Delta t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on developing effective physical models (closures) for the channel statistics, rather than providing a complete composable security proof. The mean-loss model is used as a deliberately simple control case.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare full optical model results against simplified approximations (mean loss vs. scalar surrogates) for GG02, and show that pilot-assisted key rates degrade significantly with increasing signal-pilot delay ($\Delta t$) due to differential phase variance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first modeling the full wave optics, then establishing effective closures for the standard GG02 setting using scalar surrogates. It extends this analysis to pilot-assisted architectures, where it introduces and quantifies an additional protocol-dependent reference noise penalty based on differential observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study free-space continuous-variable quantum key distribution (CV-QKD) with single-mode fiber (SMF) reception under atmospheric turbulence. The optical channel is modeled by split-step propagation through random phase screens, followed by finite-aperture collection and projection onto the guided receiving mode. We first examine the standard GG02 setting and ask which receiver-side observable is sufficient for effective key-rate prediction. We show that a mean-loss description is generally too optimistic, whereas a scalar effective law for the SMF coupling efficiency provides an accurate downstream Gaussian-channel description within the effective model considered here. We then extend the optical model to a pilot-assisted architecture in which the signal and pilot propagate through correlated but non-identical turbulent realizations generated by a frozen-flow construction. In this case, the signal coupling law alone is no longer sufficient: signal--pilot phase mismatch and loss of post-coupling coherence produce an additional protocol-dependent reference-noise penalty. The results distinguish two regimes: a scalar coupling description is largely adequate for GG02, while transmitted-reference architectures require an additional differential reference observable beyond the signal coupling statistics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a deterministic quantum algorithm based on Gray codes to prepare complex multi-qudit quantum states within specific weight subspaces. By decomposing the state preparation into sequential controlled rotations, it successfully applies this method to generate exact eigenstates of Hamiltonians relevant to strongly correlated physics, such as Bethe and Dicke states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete quantum computational blueprint—a universal gate sequence—for preparing highly entangled, symmetry-protected many-body eigenstates (like Bethe or Dicke states) that are central to understanding quantum magnetism and interacting systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to formulate a deterministic quantum algorithm for preparing arbitrary multi-qudit states within a specific definite-weight subspace.

**Main result.** A concrete, gate-based procedure using Gray codes and controlled rotations (Gray gates) is established to prepare these complex entangled states, including Bethe and Dicke states.

**Method.** The method maps the state preparation problem onto traversing an ordered sequence of basis states defined by a multiset permutation Gray code. Each transition requires applying a specific controlled unitary rotation (the 'Gray gate').

**Model / system.** The system involves $n$-qudit systems using $d$-dimensional qudits, whose states are characterized by weight vectors $\vec{k}$. The target states are eigenstates of various Hamiltonians, such as the SU(3)-invariant Heisenberg Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** State amplitudes ($a_w$) defining the superposition coefficients, and the eigenvalues of the relevant symmetry-preserving Hamiltonians (e.g., $E = -rac{1}{2}n(n-1)$ for Dicke states).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The weight vector $\vec{k}=(k_0, \dots, k_{d-1})$, the qudit dimension $d$, and the deformation parameter $q$ (for $	ext{SU}_q(d)$ generalizations).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The algorithm assumes that a Gray code ordering exists for the multiset permutations such that adjacent states differ by exactly one controlled swap, allowing the state preparation to be decomposed into sequential two-qudit rotations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the required Gray gate circuit (Figure 1) and optimized tree structures ensuring the single-swap property for multiset permutations (Figure 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the general framework using Gray codes to reduce state preparation to controlled rotations. It then applies this method specifically to prepare eigenstates of the SU(3)-invariant Heisenberg Hamiltonian (Bethe states) and generalizes it to $	ext{SU}(d)$ Dicke states, detailing both the circuit structure and the underlying algebraic formulas for amplitudes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate a deterministic algorithm for preparing arbitrary multi-qudit states in a definite-weight subspace. By ordering the corresponding computational basis states according to a Gray code for multiset permutations, the state-preparation task is reduced to performing a sequence of controlled 2-qudit Gray rotations. We use this algorithm to prepare exact eigenstates of the SU(3)-invariant Heisenberg Hamiltonian, whose Bethe ansatz is nested. In particular, we describe the preparation of the Bethe states, which are SU(3) highest-weight states, as well as their lower-weight descendants. We also consider the preparation of $SU(d)$ Dicke states and their q-deformations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel model-based Reinforcement Learning framework to solve the challenge of generating quantum control pulses that remain high-fidelity despite variations in experimental parameters. By training a single neural network on an entire family of gate configurations, it achieves rapid, adaptable pulse generation superior to traditional methods like GRAPE for exploring parameter space robustness. This significantly accelerates the design cycle for complex quantum operations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful machine learning tool for quantum control, moving beyond computationally expensive, point-wise optimization methods. The ability to encode system physics directly into the loss function makes it highly relevant for designing robust protocols in experimental AMO platforms like superconducting qubits or trapped ions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving real-time, adaptive quantum control by generating robust, high-fidelity control pulses across a continuous range of varying experimental parameters.

**Main result.** A model-based Reinforcement Learning approach using a single neural network can generate highly accurate and adaptable control pulses that maintain fidelity comparable to multiple independent optimization runs (GRAPE).

**Method.** The authors train a neural network to map input gate configurations directly to optimized pulse phase profiles, bypassing the need for iterative optimization for every parameter set.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a single-spin (two-level) quantum system driven by an external field, described by a time-dependent Hamiltonian that includes tunable parameters like detuning and inhomogeneity.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($\mathcal{J}$) and ensemble-averaged infidelity ($ar{\mathcal{J}}$), which quantify how well the generated pulse achieves the target gate operation across parameter variations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate configuration parameters include rotation angle ($eta$), pulse duration ($T_P$), detuning range width ($\Delta\delta$), and amplitude inhomogeneity range width ($\Delta s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the underlying physical model (Hamiltonian) can be embedded into a differentiable computational graph for training. GRAPE's memoryless nature is contrasted with the network's ability to generalize.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare pulse phase profiles and robustness fingerprints ($\log_{10} J(\delta)$) generated by the NN versus standard GRAPE, demonstrating structural consistency across varying parameters and durations.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of adaptive control limitations in traditional methods (GRAPE), proposes the model-based RL framework using a single network to learn the entire parameter manifold, details the training process involving ensemble averaging, and validates performance by comparing generalization capabilities against multi-seed GRAPE runs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Real-time adaptive control of quantum systems requires rapid generation of robust, high-fidelity pulses across a continuous range of operating conditions. Standard optimization algorithms such as gradient-ascent pulse engineering (GRAPE) solve each instance independently, discarding information between runs and requiring costly reinitialization when parameters change. We present an approach to robust optimal quantum control based on model-based reinforcement learning, in which a single neural network -- embedding the Hamiltonian directly into the training pipeline -- generates robust gates across an entire family of gate configurations, without pre-computed training data. Demonstrated on a single-spin (two-level) system, the trained networks produce pulses for arbitrary rotation angles over a range of pulse durations, detunings, and field inhomogeneities in milliseconds, at fidelities comparable to multi-seed GRAPE. The framework is inherently adaptable: any parameter entering the Hamiltonian can serve as a network input, extending the approach to different systems and control settings. Beyond speed, the network reveals structure in the control landscape: it discovers the same structured phase profiles that appear in GRAPE solutions -- made identifiable through fidelity-invariant symmetry transformations -- but more consistently than independent optimization. This consistency enables smooth interpolation across the entire trained parameter space.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report observing a large, enhanced phonon thermal Hall effect in the nickelate $	ext{La}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10}$ below its density-wave transition temperature. By measuring multiple transport properties and ruling out electronic or simple phonon-drag contributions, they attribute the signal to magnon-phonon hybridization driven by spin-lattice coupling. This reveals a crucial interplay between magnetic order and lattice dynamics in these superconducting materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides experimental evidence for how electronic order (density waves) can couple strongly to lattice vibrations (phonons), leading to exotic thermal responses. This coupling mechanism is relevant to understanding emergent quantum phenomena in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the role of lattice degrees of freedom in the normal state of Ruddlesden–Popper nickelates, specifically focusing on the origin and enhancement mechanism of the phonon thermal Hall effect.

**Main result.** The enhanced transverse thermal conductivity ($\kappa_{xy}$) below the density-wave transition is strongly attributed to phonons coupled via magnon-phonon hybridization driven by spin-lattice coupling. This signal significantly exceeds contributions from electronic carriers or simple phonon drag mechanisms.

**Method.** The research employs transport measurements, measuring longitudinal and transverse thermal conductivities ($\kappa_{xx}, \kappa_{xy}$), magnetoresistance, and Seebeck/Nernst coefficients as a function of temperature and magnetic field.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the trilayer nickelate $	ext{La}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10}$, which exhibits density-wave order. The underlying physics involves charge-lattice coupling, where hybridization between apical oxygen $2p$ and Ni $3d_{z^2}$ orbitals creates local dipoles that interact with magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Thermal Hall angle ($
abla T_y / 
abla T_x$), transverse thermal conductivity ($\kappa_{xy}$), longitudinal thermal conductivity ($\kappa_{xx}$), Seebeck coefficient ($S$), and Nernst coefficient ($\alpha_{xy}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Density-wave transition temperature ($T^* \approx 140$ K); characteristic energy scales ($\sim 3.2 - 4.1$ meV); magnetic field strength (up to 10 T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the electronic contribution can be estimated via the Wiedemann–Franz law, and it estimates an upper bound for phonon-drag using $S_{xx}T\alpha_{xy}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $\kappa_{xy}$ enhancement below $T^*$, confirming phononic origin over electronic contributions. They also compare measured $\kappa_{xy}$ against the calculated phonon-drag limit, showing a large discrepancy.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically measures various transport coefficients ($ho, \kappa, S, \alpha$) under varying magnetic fields and temperatures to isolate the source of transverse heat transport, culminating in a comparison that rules out simple models like phonon drag.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ruddlesden--Popper nickelates have emerged as a promising platform for high-temperature superconductivity, yet the role of lattice degrees of freedom in their correlated normal state remains largely unexplored. Here, we report the observation of a finite phonon thermal Hall effect in the trilayer nickelate La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10}$ at ambient pressure. Remarkably, the thermal Hall response is strongly enhanced below the density-wave transition at $T^*\approx140$ K, exhibiting two distinct plateaus in the thermal Hall resistivity. The characteristic energy scale extracted from the thermal Hall response ($\sim4.1$ meV) closely matches the magnon--phonon crossing span energy ($\sim3.2$ meV), pointing to magnon--phonon hybridization as the primary mechanism enhancing the thermal Hall effect. These results provide new insight into the interplay between lattice and spin excitations in nickelates, with implications for understanding both their superconductivity and the multiple possible origins of insulating thermal Hall signals.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles a generalization of the no-cloning theorem by examining the deletion of one copy from two non-identical unitary copies of an unknown quantum state. The authors define these copies using arbitrary unitaries $U$ and $V$ acting on $|\psiangle$. Through rigorous mathematical analysis, they prove that such a universal deletion operation is impossible.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends fundamental no-cloning theorems into the realm of distinguishing between different types of quantum state redundancy (unitary vs. identical copies), which has implications for quantum error correction protocols and information processing limits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental impossibility of deleting one copy when presented with two non-identical, unknown quantum states.

**Main result.** It is shown that a proposed 'deleting machine' for two unitary copies of an unknown state cannot successfully delete one of them.

**Method.** The authors analyze the action of a defined linear operator (the deleting machine) on arbitrary input states and derive mathematical contradictions based on the assumption that deletion is possible.

**Model / system.** The system models two 'unitary copies' of an unknown state $|\psiangle$, represented by $U|\psiangle$ and $V|\psiangle$, where $U$ and $V$ are arbitrary unitary operators. The process is formalized using a linear operator acting on the joint Hilbert space.

**Key observables.** The success or failure of the deletion operation, analyzed through the structure of the resulting state in an ancilla register.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The unitaries $U$ and $V$, and the unknown quantum state $|\psiangle$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption tested is the equality between the input description (LHS) and the required output structure (RHS) of the proposed deleting machine.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon prior work on identical copies, defines the non-identical unitary copies ($U|\psiangle$ and $V|\psiangle$), proposes a specific deletion operator, and then rigorously proves its impossibility through algebraic manipulation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pati and Braunstein defined a deleting machine and showed the impossibility of deleting one of two identical copies of an unknown quantum state. So far, no one has defined two non-identical copies of a quantum state, of course no one has discussed the impossibility of deleting one of two non-identical copies of an unknown quantum state. In this paper, we define $u|ψ>$ and $U|ψ>$, where $u$ and $U$ are any unitary operators, as two unitary copies of a quantum state $|ψ>$, and show that it is impossible to delete one of two unitary copies of an unknown state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive study determining the nonlinear refractive index of warm rubidium vapor by combining advanced theoretical modeling with precise interferometric measurements. The authors successfully modeled the complex atomic response using density matrix equations, achieving quantitative agreement with experimental data. This work is significant for quantum optics as it provides reliable parameters ($n_2$ and $I_s$) for designing optical devices utilizing alkali vapors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly practical, quantitative tool for characterizing light-matter interaction in warm atomic media, which is crucial for developing quantum sensors and optical components based on alkali vapors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To theoretically calculate and experimentally measure the nonlinear refractive index of warm rubidium vapor.

**Main result.** The study achieved excellent quantitative agreement between theory and experiment, determining a Kerr nonlinear refractive index ($n_2$) up to $10^{-4} 	ext{ cm}^2/	ext{W}$. The results confirm that the nonlinearity is saturable.

**Method.** Theoretical calculations utilize the optical Bloch equations solved via density matrix formalism, which are then compared against measurements taken using a Mach-Zehnder interferometer setup.

**Model / system.** The system involves warm alkali vapors (specifically Rb) modeled by considering 6-level atoms interacting with a probe laser. The model incorporates complex broadening effects like Doppler, transit time, and pressure broadening.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear phase shift ($\delta\phi$), the nonlinear refractive index ($n_{NL}$), saturation intensity ($I_s$), and the Kerr coefficient ($n_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning from atomic resonance, laser power/intensity, and temperature (which dictates atom concentration).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model uses the rotating wave approximation and includes various collision broadening mechanisms while neglecting certain population transfer effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare simulated hyperfine absorption spectra to experimental data across varying temperatures; they also plot nonlinear phase versus detuning and versus laser power, demonstrating saturation behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using density matrix equations (optical Bloch equations) to applying this theory to simulate linear and nonlinear refractive indices. This is validated by comparing these simulations against detailed measurements obtained via interferometry.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The potential to precisely control both the linear and nonlinear index of refraction through optical manipulation of the atomic states has recently pushed warm alkali vapors to the forefront of research in the field of quantum sensors, quantum memories, and quantum fluids of light. Rubidium (Rb) vapor in centimeter-scale glass cells or millimeter-scale MEMS cells has proven to be a very promising platform for these applications, yet only a handful of research works have been dedicated to the investigation of the (non)linear refractive index of Rb vapor. We present results of theoretical calculations of the (non)linear refractive index of warm Rb vapor, based on the optical Bloch equations for 6-level Rb atoms interacting with a probe laser. They are compared to the experimental results obtained using an interferometric technique, showing excellent quantitative agreement. A Kerr nonlinear refractive index $n_2$ of up to $10^{-4}$ cm$^2$/W is obtained. Python scripts for all theoretical calculations presented in this work are provided, including the refractive index calculation, that can readily be used in practical implementations for simulating the (non)linear refractive index of Rb vapor including the effects of Doppler broadening, transit time broadening, pressure broadening, saturation, optical pumping, and spin-exchange collisions.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper studies quantum chaos in 4D Superconformal Field Theories by mapping their operator mixing dynamics onto an effective spin chain model. By analyzing the energy spectrum's statistical properties—such as level repulsion and spectral rigidity—the authors demonstrate that tuning the SCFT couplings can drive the system into a chaotic regime, confirming theoretical predictions using random matrix theory diagnostics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, calculable link between high-energy theoretical physics (SCFTs/AdS/CFT) and fundamental concepts from quantum chaos theory, using spectral statistics as the primary diagnostic tool.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how signatures of quantum chaos manifest within strongly coupled, higher-dimensional Quantum Field Theories (QFTs), specifically Superconformal Field Theories (SCFTs). It aims to diagnose the onset of chaos by analyzing spectral statistics.

**Main result.** The system exhibits chaotic spectral statistics when the coupling parameters ($b_i$) are tuned according to a specific power law distribution, confirming that quantum chaos can be diagnosed via random matrix ensemble signatures in these field theories.

**Method.** Chaos is diagnosed by analyzing the statistical distribution of eigenvalues of the dilatation operator, focusing on metrics like eigenvalue level repulsion, spectral rigidity ($\Delta_3(E)$), and the spectral form factor. The dynamics are modeled using an effective spin chain Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system models a class of 4D SCFTs derived from orbifolds of $\mathcal{N}=4$ Super Yang–Mills theory. The low-energy dynamics are effectively described by a nearest-neighbor interacting spin chain Hamiltonian governing operator mixing in a controlled subsector.

**Key observables.** Eigenvalue level repulsion, spectral rigidity ($\Delta_3(E)$), and the spectral form factor; Krylov complexity ($C_K(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The marginal couplings of the SCFT determine the hopping parameters $b_i$ in the spin chain. The analysis distinguishes between integrable regimes (e.g., all $b_i=b$), chaotic regimes, and intermediate Anderson localization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is primarily confined to one-loop mixing effects within the dilute gas approximation for the SCFT dynamics. Furthermore, the single spin chain approximation is noted to break down at large 't Hooft coupling, requiring multi-trace corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares Krylov state complexity $C_K(t)$ evolution, showing distinct growth patterns for integrable versus chaotic regimes; Figure 1 shows level spacing ratios compared against theoretical predictions for various ensembles.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical context by mapping SCFT dynamics onto a spin chain Hamiltonian. It then applies advanced diagnostics from quantum chaos theory (RMT statistics, complexity measures) to determine when the system transitions into a chaotic regime based on tuning the underlying couplings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chaotic dynamics play an important role in a number of physical systems. One of the qualitative hallmarks of this behavior is the appearance of a sufficiently "complex" spectrum of energy levels. This also makes it challenging to directly verify the onset of chaos in interacting quantum field theories. We present a class of 4D superconformal field theories (SCFTs) given by orbifolds of 4D $\mathcal{N} = 4$ Super Yang--Mills theory in which operator mixing in a controlled subsector is described by an effective spin chain in one spatial dimension with nearest neighbor interactions tuned by the marginal couplings of the SCFT. Tuning the marginal couplings results in a chaotic spectrum, while generically the spin chain exhibits Anderson localization. We diagnose the onset of chaos by analyzing the statistical distribution of eigenvalues of the dilatation operator, in particular properties such as eigenvalue level repulsion, spectral rigidity, and the spectral form factor. We also show that other diagnostics such as Krylov complexity sometimes do not faithfully capture this information. This structure defines a chaotic billiard in the target space of the stringy realization. We also comment on the large $N$ holographic dual description, where the controlled single spin chain approximation must be supplemented by multi-trace dynamics, i.e., the splitting and joining of multiple spin chains.

</details>
