**Summary.** The paper enhances configuration interaction methods by replacing heuristic determinant selection with an active-learning loop guided by a neural network classifier. This framework recasts importance determination as a binary classification task, allowing the method to efficiently compress variational spaces. The results demonstrate significant computational savings while maintaining high accuracy for calculating ground-state energies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a general, data-driven tool for compressing large Hilbert spaces in quantum chemistry calculations, which is highly relevant for simulating complex many-body systems beyond standard approximations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The efficiency of Selected Configuration Interaction (SCI) methods for electronic structure calculations is limited by heuristics used to select important determinants from exponentially growing variational spaces.

**Main result.** The proposed framework achieves result parity with traditional CI methods at substantially lower computational cost, showing up to a 5x reduction in memory/iteration cost and dramatically faster convergence for quantum-classical variants.

**Method.** A data-driven selection framework is introduced that treats determinant importance as a binary classification task, integrating an active-learning loop powered by a neural network classifier into the iterative CI workflow.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to electronic structure calculations, specifically determining the ground state energy of diatomic molecules (e.g., N2) using classical and quantum configuration interaction methods ($	ext{cHCI}$ and $	ext{cSQD}$).

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy; determinant importance (binary classification output); correlation energy recovered relative to CASCI.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The selection process involves random sampling, temporary diagonalization for labeling, and the use of a trained MLP classifier to guide subsequent selections.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that determinant importance can be successfully modeled as a binary classification problem; the method relies on generating labels from small temporary diagonalizations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between standard SCI schemes and the proposed framework, showing energy recovery vs. space size for $	ext{cHCI}$ and accelerated convergence curves for $	ext{cSQD}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical foundation of using ML classifiers to guide variational space expansion in CI methods, presenting specific implementations ($	ext{cHCI}$, $	ext{cSQD}$) and benchmarking results on molecular systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Selected configuration interaction methods achieve near-exact electronic structure calculations by iteratively constructing compact variational spaces, but their efficiency depends critically on the heuristics used to identify important determinants. Here, we introduce a data-driven selection framework that recasts determinant importance as a binary classification task and integrates a neural-network classifier into the iterative CI workflow through an active-learning loop. At each iteration, a random subset of candidate determinants is labelled via temporary diagonalisation, and the trained classifier guides selection of the remaining configurations. We demonstrate the utility of this framework for both classical and quantum CI methods by calculating the ground-state energy of a diatomic molecule. Our method achieves result parity with traditional configuration interaction methods at substantially lower computational cost: roughly a $\times 5$ reduction in memory and per-iteration cost for the classical cHCI variant, and convergence in markedly fewer iterations for the quantum-classical cSQD variant. These results establish classifier-assisted determinant selection as a lightweight, method-agnostic tool for compressing variational spaces and accelerating both classical and hybrid quantum-classical configuration interaction algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to analyze Perfect State Transfer (PST) in discrete-time quantum walks by relating the walk's evolution on a graph $G$ to its quotient graph $G/\pi$. By leveraging shunt decomposition and equitable partitions, the authors prove that PST is equivalent between the two structures. This methodology allows for the rigorous analysis of complex systems like line digraphs associated with bipartite or circulant graphs.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful algebraic tools—the quotient graph formalism—to simplify the study of complex quantum dynamics on large graphs by reducing them to smaller, structurally related systems, which is highly relevant for designing efficient quantum circuits or understanding quantum information flow.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Perfect State Transfer (PST) in discrete-time quantum walks defined on a graph $G$ by relating the dynamics to those of its quotient graph $G/\pi$. It aims to establish necessary and sufficient conditions for PST based on structural decompositions.

**Main result.** It proves that PST occurs on the parent graph $G$ if and only if it occurs on the quotient graph $G/\pi$, providing explicit criteria using Chebyshev polynomials and applying this to families like cycle graphs and complete bipartite digraphs.

**Method.** The methodology involves utilizing shunt decomposition, defining an equitable partition $\pi$, deriving intertwining relations between the shift operators of $G$ and $G/\pi$, and analyzing the unitary evolution operator via projection techniques.

**Model / system.** The model is a discrete-time quantum walk (DTQW) defined on a graph $G$. The analysis extends to line digraphs $	ext{LD}(G)$ when considering arc partitions, using the quotient structure induced by an equitable partition $\pi$ of the vertices.

**Key observables.** Perfect State Transfer (PST), characterized by $|\langle e_b | U^k | e_a angle| = 1$ for some time step $k$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the equitable partition $\pi$, the graph type ($C_{2n}$, $K_{n,n}^{ightleftharpoons}$, $	ext{Circ}(2n, S)$), and the specific time steps $k$ where PST is observed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis critically relies on the existence of an equitable partition $\pi$ that allows for the derivation of consistent intertwining relations between the transition operators of $G$ and $G/\pi$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction process, showing how vertex partitions ($\pi$) lead to quotient graphs ($G/\pi$), and how these structural relationships are maintained when analyzing the line digraphs $	ext{LD}(G)$ and $	ext{LD}(G/\pi)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing foundational relations between shift operators on $G$ and $G/\pi$, deriving PST conditions using Chebyshev polynomials, and then applying this framework systematically to specific graph families (e.g., cycle graphs, complete bipartite digraphs, circulant graphs) to answer open questions regarding PST equivalence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper investigates perfect state transfer (PST) in discrete-time quantum walks constructed via the shunt decomposition method. The walks are defined on a graph $G$ and its associated quotient graph $G/π$, induced by an equitable partition $π$. Through the shunt decomposition of $G$, we derive an explicit relation between the shift operator of the parent graph $G$ and that of its quotient graph $G/π$. We construct a reflection operator based on the characteristic matrix, which establishes a connection between the transition operator of the parent graph and that of its lower-dimensional quotient graph. We then prove that PST occurs on $G$ if and only if it occurs on $G/π$. Furthermore, we express the unitary evolution operator of the quotient graph in terms of Chebyshev polynomials of the first kind, from which we derive explicit criteria for PST. As an application, we establish PST on the cycle graph $C_{n}$ at time $k = n/2$, and lift the result to the parent graph $C_{2n}$ via the equitable partition $π$. We further show that if an equitable partition $π$ of $G$ induces a quotient isomorphic to $K_n^{\circlearrowleft}$, the complete digraph on $n$ vertices with a loop at every vertex, then PST occurs at step $k = n$, and the walk is periodic at $k = 2n$. This framework is applied to two families of graphs, which are the complete bipartite digraph $K_{n,n}^{\rightleftharpoons}$ and the circulant graph $\operatorname{Circ}(2n, S)$, where $S$ consists of all odd residues modulo $2n$ and $n = 2^s$ for some $s \geq 1$, establishing PST in their respective line digraphs. Collectively, these results also answer the question posed by Godsil and Zhan concerning which shunt decompositions or embeddings of a graph admit PST.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a theoretical breakthrough showing that if the ground state of a sparse Hamiltonian is exactly sparse, its support must be highly localized within the system's connectivity graph. This localization allows for an exact eigenvector to be computed classically in polynomial time relative to the sparsity, bypassing exponential scaling issues inherent in full diagonalization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful classical algorithmic tool for quantum many-body physics. By transforming an exponentially hard diagonalization problem into a polynomially solvable graph search problem based on sparsity structure, it offers new computational pathways beyond standard iterative solvers like Lanczos.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is efficiently computing exact eigenvectors (like ground states) for quantum systems described by large, sparse Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** The authors prove that if an eigenvector is sufficiently sparse, its support can be localized to a small region of the graph, allowing for polynomial-time classical computation of the exact eigenvector.

**Method.** They introduce the 'eigenwalk problem' and develop algorithms based on graph localization theorems (Theorem 3) which restrict the search space from the entire system size ($2^n$) to a polynomially sized ball around a known support vertex.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum system governed by a Hermitian Hamiltonian matrix $H$, whose non-zero entries define an underlying graph structure. The focus is on finding sparse eigenvectors $|\psiangle$ corresponding to specific eigenvalues $\lambda$.

**Key observables.** Eigenvector support ($	ext{supp}(|\psiangle)$), eigenvalue ($\lambda$), and the energy variance of projected states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sparsity ($s$) of the eigenvector, sparsity of $H$ (poly($n$)), and the distance/diameter of the localized support region.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The algorithm requires that the target eigenvector is exactly sparse, and for non-extremal cases, an additional 'sparsity-separation' promise regarding other eigenvectors is assumed to guarantee uniqueness or tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the eigenwalk problem setup, examples of graph structures dictating support constraints (degeneracy), and visualizations of how the full support can be localized into a smaller search ball around a guiding vertex.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by formalizing the eigenwalk problem, proving structural theorems that guarantee the localization of sparse supports, and culminating in a polynomial-time algorithm derived from these localization bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Computing quantum ground states is generically difficult, but additional structure can sometimes allow diagonalization to be recast as a more feasible problem. For example, when the desired ground state is sparse in a given basis, diagonalization can be facilitated via graph search. We make this reformulation precise by introducing the eigenwalk problem, which seeks the support of a sparse eigenvector of a Hermitian matrix by exploring the graph induced by its nonzero entries. However, it is not obvious whether the relevant support vertices must always be efficiently reachable by a search on the graph. To resolve this question, we prove that for every sparse eigenvector, there exists a (possibly different) sparse eigenvector with the same eigenvalue whose support is tightly localized in the graph, with diameter scaling only linearly in the sparsity and independently of the total number of vertices. As a consequence, if a $2^n$-dimensional, ${\rm poly}(n)$-sparse Hamiltonian has an $\mathcal{O}(1)$-sparse extremal eigenvector and one support element is known, then an exact eigenvector with the same eigenvalue can be computed classically in ${\rm poly}(n)$ time. The same conclusion follows when the $\mathcal{O}(1)$-sparse eigenvector is non-extremal, provided that it is sparser than every eigenvector with a different eigenvalue. These results hold with no assumptions on the degeneracy, locality, spectral width, or spectral gap of the Hamiltonian, and the underlying support-localization principle also extends to problems beyond exact diagonalization, such as sparse principal component analysis.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper compares two methods—the Augmented Lagrangian Function (ALF) and the standard penalty function—for encoding constraints into Ising machine Hamiltonians. By benchmarking against the QKP using $	ext{TT}\epsilon$, the authors demonstrate that ALF significantly improves solution performance by allowing for a better balance between satisfying constraints and optimizing the objective function, even with smaller penalty coefficients.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses how advanced mathematical techniques from continuous optimization (like ALM) can be adapted to improve heuristic search performance on quantum-inspired hardware for discrete problems, which is highly relevant to understanding the limitations and enhancements of current NISQ devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study evaluates the performance of using an augmented Lagrangian function (ALF) formulation versus a standard penalty function for solving constrained combinatorial optimization problems on Ising machines.

**Main result.** The ALF significantly reduces the time-to-epsilon ($	ext{TT}\epsilon$) by approximately an order of magnitude compared to the penalty function, especially when keeping the penalty coefficient $\mu$ small.

**Method.** Performance is evaluated using the time-to-epsilon ($	ext{TT}\epsilon$), which measures the computation time required to find a solution satisfying both feasibility and objective precision. The comparison involves analyzing how different constraint handling Hamiltonians affect this metric.

**Model / system.** The problem is mapped onto an Ising model/QUBO Hamiltonian, representing constrained combinatorial optimization problems like the Quadratic Knapsack Problem (QKP). Two primary formulations are compared: $H_{AL}$ using ALF and $H_P$ using a penalty function.

**Key observables.** Time-to-epsilon ($	ext{TT}\epsilon$), constraint violation, normalized objective value, feasibility probability ($p_{\epsilon}(	au)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hyperparameters $\mu$ (penalty coefficient) and $\lambda$ (Lagrange multiplier); the relative scales of the objective and constraint energy terms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison is based on solving the QKP instance 'r 100 50 5' using a specific annealing engine, and results are limited to tested parameter grids.

**Figures summary.** Figures show heatmaps of $	ext{TT}\epsilon$ across the $\lambda-\mu$ space for both ALF and PF formulations, demonstrating that positive $\lambda$ allows for lower $	ext{TT}\epsilon$ with smaller $\mu$. Temporal evolution plots compare how quickly the objective value plateaus.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem formulation (Ising/QUBO) and the two constraint methods ($H_P$ vs $H_{AL}$). It then details the evaluation metric ($	ext{TT}\epsilon$) and presents comparative results across parameter sweeps, culminating in a discussion of why ALF maintains better energy balance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We apply the augmented Lagrangian function (ALF) as a formulation for Ising machines and evaluate its performance by time-to-epsilon (\mathrm{TT\varepsilon}). The ALF has been well studied in continuous optimization for its numerical stability and convergence, and its advantage over the penalty function formulation is demonstrated here through the following results. Using the quadratic knapsack problem as a benchmark, we examine the dependence of \mathrm{TT\varepsilon} on the hyperparameters μand λ. The augmented Lagrangian formulation reduces \mathrm{TT\varepsilon} by roughly an order of magnitude compared with the penalty function formulation, keeping μsmall while obtaining feasible solutions and, for representative parameter settings, reaching high-precision solutions earlier in the search. These findings indicate that the augmented Lagrangian function is a promising formulation for improving the solution performance of Ising machines.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel and significantly faster algorithm to construct minimal-size Pauli-LIMDDs for representing quantum states. By rigorously enforcing canonical forms using advanced group theory techniques, the authors demonstrate that these diagrams can simulate quantum circuits exponentially faster than existing methods. This breakthrough improves the practical realization of theoretical speedups in quantum computation simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts the computational feasibility of simulating complex quantum circuits, which is crucial for understanding dynamics in many-body systems or designing quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a faster, more efficient algorithm for constructing minimal-size quantum decision diagrams (Pauli-LIMDDs) to simulate quantum circuits.

**Main result.** A novel normal-form algorithm achieves significant speedups in the worst-case complexity of node processing, realizing theoretically proven exponential advantages over existing simulation structures like QMDDs.

**Method.** The authors introduce and implement a refined normal-form procedure for Pauli-LIMDDs, which involves complex group-theoretic calculations (like adapted Zassenhaus procedures) to enforce canonicality rules such as 'high determinism' and 'max precedence'.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum circuit simulation where quantum states $|\psiangle$ are represented by vectors in $\mathbb{C}^{2^n}$ and encoded using specialized data structures like Local Invertible Map Decision Diagrams (LIMDDs).

**Key observables.** Run time, final node count, and the ability to represent highly entangled states efficiently.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity improvements are quantified by reducing worst-case time from $O(n^3)$ to $O(n^2)$ for certain node types.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that enforcing full canonicity (the max-reduced form) is necessary and sufficient to realize the exponential speedup over less canonical representations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of DDs, compare vector representations across QMDD and LIMDD formats, detail normalization rules (low/max precedence), and show performance comparisons on benchmark sets like RandCliff and MQT-Bench.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the need for canonicalization in LIMDDs to presenting the novel $O(n^2)$ algorithm for single-child nodes, followed by rigorous proofs establishing that the resulting max-reduced form is unique for any given quantum state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The decision diagram (DD) data structure enables fast linear-algebra calculations by bringing vectors into a normal form and subsequently merging equivalent ones, yielding a minimally-sized DD modulo the equivalence relation. A fruitful application area is quantum-circuit simulation, where the vectors represent quantum states. The Local Invertible Map Decision Diagram (LIMDD) type, merges LIM-equivalent (typically Pauli-gate equivalent) vectors, can efficiently simulate Clifford circuits as well as some high-T-count circuits, and has theoretically been proven exponentially faster for simulation than other well-developed data structures, including other common DD variants. However, these exponential advantages have not fully materialized yet in existing implementations, for which the normal-form procedure, which is a highly complex algorithm, is either absent or only partially implemented. We here present a novel normal-form algorithm for Pauli-LIMDDs, achieving a worst-case speedup from $O(n^3)$ to $O(n^2)$ for an $n$-qubit DD node with a single child node while keeping the $O(n^3)$ run time in case of two distinct children nodes. We implement the algorithm as part of QolDDer, our Pauli-LIMDD simulator for quantum circuits, written from scratch in C/C++. The implementation realizes the theoretically-proven advantages of Pauli-LIMDDs on Clifford circuits, is significantly faster than the existing LIMDD simulators on such circuits, and on a public quantum-circuit data set often outperforms them by an order of magnitude. In the future, we envision that our work will enable further application and development of LIMDD variants, not only for quantum design tasks, but also for analysis of linear-algebra-based systems in general.

</details>

**Authors:** Afraa Mahboubi, Büşra Gökçe Zolmaz, Devrim Karayer, Handenur Şay, Oğuzhan Kaya, Abdulkadir Senol

**Summary.** This paper calculates the ground-state energy of the lithium atom using multiple sophisticated quantum mechanical techniques. It rigorously compares results from second-order perturbation theory against a non-orthogonal variational method. The study successfully demonstrates that the variational approach provides a highly accurate upper bound, offering deep insight into electron correlation and screening effects in multi-electron atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed pedagogical comparison between two cornerstone techniques—perturbation theory and variational methods—for calculating correlation energy in light atoms, which is foundational knowledge for advanced AMO/many-body physics simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work systematically investigates the ground-state energy of the lithium atom by comparing results from time-independent perturbation theory and the variational method to understand electron correlation and screening in multi-electron atoms.

**Main result.** The non-orthogonal two-parameter variational approach established a superior upper bound energy of -201.187 eV, demonstrating better convergence than the second-order perturbation expansion.

**Method.** The study employs Rayleigh-Schrödinger time-independent perturbation theory (up to second order) and a non-orthogonal variational method that optimizes effective nuclear charges for core and valence shells.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Lithium atom ($Z=3$) with three electrons. The Hamiltonian is treated by partitioning it into an unperturbed, independent-particle part ($\hat{H}_0$) and a perturbation term ($\hat{H}^{(1)}$) accounting for inter-electronic repulsion.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy (in eV), effective nuclear charges ($\alpha$ and $eta$), Coulomb ($J$) and Exchange ($K$) integrals.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The calculation relies on the non-relativistic approximation, using orbital wave functions derived from hydrogenic solutions for initial states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include enforcing fermionic anti-symmetry via Slater determinants and restricting calculations to s-wave channels due to computational tractability in certain steps.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the total ground-state energy convergence across successive perturbation orders against a reference baseline, illustrating systematic error reduction.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically by establishing a zeroth-order baseline using independent particles, then systematically applying first and second-order corrections from perturbation theory. This is contrasted with the variational method's optimization of effective charges to achieve an upper bound, culminating in a comparison of convergence rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, the ground-state energy of the lithium atom is systematically investigated using both time-independent perturbation theory and the variational method to provide a comprehensive pedagogical analysis of many-body atomic systems. The unperturbed Hamiltonian is initially constructed by neglecting electron-electron interactions, treating the system as three independent hydrogen-like electrons to yield a zeroth-order energy baseline of -275.51 eV. The antisymmetric fermionic nature of the exact wave function is rigorously enforced through the Slater determinant formalism. First-order perturbation theory is applied to evaluate static inter-electronic repulsion using exact Coulomb and exchange integrals, refining the energy state to -192.01 eV. To account for dynamical electronic correlation, second-order perturbation theory is computed numerically for virtual single-electron s-orbital transitions, leading to a total perturbative energy of -196.36 eV. A brief discussion of two-electron excitations is also included to encapsulate further physical realism within the framework. Furthermore, a non-orthogonal two-parameter variational approach is employed to model the shell-specific shielding effect. By optimizing the effective nuclear charges, the variational method establishes a superior upper bound energy of -201.187 eV. The results of both methods are comprehensively contrasted against each other and the reference baseline to provide critical insights into the nature of electron correlation and screening in multi-electron atoms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reviews multiple advanced mathematical approaches to understanding the Berry-Keating operator ($H_{BK}$), an operator hypothesized to encode information about the zeros of the Riemann zeta function. Using techniques like Mellin transforms and bi-coherent states, the authors analyze $H_{BK}$'s spectral properties but ultimately conclude that establishing its self-adjoint nature with the correct spectrum remains a major unsolved problem.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges deep areas of mathematical physics—spectral theory, quantum mechanics, and analytic number theory—making it highly relevant for theorists exploring fundamental connections between seemingly disparate fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is connecting the zeros of the Riemann zeta function ($\zeta(s)$) to the spectral theory of a quantum Hamiltonian, encapsulated by the Berry-Keating operator ($H_{BK}$). The authors review multiple mathematical approaches to this connection.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that $H_{BK}$ can be unitarily equivalent to the momentum operator in certain representations and analyzes its spectrum using both Hilbertian and distributional methods. However, it concludes that constructing a self-adjoint Hamiltonian with the required discrete spectrum remains an open problem.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced mathematical techniques including Mellin transforms, dilation operators, pseudo-boson formalisms, and generalized coherent states (bi-coherent states) to analyze the operator's spectral properties.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Berry-Keating operator, $H_{BK} = rac{1}{2}(\hat{x}\hat{p} + \hat{p}\hat{x})$, which acts on quantum mechanical wave functions. The context is deeply rooted in spectral theory and its hypothesized link to number theory.

**Key observables.** The spectrum of $H_{BK}$ ($\sigma(H)$), the existence of a discrete spectrum matching $\zeta(s)$ zeros, and the properties of generalized eigenstates/coherent states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure depends on the choice of Hilbert space realization (e.g., $L_{\pm}^2(\mathbb{R})$) and the specific mathematical extension theory applied to the operator.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis frequently requires working in distributional settings or assuming certain unitary equivalences, acknowledging that these methods are far from a complete proof of the conjecture.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a review, first presenting a Hilbertian viewpoint using Mellin transforms and dilation operators. It then transitions to a distributional approach involving ladder operators and generalized coherent states, culminating in discussions about spectral extensions and the unresolved connection to the Riemann hypothesis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We review here two different viewpoints on the Berry-Keating operator $H_{BK}$, whose connection to the Riemann hypothesis remains an intriguing and not yet fully understood question, despite considerable attention in the recent literature. In particular, we propose two somehow complementary views to $H_{BK}$: the first is based on a purely Hilbertian point of view, on dilation operators and on the Mellin transform. The second is a distributional approach, with a specific view to ladder operators, generalized eigenstates of $H_{BK}$, and generalized coherent states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous theoretical framework to define the momentum operator in general curvilinear coordinates. By relating the momentum to the velocity operator via the Heisenberg equation, the authors construct Hermitian vector and canonical components that correctly account for geometric curvature effects. This provides essential mathematical machinery for quantum mechanics on curved spaces.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental mathematical tools necessary for correctly formulating quantum mechanics on curved manifolds, which is crucial when modeling physical systems whose geometry cannot be approximated by flat Euclidean space.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The construction of the Hermitian vector and canonical components of the momentum operator ($\vec{p}$) in 3D Euclidean space when expressed using general curvilinear coordinates (GCC's).

**Main result.** A unified, natural approach is developed showing that while individual coordinate derivatives are problematic, the full momentum operator $\vec{p}$ remains Hermitian due to cancellation of boundary terms.

**Method.** The method utilizes the Heisenberg equation of motion applied to the position operator ($\vec{r}$) and identifies $\vec{p} \propto m\dot{\vec{r}}$ to derive corrected, Hermitian vector components ($\vec{p}_H$) before projecting them onto canonical forms ($p_H^a$).

**Model / system.** The study is purely theoretical, focusing on the mathematical structure of quantum mechanics in curved coordinate systems. The underlying physical system involves a particle whose Hamiltonian includes the Laplace-Beltrami operator.

**Key observables.** Hermiticity of $\vec{p}$ and its components; canonical commutation relations $[q^a, p_H^b] = i\hbar \delta^a_b$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Planck's constant ($\hbar$), mass ($m$), metric tensor ($g_{ij}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard physical boundary conditions (e.g., square-integrability) are assumed for the necessary integration by parts to prove Hermiticity.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the text develops explicit forms for operators like $\vec{p}_H$ and $p_H^a$ in specific coordinate systems (e.g., polar/spherical).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem by noting that naive momentum components fail Hermiticity; it then derives a general procedure using the Heisenberg equation to construct $\vec{p}_H$; this is demonstrated explicitly for simple cases like polar coordinates before generalizing the formalism to arbitrary GCC's.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct the Hermitian vector and canonical components of the momentum operator in 3D Euclidean space spanned by general curvilinear coordinates (GCC's) using a simple, natural and unified approach based on identifying the momentum operator in any coordinate system as mass times the velocity operator. When this latter is calculated by applying the Heisenberg equation of motion, it returns ($-i\hbar$ times) the gradient operator plus an additional zero-valued sum, which when distributed among the components of the gradient, it makes each the Hermitian vector component of the momentum operator in GCC's. The canonical components follow immediately upon symmetrizing each of these vector components in the corresponding base vector. For accessability by wider audiences, we first develop the formalism for the simple polar coordinates and then we develop the case for GCC's.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how coupling minimal Kitaev chain Josephson junctions to normal leads introduces non-Hermiticity, leading to complex Andreev spectra. The key finding is the realization of controllable Andreev Exceptional Points (EPs) whose existence depends critically on the superconducting phase difference ($\phi$). This work demonstrates that non-Hermitian physics offers a robust resource for engineering novel topological phases in superconducting quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between non-Hermiticity (from open systems) and the emergence of topological features like EPs provides a powerful new tool for engineering quantum states in superconducting circuits, relevant to advanced condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how non-Hermiticity, introduced via coupling to normal reservoirs, enables stable topological states in Josephson junctions formed by minimal Kitaev chains.

**Main result.** The system exhibits Andreev Exceptional Points (EPs) whose existence and properties are controlled by the superconducting phase difference ($\phi$), allowing for the engineering of non-Hermitian topological phases.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving the retarded Green's function in Nambu space, solving for energy poles, and analyzing symmetries like $	ext{PHS}^\dagger$.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Josephson junction connecting two minimal Kitaev chains. The model incorporates non-Hermiticity through coupling to normal reservoirs ($\Gamma$), leading to an effective complex Andreev spectrum.

**Key observables.** Andreev Exceptional Points (EPs) and lines, the superconducting phase difference ($\phi$), local and nonlocal conductances ($G_{LL}, G_{-LR}$), and spectral functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting phase difference ($\phi$), non-Hermiticity strength ($\Gamma$), onsite energies ($\epsilon_{\alpha j}$), and coupling ratios ($\Delta_\alpha/t_\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the zero-frequency approximation for self-energy and utilizes symmetry arguments (e.g., $	ext{PHS}^\dagger$) to characterize topological protection.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy spectrum vs. $\phi$ showing EP crossings, and conductance measurements ($G_{LL}, G_{LR}$) confirming signatures of EPs at both zero and finite real energies.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian and symmetry constraints to analyzing the emergence of Andreev EPs as a function of $\phi$. It then details how these EPs can be robustly protected by symmetries, even away from ideal 'sweet spot' parameters. Finally, it discusses measurable signatures using local and nonlocal conductance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider Josephson junctions formed by two minimal Kitaev chains and investigate how the interplay between non-Hermiticity and superconducting phase difference enables the realization of stable topological states that do not exist in the Hermitian realm. In particular, we focus on non-Hermiticity produced by coupling the minimal Kitaev chain Josephson junction to normal reservoirs, which renders the system open and characterized by a complex Andreev spectrum. Interestingly, we find that this complex spectrum hosts second order exceptional points, where a pair of eigenvalues and their respective eigenvectors coalesce, and are fully controlled by the superconducting phase difference. Depending on the spatial unequal distribution of non-Hermiticity, these Andreev exceptional points can appear at zero or finite energies connecting stable energy lines protected by non-Hermitian topology. Moreover, tuning the system parameters, such as onsite energies, non-Hermiticity, or electron cotunneling, the Andreev exceptional points give rise to Andreev exceptional lines enclosing protected two-dimensional zero real energy areas. We also discuss potential detection schemes of Andreev exceptional points by using local and nonlocal conductance signatures. Our results demonstrate the utility of non-Hermiticity from normal reservoirs as a useful resource for engineering non-Hermitian topological phases in minimal Kitaev chain Josephson junctions.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes an experimental route to realize the Haldane model—a key Chern insulator—by combining patterned electrostatic potentials with periodically modulated magnetic fields in GaAs heterostructures. Using advanced theoretical techniques like plane-wave diagonalization, the authors demonstrate that this combination successfully engineers non-trivial band topology, controllable via geometric misalignment parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, materials-based blueprint for realizing topological quantum matter using external electric and magnetic field engineering, which is highly relevant for solid-state quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work theoretically investigates simulating the Haldane model, a minimal Chern insulator realization, using solid-state systems to engineer topological band structures.

**Main result.** Superimposing engineered electrostatic lattices with spatially periodic magnetic fields can induce non-trivial band topology, successfully mapping the low-energy physics onto the Haldane model Hamiltonian.

**Method.** The study employs plane-wave diagonalization techniques and utilizes advanced mathematical tools like the Fukui-Hatsugai-Suzuki algorithm to calculate topological invariants (Chern number).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as spinless, non-interacting electrons in a 2D honeycomb lattice realized in ultra-clean GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructures. The Hamiltonian incorporates kinetic energy, crystal potential, and coupling to engineered vector potentials derived from patterned magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Chern number (C), band dispersion ($\epsilon_k$), effective mass terms ($m_H, m_S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relative shift $\mathbf{r}_0$ between the potential and field patterns; magnetic flux amplitude ($eta\Phi_0/2\pi$); lattice constant $a_0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the full Hamiltonian onto a low-energy three-plane-wave manifold around the K and K' valleys, neglecting higher-shell harmonics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spatial profiles of potentials/fields for different shifts ($\mathbf{r}_0$), energy band structures along high-symmetry paths showing gap opening, and topological phase diagrams mapping Chern number $C$ vs. control parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the general Hamiltonian in a solid-state context to analyzing symmetries (P, T) and then deriving the low-energy effective Dirac Hamiltonian by treating magnetic perturbations perturbatively up to $O(A^2)$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Haldane model represents the minimal lattice-based realization of a Chern insulator, exhibiting a quantized Hall conductance in the absence of Landau levels. Despite its conceptual elegance, the implementation in crystalline solids of the requisite pattern of Peierls phases breaking time-reversal symmetry remains experimentally demanding. In this work, we theoretically investigate the possibility to simulate the Haldane model in ultra-clean GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructures. Our proposal relies on recent experiments in which a high-mobility two-dimensional electron gas is subject to a gate-defined honeycomb electrostatic potential and a laterally periodic magnetic field generated by patterned ferromagnetic structures. The combined electrostatic and magnetic superlattices furnish a viable route to emulate the topological properties of the Haldane model.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how proximity effects from TMDs induce spin-orbit coupling in graphene, modifying its cyclotron resonance spectrum. The authors predict characteristic fine double-peak structures and new spin-flip absorption lines (CCRs). These modifications lead to a measurable magneto-optical response, providing a powerful spectroscopic tool for characterizing spin interactions in van der Waals heterostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it uses optical spectroscopy (CR) to probe subtle quantum phenomena—namely spin-orbit coupling and spin texture—in 2D materials heterostructures, which are key areas for developing spintronic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes the fine structure of cyclotron resonance (CR) and combined cyclotron resonance (CCR) absorption in heterobilayers formed by proximitizing graphene with transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs). The focus is on how proximity-induced spin interactions modify these optical responses.

**Main result.** The theory predicts a polarization-dependent fine double-peak structure in the main interband CR transitions and identifies additional spin-flip absorption lines corresponding to CCRs. Furthermore, it shows that these effects lead to a measurable Magneto-Optical Kerr Effect (MOKE) signal.

**Method.** The analysis employs an effective Dirac model augmented by proximity terms ($H_{vz}, H_{st}, H_R$) and calculates the optical absorption spectrum using the Kubo–Greenwood formula in the Landau Level basis. Numerical diagonalization is used to incorporate the Rashba coupling effects.

**Model / system.** The system is a heterobilayer of graphene proximitized by TMDs, modeled with an effective Hamiltonian including valley-Zeeman ($H_{vz}$), staggered potential ($H_{st}$), and crucial spin-orbit coupling via the Rashba term ($H_R$). The analysis operates in the Landau Level basis derived from minimal coupling.

**Key observables.** Cyclotron Resonance (CR) absorption spectrum, Combined Cyclotron Resonance (CCR) lines, Magneto-Optical Kerr Effect (MOKE) signal, and polarization dependence ($\sigma^+$ vs $\sigma^-$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Moderate magnetic fields ($0.5–2$ T), where cyclotron energies dominate proximity terms; the strength of the Rashba coupling ($\lambda_R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the LL basis derived from minimal coupling is sufficient, and it treats the Rashba term perturbatively or within a truncated diagonalization scheme.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate CR/CCR absorption spectra showing fine double-peak structures (Figure 3) and demonstrate selection rules for these transitions. Figure 4 specifically shows resolved CCR lines below main interband CR lines at specific magnetic fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system and Hamiltonian, details the theoretical methods using LL basis diagonalization, calculates the key observables (CR/CCR absorption), and concludes by interpreting the results in terms of measurable MOKE signals and experimental verifications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A monolayer graphene and its Dirac electrons can be equipped with an enhanced spin-orbit coupling (SOC) when proximitized by other van der Waals (vdW) materials, such as transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs). In this work we analyze the cyclotron resonance (CR) absorption of such heterobilayers at quantizing magnetic fields in the presence of proximity-induced spin interactions, including the spin-pseudospin Rashba coupling. We evaluate the spin-textured wave functions of the Landau levels and calculate the absorption spectrum paying special attention to its spin proximity induced modifications. We reveal the formation of a fine double-peak structure of the main interband CR transitions, as well as the presence of additional spin-flip absorption, the combined cyclotron resonance (CCR), centered at different resonant frequencies. The selection rules for CCRs are identified and complemented by the perturbation theory analysis. We also discuss the polarization dependence of the absorption and the proximity-induced emerging magneto-optical responses. Our theory explains the effect of proximity-induced spin interactions for Dirac electrons cyclotron resonance and points out at its experimental verifications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes K2Cr3As3 as a model spin-triplet superconductor by experimentally revealing three distinct superconducting phases (A, B, C). Using NMR measurements, the authors map out how the pairing symmetry and the direction of the d(k)-vector evolve under applied magnetic fields. These findings highlight the material's potential for manipulating topological quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the detailed mapping of topological phases and symmetry breaking in response to external fields provides a rich platform for studying emergent quantum order parameters relevant to advanced condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating spin-triplet superconductivity, a state crucial for hosting Majorana bound states in quantum computing.

**Main result.** The authors identified three distinct superconducting phases (A, B, and C) in K2Cr3As3, demonstrating the high tunability of its topological superconducting phases by manipulating magnetic fields.

**Method.** Measurements utilized Nuclear Magnetic Resonance ($	ext{}^{75}	ext{As}$-NMR) to probe the spin susceptibility and gap structure, alongside $ac$ susceptibility measurements.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the Cr-based compound K2Cr3As3, which is proposed as a non-magnetic alternative to U-based superconductors. The analysis maps out the superconducting phase diagram using magnetic field (B) and temperature (T).

**Key observables.** Knight shift ($K$), spin-lattice relaxation rate ($1/T_1$), and the evolution of the pairing vector $\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{k})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical temperature ($T_c \ge 6.2 	ext{ K}$), applied magnetic field (up to $13 	ext{ T}$), and temperature dependence exponents ($\propto T^{-3}$ vs. $\propto T^{-5}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on interpreting the temperature power laws of $1/T_1$ to distinguish between line-nodal and point-nodal gap structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing multiple superconducting phases (A, B, C) as a function of magnetic field for $B\parallel c$, detailing characteristic temperatures ($T^*$) derived from NMR measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes experimental signatures—NMR Knight shift and $1/T_1$ dependence—to map out the phase diagram, identifying distinct superconducting states characterized by different $\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{k})$-vector orientations and gap symmetries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-triplet topological superconductors are rare but of fundamental interest as they can host Majorana bound states that can be used in fault-tolerant quantum computing. Recent efforts have been devoted to searching for spin-triplet states in U-based compounds, but these materials have a low transition temperature (Tc) and coexisting competing orders, which creates significant experimental challenges and often leads to contradictory conclusions. The Cr-based candidate K2Cr3As3 offers a promising alternative: it has a much higher Tc of 6.2 K and no magnetic order. Here we report a hallmark signature of spin-triplet superconductivity arising from the internal spin degrees of freedom via nuclear magnetic resonance measurements, and demonstrate the high tunability of the topological phases. We discovered three distinct superconducting phases and revealed the evolution of the paired-spins direction (d(k)-vector). At low magnetic fields, K2Cr3As3 evolves from a helical (Phase A) to a chiral state (Phase B) with a rotation of the d(k)-vector from in-plane to out-of-plane direction upon cooling, although both phases have point nodes in the gap. A linenodal gap is realized in the high-field Phase C, where the d(k)-vector lies in the basal plane. These findings establish K2Cr3As3 as a model spin-triplet superconductor and a promising platform for manipulating topological phases.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose that odd-parity coplanar magnets offer a nonrelativistic route to engineering strong Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) effects. By constructing a bilayer system and tuning its magnetic order, they demonstrate the realization of distinct Rashba, Weyl, and Dresselhaus spin textures. This work is significant because it shows this engineered SOC can achieve physical equivalence to its relativistic counterparts while offering unprecedented tunability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a mechanism to engineer complex electronic couplings like SOC using structural/magnetic degrees of freedom rather than relying solely on heavy elements or intrinsic material properties, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To find a nonrelativistic pathway to highly tunable Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) effects, overcoming the limitation that strong SOC is typically restricted to heavy elements and fixed by material symmetry.

**Main result.** The proposed odd-parity coplanar magnet bilayer realizes Rashba, Weyl, and Dresselhaus spin textures nonrelativistically, achieving physical equivalence to relativistic SOC via superconducting phases.

**Method.** Symmetry analysis combined with k.p expansion and projection onto low-energy continuum Hamiltonians was used to derive effective models for the system's electronic structure.

**Model / system.** The model is an odd-parity coplanar magnet realized as a bilayer stack of two monolayer antiferromagnets, described by a multi-band Hamiltonian incorporating interlayer coupling and magnetic order parameters.

**Key observables.** Rashba, Weyl, and Dresselhaus spin textures ($\langle s_x(\mathbf{k}) angle, \langle s_y(\mathbf{k}) angle$), Spin Edelstein Effect susceptibility ($\chi_{ij}$), and the existence of fully gapped chiral topological superconducting phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer Néel vector tuning (used to switch SOC types); magnetic field ($B$) relative to pairing gap ($\Delta$); interlayer coupling ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on symmetry requirements for the nonrelativistic mechanism and approximations like linear response theory for transport calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the bilayer stacking strategy, energy spectra showing distinct SOC types (Rashba/Weyl/Dresselhaus), spin textures in momentum space, and topological phase diagrams mapping out superconducting regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model via symmetry-guided stacking, deriving low-energy Hamiltonians for different SOC types, calculating characteristic spin textures and transport responses (SEE), and finally demonstrating the realization of topological superconductivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-orbit coupling (SOC) is a relativistic effect that underpins a broad spectrum of phenomena in condensed matter physics, from topological phases of matter to spintronic functionality. Its relativistic origin, however, restricts strong SOC to heavy-element materials and locks spin-momentum texture into a fixed, material-specific pattern. Here we show that odd-parity coplanar magnets offer a nonrelativistic pathway to highly tunable SOC effects. We construct a bilayer coplanar magnet via symmetry-guided stacking of two monolayer odd-parity altermagnets and demonstrate that Rashba, Weyl, and Dresselhaus spin textures can all be realized, and that the spin texture can be switched between these forms simply by tuning the layer Neel vector. Through the spin Edelstein effect and the realization of fully gapped chiral topological superconducting phases, we demonstrate that this nonrelativistic SOC achieves physical equivalence to its relativistic counterpart. Our findings identify a new class of odd-parity coplanar magnets as a versatile platform for engineering SOC effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports an experimental breakthrough demonstrating electrical readout of Néel vector reversal in vdW antiferromagnets using $	ext{CrSBr}$. By constructing a tunnel junction with a ferromagnetic electrode, the authors achieved a measurable magnetoresistance (MR) signal that flips polarity when the AFM's magnetic order is reversed. This establishes a robust and generalizable strategy for building next-generation spintronic memory devices based on AFMs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter/spintronics, the concept of using quantum transport phenomena (spin-dependent tunneling) to read out a fundamental symmetry property ($\mathbf{N}$ reversal) is highly relevant to developing novel quantum memory elements and controlling spin states in solid-state systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is demonstrating an electrical readout mechanism for the $180^\circ$ reversal of the Néel vector ($\mathbf{N}$) in thin-film van der Waals antiferromagnets (AFMs). This capability is crucial for developing next-generation AFM-based spintronic and magnonic memory devices.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated this readout by utilizing a spin-dependent tunneling magnetoresistance (MR) measurement in $	ext{Co}/	ext{hBN}/	ext{CrSBr}$ heterostructures. They showed that reversing the Néel vector results in an opposite hysteresis pattern in the MR response, confirming electrical sensitivity to $\mathbf{N}$ orientation.

**Method.** The methodology involves fabricating vertical tunnel junctions using mechanical exfoliation of vdW materials ($	ext{CrSBr}$, $	ext{hBN}$) and measuring the magnetoresistance (MR) as a function of applied magnetic field ($B$) and bias voltage ($V_{	ext{bias}}$).

**Model / system.** The system is based on atomically thin, A-type vdW AFMs like $	ext{CrSBr}$, which possess sublattices ($\mathbf{m}_A$, $\mathbf{m}_B$) defining the Néel vector $\mathbf{\hat{N}}$. The measurement relies on the spin-dependent tunneling magnetoresistance (MR), modeled by the Jullière tunnelling model, between a ferromagnetic (FM) electrode ($	ext{Co}$) and the AFM layer.

**Key observables.** Magnetoresistance (MR) response exhibiting clear two-level (low/high resistance) switching behavior; the reversal of this MR hysteresis pattern when the initial magnetic field polarity is reversed.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurements are sensitive to bias voltage ($V_{	ext{bias}}$), temperature ($T$), and applied magnetic fields, with key transitions observed near $T_N \sim 130-140\ 	ext{K}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the measured MR is dominated by spin-dependent tunneling through the tunnel barrier, making it sensitive to the relative alignment between the FM magnetization and the AFM sublattice magnetization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the A-type AFM order and the proposed tunnel junction setup. Key results show field-dependent and bias-dependent MR hysteresis loops that reverse polarity when the initial magnetic field used for initialization is reversed, confirming $\mathbf{N}$ reversal.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the critical gap (electrical readout of Néel vector) to proposing a specific tunnel junction geometry. It then details experimental measurements showing spin-valve behavior and conclusively proves Néel vector reversal by observing opposite MR hysteresis patterns upon reversing the initial high magnetic field polarization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Owing to its robustness against external perturbations and intrinsically ultrafast dynamics, the Néel vector in antiferromagnets (AFMs) can enable the development of next-generation spintronic and magnonic devices for memory and computing applications. To realize AFM-based magnetic memory devices, one of the key requirements is to demonstrate electrical readout of 180-degree reversal of Néel vector in thin film AFMs, which remains critically missing. In this work, we report experimental demonstration of a novel transport methodology to detect Néel vector reversal in atomically thin films of a van der Waals (vdW) based A-type AFM. For this, we utilize spin-dependent electronic band properties of CrSBr by coupling it to a spin-polarized layer, separated by a tunnel barrier. In this configuration, the spin-dependent tunnelling magnetoresistance (MR) becomes sensitive to the relative orientation between the magnetization of the reference electrode and the interfacial sublattice magnetization of the AFM layer, in turn enabling electrical detection of the Néel vector orientation. Importantly, the observed MR can also reveal 180-degree reversal of Néel vector in even-layers of CrSBr, wherein adjacent sublattice magnetic layers are exactly compensated and the net magnetization vanishes and thus establishes a broadly applicable strategy for electrical detection of Néel vector in vdW-based AFMs.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work uses Surface Acoustic Waves in a high-field magnet setup to study the AC conductance of an InSb quantum well. The key innovation is successfully separating the electrical response originating from the QW itself versus that from a parallel conducting layer. This allowed for detailed characterization, including determining the electron g-factor and confirming different conduction regimes like hopping transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state transport, the use of high magnetic fields and quantum confinement effects touches upon topics relevant to strongly correlated electron systems and mesoscopic physics, which share theoretical overlap with open quantum system dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize the AC conductance of an n-InSb quantum well structure while simultaneously separating its contribution from a parallel conducting shunting layer.

**Main result.** The authors successfully separated the conduction mechanisms, determined the electron g-factor in the QW using acoustic measurements under tilted magnetic fields, and confirmed hopping transport in both the QW and the shunting layer at high fields.

**Method.** Acoustic methods utilizing Surface Acoustic Waves (SAW) generated on $	ext{LiNbO}_3$ are employed to measure changes in SAW attenuation ($\Delta\Gamma$) and velocity ($\Delta v/v$), which are then used to derive the complex AC conductance components.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an n-InSb quantum well structure grown on GaAs, featuring a 21 nm QW shunted by a parallel conducting layer. Measurements are conducted in high magnetic fields (up to 18 T) and cryogenic temperatures (20–500 mK).

**Key observables.** SAW attenuation change ($\Delta\Gamma$), relative velocity shift ($\Delta v/v$), complex AC conductance components ($\sigma_{ac}$), electron g-factor ($g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field $B$ (up to 18 T), Temperature $T$ (20–500 mK), Quantum Well width (21 nm).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ability to model and subtract the background contribution from the parallel conducting layer ($\Delta\Gamma_b(B) \propto 1/B^2$) is crucial for isolating the QW response.

**Figures summary.** Figures show magnetic field dependences of $\Delta\Gamma$ and $\Delta v/v$, illustrating the subtraction process to isolate QW contributions, and presenting results confirming distinct conduction regimes (e.g., hopping vs. delocalized).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes SAW measurements to derive AC conductance components ($\sigma_{QW}, \sigma_{layer}$), uses background subtraction techniques to separate the two material contributions, and finally applies a coincidence technique in tilted fields to measure fundamental parameters like the g-factor.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We studied the ac conductance of an $n$-InSb quantum well structure using acoustic methods in magnetic fields up to 18 T and at temperatures ranging from 20 to 500 mK. We attribute the unusual magnetic field dependences of surface acoustic wave (SAW) attenuation and velocity observed in the experiment to the presence of a conducting layer parallel to the quantum well in the sample. We successfully separated the contributions from both the quantum well and the shunting layer, enabling the identification of their distinct conduction mechanisms. Furthermore, by employing the coincidence technique, we determined the electron g-factor in the quantum well and investigated its dependence on the degree of spin polarization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses muARPES to characterize the electrical contact formed between a 2D semiconductor (WSe2) and a layered Mott insulator ($\alpha$-RuCl3). The researchers found that forming this junction causes a measurable valence band shift, suggesting successful charge transfer for ohmic contact. Crucially, they highlight that achieving stable, high-quality contacts is extremely sensitive to the experimental handling procedures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily experimental condensed matter physics, the study deals with Mott insulators ($	ext{RuCl}_3$) and interface electronic structure engineering, which touches upon correlated electron phenomena and emergent quantum states at boundaries.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the electronic structure and robustness of band offsets at the interface between single-layer WSe2 and alpha-RuCl3 van der Waals charge-transfer contacts.

**Main result.** Contact formation induces a significant valence band shift of (0.68 ± 0.05) eV in WSe2, establishing an ohmic contact via hole doping. However, the charge transfer effect is highly sensitive to fabrication conditions and RuCl3 degrades upon heating.

**Method.** Micro-focused angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (muARPES) was employed to measure the electronic structure of the heterostructure in ultra-high vacuum.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a van der Waals heterostructure composed of single-layer WSe2 contacted with alpha-RuCl3, which acts as a layered Mott insulator. The analysis focuses on charge transfer effects and band alignment at this interface.

**Key observables.** Valence band maximum (VBM) shift ($\Delta E_{ct}$), core level peak shifts, electronic structure mapping across the Brillouin zone.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Charge transfer magnitude (0.68 eV); Fermi energy relative to VBM; temperature/heating history during sample preparation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The charge transfer effect is assumed to be measurable via ARPES shifts, but its observation is critically dependent on minimizing environmental contamination and thermal stress.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band alignments showing the energy shift (Fig. 1), ARPES spectra confirming Mott insulating state and degradation upon heating (Fig. 2), and spatial maps/valence band dispersions comparing WSe2/hBN vs WSe2/RuCl3 contacts (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need to characterize contact interfaces, details the muARPES methodology applied to the WSe2/RuCl3 system, presents quantitative measurements of band shifts and charge transfer, discusses the limitations imposed by sample handling and material stability, and concludes with implications for device design.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The layered Mott insulator $α$-RuCl$_3$ induces degenerate hole-doping in two-dimensional semiconductors due to its large electron affinity, making it a promising charge-transfer material for establishing ohmic contacts in electronic devices. In order to assess the applicability and guide the design of devices incorporating RuCl$_3$ it is critical to determine the electronic structure and robustness of the band offsets that underpin the transport properties of semiconductors in contact with RuCl$_3$. Here, we apply micro-focused angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy to determine the electronic structure of single-layer WSe$_2$ contacted to RuCl$_3$ on hexagonal boron nitride substrates. We find that formation of a functioning WSe$_2$/RuCl$_3$ contact leads to a valence band shift of $(0.68 \pm 0.05)$ eV towards the Fermi energy in WSe$_2$. The charge transfer effect is challenging to observe as it depends sensitively on fabrication conditions such as solvent exposure, quality of interface encapsulation and heating of RuCl$_3$, imposing strict requirements on device design to attain high-quality contacts.

</details>

**Summary.** This work derives the ultimate theoretical limits governing how much light a small particle can absorb across a given frequency bandwidth. By mapping electromagnetism to an impedance-matching circuit problem, the authors establish Bode–Fano inequalities that quantify this fundamental trade-off. The paper provides a practical synthesis route using filter design theory to engineer materials whose dispersion approaches these physical boundaries.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical rigor connecting classical circuit theory (filter synthesis) to fundamental electromagnetic scattering limits provides powerful analytical tools for designing nanophotonic devices operating near physical constraints, which is relevant for understanding energy transfer in quantum optical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives fundamental, rigorous absorption-bandwidth limits for passive, causal, linear, and temporally dispersive subwavelength objects.

**Main result.** It establishes Bode–Fano-type constraints that quantify the ultimate trade-off between achieving high in-band absorption and maintaining a broad operational bandwidth.

**Method.** The electromagnetic scattering problem is mapped onto an equivalent single-mode impedance-matching circuit model, allowing the application of complex analysis techniques like Darlington's synthesis theorem and contour integration.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is a subwavelength nanoparticle or object ($\Omega$) characterized by its material susceptibility ($\chi$) and radiation properties ($Z_\Omega$), modeled as an equivalent electrical circuit.

**Key observables.** Absorption power ($P_a$), Scattering power ($P_s$), Extinction cross-section ($\sigma_e$), Reflection coefficient ($\Gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bode–Fano integral ($\eta_{BF}$), material dispersion parameters, and the order of the synthesized filter ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the object is passive, linear, causal, time-invariant, and that the electric dipole mode dominates due to subwavelength confinement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the equivalent single-mode circuit model, Darlington representations of impedances, low-pass prototypes in the s-domain, and comparisons of synthesized absorption spectra (e.g., Chebyshev vs. Drude) against the theoretical Bode–Fano limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from formulating the scattering problem via impedance matching to deriving fundamental sum rules, then refining these into rigorous Bode–Fano inequalities using complex analysis, and finally providing a constructive synthesis framework based on filter design techniques (e.g., Chebyshev realization) to approach these theoretical bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanostructures can be designed to absorb light efficiently at resonance despite their subwavelength footprint, but causality and passivity fundamentally limit the bandwidth over which strong absorption can be maintained. Here we derive fundamental absorption-bandwidth limits for passive, causal, linear, and temporally dispersive subwavelength objects by rigorously casting electromagnetic scattering as an equivalent impedance-matching problem. This mapping yields ultimate Bode-Fano-type constraints for optical absorption and provides rational synthesis guidelines for the material dispersion of passive nanoparticles that can approach the bounds. Our results clarify the ultimate limits for broadband light harvesting and dissipation, with implications for solar-energy conversion, photothermal hyperthermia, thermal management, and related nanophotonic technologies.

</details>

**Authors:** A. C. Opus, J. Q. Lu  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24031v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<sub>Figure 1: Hybrid execution. The 4-bit experts reside in page-locked, NUMA-interleaved host memory. Prefill streams each layer’s experts across PCIe 2.0 into a 6 GB-resident scratch buffer and runs the batched GPU kernels; decode keeps the single-token computation on the host CPU (W4A8 integer SIMD), avoiding a per-token PCIe round trip. Pruning and the prefix cache act on the prefill (GPU) path; the integer kernel acts on the decode (CPU) path.</sub>

The GDN recurrence (a sequential scan) and softmax attention account for the rest; the three components sum to ≈36.7 s of the 37.5 s prefill.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details an intensive hardware benchmarking study showing how to run a massive 35B MoE language model on severely outdated, resource-limited GPU hardware. The authors developed a hybrid inference engine that splits computation between the GPU (for prefill) and the CPU (for decode), achieving significant speedups by hand-optimizing kernels for legacy instruction sets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on LLMs, the core engineering challenge—optimizing computation flow around severe memory bandwidth and compute capability bottlenecks using custom low-level kernels—is highly relevant to simulating complex quantum many-body systems that exceed available classical memory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the practical limits of running a contemporary, large Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language model on severely constrained, legacy hardware.

**Main result.** The authors achieved significant performance gains by implementing a hybrid execution strategy, reducing prefill latency by $\sim 34\%$ and tripling decode throughput compared to baseline methods.

**Method.** A hybrid inference pipeline was designed: GPU handles batched prompt prefill using streamed expert weights, while the host CPU executes single-token decoding using a hand-written integer-SIMD kernel.

**Model / system.** The system uses a 35B parameter MoE model (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B) running on an outdated NVIDIA Tesla C2075 GPU (Fermi architecture, 6GB VRAM). The process is constrained by the need to fit a $\sim 10.5 	ext{ GB}$ model onto $6 	ext{ GB}$ of memory.

**Key observables.** Prefill latency (e.g., $57.2 	ext{ s} 	o 37.5 	ext{ s}$), decode throughput ($	ext{tok/s}$, e.g., $2.8 	o 8.6$), and prefix reuse time ($78 	ext{ s} 	o 0.5 	ext{ s}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Model size (35B total, $\sim 3$B active), GPU compute capability ($	ext{sm\_20}$), memory constraint ($10.5 	ext{ GB} > 6 	ext{ GB}$), and quantization level (4-bit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes the hardware limitations define a practical performance floor, as evidenced by negative results showing that standard optimizations failed to improve speed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the hybrid execution flow: experts are streamed from host RAM for GPU prefill and CPU decode. Figure 2 decomposes prefill time components (MoE GEMM, GDN recurrence, softmax attention).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the problem setup by identifying hardware constraints, proposes a novel hybrid inference strategy combining streaming GPU prefill with CPU-based integer-SIMD decoding, and rigorously measures performance improvements across various stages like prefill, decode, and prefix caching.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report end-to-end inference of \textbf{Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} -- a 35-billion-parameter, $\sim$3B-active Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with a hybrid gated-delta-net / full-attention backbone -- on a \textbf{2011 NVIDIA Tesla C2075} (Fermi, compute capability \smtwenty, 6\,GB), a GPU that predates tensor cores, native FP16 arithmetic, the \texttt{DP4A} integer dot-product instruction, and support in every modern CUDA toolchain. Because the 4-bit model ($\approx$10.5\,GB) is roughly twice the device memory, we adopt a \emph{hybrid} execution strategy: the GPU performs batched prompt \emph{prefill} with expert weights streamed layer-by-layer from host RAM, while \emph{decode} runs on the host CPU using a hand-written W4A8 integer GEMV built on the SSSE3 \texttt{pmaddubsw} instruction. The entire engine -- GEMM, hybrid-attention recurrence, MoE routing, and a from-scratch vision tower -- is written by hand for \smtwenty{} and compiled with the legacy CUDA 8.0 toolchain. On a 947-token prompt we reduce prefill latency from 57.2\,s to 37.5\,s ($-34\%$) through expert pinning, single-pass prefill, and NUMA interleaving, and we raise decode throughput from 2.8 to 8.6\,\tps{} ($\approx 3\times$) with the integer-SIMD kernel. A position-indexed snapshot cache for the recurrent (gated-delta-net) state restores prefix reuse on a recurrent architecture, cutting a repeated 78\,s prefill to 0.5\,s. We also report a set of \emph{negative} results -- offloading the language-model head to the idle GPU, hyper-threading, and three GPU-kernel rewrites all fail to help -- % which together pin down the practical floor of this hardware. Our aim is not a speed record but a careful account of what it takes, and where the walls are, to run a contemporary frontier-class MoE on fourteen-year-old silicon.

</details>
